## Question 1 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q1-unacademy**
**Q1.Underline the changes in the field of society and economy from the Rig Vedic to the later Vedic period. (150 Words 10 Marks)**

#

## Answer

**Answer:** **Introduction:** The transition from the Rig Vedic period to the Later Vedic period marked significant shifts in both societal structures and economic practices in ancient India. The society became more stratified, and economic activitiesevolvedwiththeexpansionofagricultureand trade. This is substantiated from the written sources like vedas in Rig Vedic era and upanishads, varnas, etc, in the Later Vedic period.

## **Key Changes in Society**

- **VarnaSystem:TheRigVedicSocietywasrelatively** egalitarian with flexible social classes that later evolved into a more rigid and hierarchical society. The division between Aryas and Dasas was later replaced by the varna system with Brahmins and Kshatriyas emerged as dominant classes, and the Shudraswereplacedatthebottomofthehierarchy.

## **Family Structure**

- Earlier the women had relatively higher social status, as they were allowed to participate in religious rituals and had access to education. Inthelatervedicperiod thepatriarchybecame strong as the women’s participation in public and religious life diminished, and their roles became largely confined to g spheres.
- A drastic change is seen in the institution of marriage, where earlier monogamy, polygamy and polyandry was practised which later polygamy became prominent and 8 types of marriage came into existence.
- Practice of Niyog and widow remarriage was also seen during the Rig Vedic period. But in the Later Vedic period widow remarriage was not that much popular.
- Also the practice of child marriage, emerged during the Later Vedic period, which was absent in the Rig Vedic society.
- **Religion:** The Rig Vedic religion was primarily centred around nature worship, with deities like IndraandAgnibeingprominent.Thistransformed into a more complex and ritualistic code, with the Brahmins playing a key role in conducting elaborate yajnas.

## **Key Changes in Economy**

- **Rise of Agricultural Economy:** Earlier the economy was predominantly pastoral, with cattle being the main measure of wealth. But in the later Vedic period with introduction of iron tools in cultivation, agriculture gained prominence and land became a crucial determinant of wealth.
- **Urbanisation:** In Rig Vedic era the society was predominantly rural, with semi-nomadic tribes. But with the increasing agricultural surplus and trade activities in the later Vedic era a gradual rise of towns and urban centres began. This ultimately led to the later urbanisation seen during the Mahajanapada period.
- **Specialised Professions:**Earlier the professions were not rigidly divided, and people could take on multiple roles based on needs. But with economic growth in the later Vedic era professions became more specialised and aligned with the varna system. Artisans, traders (Vaishyas), and labourers (Shudras) were increasingly defined by their birth and occupation.**Conclusion:** The transition from the Rig Vedic to the Later Vedic period marked crucial shifts in both societal and economic structures.These changes laid the groundwork for the more structured and hierarchical society that would follow in ancient India.
[Question ID: 2024-gs1-q1-unacademy]

## Question 2 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q2-unacademy**
**Q2.Estimate the contribution of Pallavas of Kanchi for the development of art and literature of South India. (150 Words 10 Marks)**

#

## Answer

## **Answer**

**Introduction:**Pallavaartandarchitecturemarktheearlydevelopment of Dravidian style, which later reached its peak under the Chola Dynasty. They were instrumental in pioneering the Dravidian architectural tradition, and their reign witnessed a significant transition from rock-cut temples to structural, free-standing temples. This period laid a strong foundation for the future evolutionofSouthIndianart,architecture,andliterary traditions.

##**Contributions of Pallavas to Art and Literature**

-**Temple Architecture**-**Rock-cut Architecture:**This was the initial phase of Pallava architecture consisting of two groups of monuments - the Mahendra group and the Mamalla group. The best examples of these are the cave temples at**Mandagapattu** **and Pallavaram.**Pallavas pioneered the construction of rock-cut temples, seen in the**Mahabalipuram complex constructed by** **Narsimhavarman.**Famous examples include**theFiveRathasandShoreTemple(anattempt** **to break free from the influence of the rock** **cut ‘Chaitya’ and ‘Vihara’).**-**Structural Architecture:** Temples evolved from rock-cut to structural stone temples, for instance the Kailasanatha Temple in Kanchipuram and the Shore temple in Mamallapuram .

- **Sculpture:**Pallava art is noted for its detailed, naturalistic depiction of gods, humans, and animals. The famous Descent of the**Ganges relief** **at Mahabalipuram (Arjuna’s Penance),** is one of the largest open-air rock carvings in the world.
- **Role in Fine Arts:**They were prominent supporters of the fine arts.**Inscriptions at** **Kudumiyanmalai and Thirumayam issued by** **Mahendraverman reflect their deep appreciation** **for music,** while the temple sculptures from this period highlight the popularity of dance. The vibrant paintings at Chithannavasal further showcase a glimpse into the rich tradition of Pallava painting. Mahendravarman I was known as Chittirakkarapuli and had composed the book, Thatchina Chitram.
- **Literature:**The Pallavas of Kanchi supported literature in a variety of languages, including**Sanskrit, Tamil, Prakrit, and Telugu.** - Pallavasplayedakeyroleinthedevelopmentof early Tamil literature, particularly the growth of bhakti literature. They were instrumental in shaping Tamil devotional literature, eventually compiled into the Tirukkural and Tevaram.
- The Pallava kings were also patrons of Sanskrit scholarship. King Mahendravarman I composed the Mattavilasa Prahasana, a farce in Sanskrit. The Pallava court became a hub of Sanskrit learning, attracting scholars from across India. The dynasty also supported the transmission of Buddhist and Jain texts written in Sanskrit.
- **ReligiousSyncretism:ThePallavaspromotedboth** Hinduism and Buddhism which reflected in their religious art and architecture. Mahabalipuram’s art depicts scenes from Hindu epics, including the Mahabharata and Ramayana.

## **Role in Education**

- The temples of Kanchi also served as ghatika, i.e. centres of education. The Pallava rulers were great patrons of learning and established educational institutions known as ghatika.
- The Pallava dynasty actively supported learning and scholarly exchanges with Southeast Asia.
- The Nalayira Divya Prabandham, composed by Alvar poets, was encouraged under Pallava rule.
- Bodhidharma (introduced Zen Buddhism to China) hailed from the Pallava region, showcasing the dynasty’s role in spreading Indian philosophies abroad. **Conclusion:** The Pallavas were not only torchbearers of art and culture within India but also played a pivotal role in spreading their influence beyond its borders. Early temple architecture in Southeast Asia bore distinct features of Dravidian art, a result of the diplomatic relations established between the Pallavas and the Srivijaya Empire.

## Question 3 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q3-unacademy**
**Q11.“Though the great Cholas are no more yet their nameisstillrememberedwithgreatpridebecause of their highest achievements in the domain of art and architecture.” Comment. (Answer in 250 words) 15 Marks**

#

## Answer

**Answer:** **Introduction:**The Chola Empire, flourishing from the**9th to 13th** **centuries** in Southern India, was a beacon of artistic and architectural innovation.

## **Chola’s contribution to Art**

- **Chola Sculptures:**They often depicted deities, dancers, musicians, and warriors, with a focus on realism and intricate details. Eg;**Nataraja** **sculpture**is famous not only for its beauty, but also for its spiritual significance. It utilised the art of**Lost Wax Technique.**-**Chola Paintings:**The murals of the Chola age bring out diverse emotions on many faces, a feature rarely seen in contemporary Indian art. They are realistic, emotive, massive and animated. Eg;**Tableau of Rajaraja Chola I** listening to his preceptor Karuvur Devar.
- **Chola Music:**In music, twenty-three**panns** were used. The seven music alphabets were used: sa, re, ga, ma, pa, da, and ni. In every temple, Alwar and Nayanarhymnsweresung.Drums,udukkai,veena, and flute were well-known musical instruments.
- **Chola Dance: Bharatanatyam**was patronised. Lord Shiva was depicted as a performer of the Karana dance. Eg:**Rajaraja I** assigned 400 dancing girls to the Tanjore Temple.
- **Chola Drama: Rajarajeswara natakam**and**Rajarajavijayam**were most celebrated Dramas. Ariyakuthu, Chakki koothu, and Santhi koothu are dramas mentioned in Chola inscriptions.**Chola’s contribution to Architecture:**The Cholas built upon the architectural legacy of the**Pallavas,** enhancing and elevating their heritage to new heights.
- Cholasusedstoneasconstructionmaterialinstead of **bricks.**High boundary walls demarcate the temple complex from outside area.**Vimana**over garbhagriha rose like a stepped pyramid. Eg:**Brihadeshwara Temple**- Massive, elaborative and decorative Gopuram (larger compared to Pallavas) acted as the main gatetothecomplex.Eg:GangaikondaCholapuram**Temple.**- Temple complex was designed in**Panchayatan** **style**with a principal temple and four subsidiary temples. Octagon shaped Shikhara was a decorative crowing element.**Mandap hall**was a pillared hall with a carved roof that housed sociocultural events.**Water tank** insidethe complex was eccentric to Chola style.
- Entrance to the temple was decorated with sculptures of **Dwarapala, Mithun**and**Yakshi.** Their temples were built on huge edifice, square in plan, encompassed by massive colonnaded perimeter wall (prakara) with shrines dedicated to the deities of direction (ashtadikpalakas) and a main entrance with a tower (gopura), an entrance porch, vestibule (antarala), and inner sanctum (garbhagriha) with many sculptures of Lord Shiva and other deities.
- Their temples had beautiful **shikhara stones**at the top. It had intricate and meticulously carved carvings.**Airavatesvara Temple**has a separate impressive**Amman shrine,**the**Periya Nayaki,**dedicated to Devi signifying regards for**female** **deity.**The Cholas approached their work like**sculptors,**refining it to perfection like**jewellers.** Their period marked a high point in South Indian culture, with their artistic contributions influencing generations and leaving an enduring legacy in Indian art and architecture.

## Question 4 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2023-gs1-q4-unacademy**
**Q1. Explain the role of geographical factors towards the development of Ancient India. (10 Marks, 150 Words)**

#

## Answer

## **Approach**

- **Introduction:** Briefly outline the major geographical features of the Indian subcontinent.
- **Body:** Explain how geography influenced economy, polity, invasions, culture, and spread of religions.
- **Conclusion:**Conclude by emphasizing geography as a foundational force shaping Ancient India.**Answer:** **Introduction:** The Indian subcontinent is marked by diverse geographical features like the Himalayas, IndoGangetic plain, Deccan plateau, coastal plains, deserts, and mountain passes. These natural features played a pivotal role in determining settlement patterns, trade networks, cultural evolution, and political development.

## **Body**

## **Indus Valley Civilization (IVC)**

- FertilealluvialsoiloftheIndusbasinsupported agriculture and urbanization. Climate shifts and declining monsoons contributed to the decline of IVC. Sites like Dholavira show evidence of coastal trade due to Gujarat’s strategic location.

## **Vedic and Later Period**

- Initially, Aryans settled in the Punjab region. Later, expansion into the Gangetic plain, with its fertile soil and abundant water, spurred agriculture, surplus production, taxation, and the second wave of urbanization.

## **Political Development through Geography**

- Northwest passes (Khyber, Bolan, Gomal) facilitated invasions by Persians, Greeks, Shakas,andKushans.Incontrast,thenortheast and peninsular India remained relatively secure due to natural barriers and climatic challenges. The Himalayas also promoted trans-regional trade with Tibet.

## **Cultural and Artistic Influence**

- Gandhara art combined Greek influences due to northwest interactions, while Mathura art developed indigenously.
- Scripts like Kharosthi came via Persians, while Brahmi dominated central India.
- Surplus agriculture required pottery for storage.
- Temple architecture adapted to locally available materials and climatic needs.
- Music evolved with influences through the Silk Road and maritime trade.

## **Spread of Religions**

- South India’s peninsular location enabled Buddhism’s spread to Sri Lanka through maritime routes. **Conclusion:** Geographical diversity shaped the socio-economic, political, and cultural trajectory of Ancient India. Fertile plains nurtured civilizations, mountain passes determined invasions, coastlines enabled trade, and natural resources enriched cultural expression— together making geography the bedrock of India’s ancient development.
[Question ID: 2023-gs1-q4-unacademy]

## Question 5 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2023-gs1-q5-unacademy**
**Q12. What were the major technological changes introduced during the Sultanate period? How did those technological changes influence Indian society? (15 Marks, 250 Words)**

#

## Answer

## **Approach**

- **Introduction:** Define the Delhi Sultanate period and its significance as a phase of technological diffusion.
- **Body:** Explain key technological innovations sector-wiseandtheirinfluenceonagriculture, economy, warfare, culture, and society.
- **Conclusion:**Conclude with the long-term impact of these changes on Indian history and later Mughal advancements.**Answer:** **Introduction:**The Delhi Sultanate (1206–1526 A.D.) marked a transformative era in Indian history. It not only introduced political centralization but also witnessed major technological advancements across agriculture, industry, military, and culture. Many of these innovations were borrowed from West and Central Asia and integrated into Indian society, leading to farreaching consequences.**Body:** **Agriculture**- Introduction of the**Araghatta (Persian wheel)** revolutionized irrigation.
- Expansion of canals, reservoirs, and water management enhanced productivity, enabling population growth and agrarian prosperity. **Textiles and Crafts**-**Spinning wheel (Charkha)** introduced, boosting textile production.
- Bengal and Kashmir gained fame for fine textiles and shawls, fostering exports.
- Techniques like **tie-and-dye (bandhana),**immersion dyeing, and later block-printing enriched Indian textile traditions.**Paper and Knowledge Dissemination** - Proliferationofpaperandbookbindingfacilitated widespread documentation, administration, and literature.
- Manuscripts, farmans, and accounts became integral to governance, literacy, and culture. **Metallurgy and Industry**- Advances in**sword-making,** tin coating, and distillation enhanced warfare and crafts.
- **Coinage reforms**introduced by Sultans strengthened trade and economy.**Military Technology**- Introduction of**gunpowder and firearms** revolutionized warfare.
- **Iron stirrups (rikab)**enhanced cavalry effectiveness, reshaping battlefield tactics.**Architecture and Building Construction**- Use of**lime mortar**and construction of the**true** **arch** facilitated massive architectural works like mosques, forts, and tombs.
- Mihrabs and domes reflected Indo-Islamic synthesis in architecture. **Shipbuilding and Navigation**- Innovations in ship design and diffusion of the**magnetic compass**improved long-distance maritime trade.**Astronomy and Science**- Feroz Shah Tughlaq’s observatory in Delhi and Mahendra Suri’s invention of the**Yantaraja** marked significant astronomical advances.
- Tas-i-gharial (water clock) symbolized early mechanical innovations. **Medical Sciences**-**Unani system** enriched Indian medicine with texts like Firdous al-Hikmah.
- Sarangdhara Samhita introduced opium-based medicines and diagnostic methods.
- The **Rasa Chikitsa**system explored mineral-based therapies.**Measurements**-**Gaz-i-Sikandari,**introduced by Sikandar Lodi, standardized measurements and facilitated fair trade practices.**Impact on Society**-**Boostedagriculturaloutput,sustainingpopulation** and urban growth.
- Expanded **trade and crafts,** integrating India into global economic circuits.
- Strengthened **military capabilities,** changing warfare and power dynamics.
- Promoted **knowledge dissemination and cultural** **synthesis,**influencing art, architecture, and education.**Conclusion:** The technological changes of the Sultanate period reshaped India’s socio-economic and cultural landscape. By revolutionizing agriculture, industry, warfare, and learning, these innovations laid the groundwork for the Mughal era’s advancements and India’s integration into a wider Afro-Eurasian network. Their legacy continues to reflect in India’s craft traditions, architecture, and cultural practices.

## Question 6 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2022-gs1-q6-unacademy**
**Q1.HowwillyouexplainthatmedievalIndiantemple sculptures represent the social life of those days? (150 words, 10 Marks) Approach**

#

## Answer

- **Introduction:** Give a brief note on the Sculptures.
- **Body:** Elaborate on how medieval Indian temple sculptures represent the social life of those days.
- **Conclusion:**Conclude the answer by stating representation of medieval indian temple sculptures in various aspects.**Answer:** **Introduction:** Sculptures are small works of art, either handmade or with tools and are more related to aesthetics than engineering and measurements. Indian temple sculptures from the medieval period (7th century CE to 1526) represent diverse cultures, religions, and social life of that era.

## **Body**

## **Everyday life representation**

- **The sculptures in Khajuraho Temples**depict everyday life, mythical stories, and spiritual values, showcasing activities like women applying makeup, musicians playing music, and people engaged in daily tasks during the medieval era. The sculptures were generally**erotic in their** **themes**and drew inspiration from**Vatsyayana’s** **Kamasutra.**- The**figure of a woman writing a love letter**is a charming specimen of the**Chandella art** of the 11th century.

## **Culture and faith representation**

- Sculpturesreflectculturalandfaith-relatedaspects. Examples include **Konark Sun Temple,** where sculptures depict literature, tolerance for multiple faiths, and representations of everyday life.
- **The Gurjara Pratiharas,**ruling during the**8th to** **10th centuries,** witnessed a significant cultural renaissance in their vast kingdom spanning Gujarat, Rajasthan, and Uttar Pradesh.
- In Rajasthan, sculptural activity flourished in places like **Vasantgarh, Devangarh, Palta,** **Osian, Dilwara, Chittor, and Mandor.**-**The Ellora Cave temples** demonstrate the coexistence of Hindu, Jain, and Buddhist traditions, with carvings depicting deities and sacred figures from each.
- ProminentsculpturessuchasMahishasuramardini in relief, **Girigovardhana panel, Arjuna’s penance** **or the Descent of the Ganga, Trivikrama Vishnu,** **Gajalakshmi, and Ananthasayanam** represent the faith and beliefs of society.
- A masterpiece from Mahabalipuram depicts the **goddess Durga in a fierce battle against a buffalo-** **headed demon,** symbolizing the eternal struggle of good triumphing over evil.
- Literarythemesexpressedinpoemsanddramasof the time are carved into the temple sculptures. **The** **Modhera temple and Dilwara temple**highlight the influence of the**Natyashastra tradition,** with intricate carvings of dancers and musicians.

## **Closeness with Nature representation**

- The representation of the **Elephant in the Arjuna’s** **penance scene** is a remarkable example in Indian art. It skillfully depicts the celestial, temporal, and animal worlds.
- In the 13th century, **Chola art showcased a** **sculpture of Bhudevi,** the earth goddess, as the consort of Vishnu, symbolizing the earth and representing patience and tolerance.
- An excellent **specimen of Sena art from Mahanad** **in West Bengal** is a lovely figure of the personified river goddess Ganga.

## **Representation of the elite social life**

- Examples like the sculptures of **Krishnadevaraya** **in Chidambaram and the Brihadeshwara temple** **patronized by Raja Raja Chola I** highlight the association of rulers with deities.
- Emperors commissioned portraits to immortalize themselves alongside deities, demonstrating political power. **Conclusion:** In summary, medieval Indian temple sculptures go beyond aesthetic beauty. They serve as symbolic representations of social, cultural, political, and religious aspects of history, preserving the diverse facets of society in stone.
[Question ID: 2022-gs1-q6-unacademy]

## Question 7 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2022-gs1-q7-unacademy**
**12. Discuss the main contributions of Gupta period and Chola period to Indian heritage and culture. (250 Words, 15 Marks) Approach**

#

## Answer

- **Introduction:** Introduce with drawing a line around the Gupta period and Chola period .

## **Body**

- Mention the contributions of Gupta period and Chola period to Indian heritage and culture .
- **Conclusion:Concludetheanswermentioning**remarkable developments from this period.**Answer:** **Introduction:**The Gupta period (c. 4th to 6th century CE) and the Chola period (c. 9th to 13th century CE) are considered golden eras in Indian history. They left indelible imprints on Indian heritage and culture.**Body:** **Gupta Period Contributions:**Literature and Arts: The Gupta period is often referred to as the “Golden Age of Sanskrit Literature.” It saw the flourishing of classical Sanskrit literature, with notable works like Kalidasa’s plays, the Kamasutra by Vatsyayana, and the scientific treatise Aryabhatiya by Aryabhata. The Gupta era also witnessed the development of Gupta art, exemplified by the exquisite Gupta sculptures. Mathematics and Astronomy: The Gupta period made significant advancements in mathematics and astronomy. Mathematician Aryabhata developed the concept of zero and provided accurate calculations of astronomical phenomena. Brahmagupta’s works on algebra and arithmetic further contributed to mathematical knowledge. Administration and Governance: The Gupta rulers established an efficient and centralised administrative system. They introduced the concept of Samanta, a feudal lord who assisted in governance. The Gupta era witnessed the flourishing of trade and commerce, fostering economic prosperity.**Chola Period Contributions:**Architecture and Temple Construction: The Chola dynasty made remarkable contributions to temple architecture,exemplifiedbytheBrihadeeswararTemple in Thanjavur. The temples featured intricate carvings, towering gopurams (entrance gateways), and exquisite sculptures. The Chola period also saw the construction of irrigation systems like the Grand Anicut (Kallanai), contributing to agricultural development. Literature and Music: The Chola period witnessed significant advancements in Tamil literature, with several literary works composed during this time. The great epic, Silappatikaram, and the devotional poetry of the Nayanars enriched Tamil literature. The Chola era also saw the development of classical music, with emphasis on Carnatic music and the patronage of musicians and composers. Maritime Trade and Naval Power: The Chola rulers established a strong naval fleet and maintained a powerful maritime trade network. They had extensive trade connections with Southeast Asian countries, contributing to cultural exchanges and fostering regional influence.**Conclusion:** The Gupta era contributed to literature, arts, mathematics, and administration, setting the foundation for Indian intellectual and cultural achievements. The Chola period witnessed remarkable developments in architecture, literature, music, and maritime trade, leaving a lasting impact on Indian cultural heritage. The contributions of these periods continue to inspire and shape India’s rich cultural legacy, fostering a sense of pride and admiration for their achievements.

## Question 8 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2022-gs1-q8-unacademy**
**13. Discuss the significance of the lion and bull figures in Indian mythology, art and architecture. (250 Words, 15 Marks) Approach**

#

## Answer

- **Introduction:** Introduce with signifying representation of lion and bull figures .

## **Body**

- Mention the significance of the lion and bull figures in Indian mythology, art, and architecture.
- **Conclusion:Concludetheanswermentioning**symbolism of these iconic figures.**Answer:**The lion and bull figures hold immense**Introduction:**significanceinIndianmythology,art,andarchitecture. These iconic symbols are deeply rooted in religious and cultural traditions, representing various deities and embodying important virtues and characteristics. Lions symbolise power, royalty, and divine protection, while bulls represent fertility, strength, and devotion.**Body: The significance of the lion and bull figures in**
**Indian mythology, art, and architecture:** **Lion Figures:**Symbol of Power and Royalty: The lion is often associated with power, strength, and royalty in Indian mythology. It is considered the vehicle (vahana) of Goddess Durga and represents her ferocity and bravery. Lions are also associated with Lord Vishnu’s Narasimha incarnation, depicting divine protection and fierce defence against evil. Architectural Depictions: Lion figures are prominent in Indian temple architecture, adorning pillars, entrance gateways (gopurams), and thrones. These majestic sculptures symbolise the divine presence and authority, adding grandeur and symbolism to sacred spaces. Cultural Significance: Lions hold cultural significance, symbolisingcourage,leadership,andnobilityinIndian folklore and ancient texts. The “Simhasana” (lion’s throne) represents the seat of power and authority in traditional Indian governance.**Bull Figures:**SymbolofFertilityandStrength:Bullsholdasignificant placeinIndianmythologyandareassociatedwithLord Shiva. Nandi, the celestial bull, is the loyal vehicle and gatekeeper of Lord Shiva. The bull represents strength, fertility, and the untamed natural forces. Temple Iconography: Bull figures, particularly Nandi, are found in temple complexes across India. They are often placed facing the sanctum sanctorum, symbolising devotion and acting as intermediaries between devotees and deities. Agricultural Symbolism: Bulls are also associated with agricultural traditions and are considered auspicious symbols of prosperity. They represent the divine connection between humans and nature, highlighting the importance of agriculture and fertility in Indian society.**Conclusion:** The lion and bull figures hold great significance in Indian mythology, art, and architecture. Their depictions in sculptures and architectural elements enrich India’s cultural heritage, adding symbolism and aesthetic appeal to religious spaces. These iconic figures continue to inspire reverence, evoke a sense of awe, and reflect the rich mythological and cultural tapestry of India.

## Question 9 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2021-gs1-q9-unacademy**
**Q1. Evaluate the nature of the Bhakti literature and its contribution to Indian culture. (10 Marks, 150 Words) Tags: Salient aspects of Art Forms, Literature, andArchitecturefromAncienttoModerntimes.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **Introduction: Give a brief description about** **the origins of Bhakti literature.**-**Body: Write about the nature of Bhakti** **literature.**-**Conclusion: Give an account of its** **contribution to Indian culture.** **Answer:**TheoriginsofBhaktiliteraturecanbelinked to ancient texts like the Vedas, Upanishads, and Gita as well as to the Brahmanical and Buddhist traditions. Between the 7th and the 12th centuries, Tamil Nadu saw the growth of the Bhakti movement. Bhakti movement is considered a cultural revolution againsttheillpracticescarriedoutinHinduismduring the Medieval period of India. This cultural revolution had a very significant impact on the treasure of Indian literature. This literary work was mainly written by elaborating new forms of devotion towards the God and focussed on the close relationship between the devotee and deity.**Nature of Bhakti Literature**-**The devotional nature of the literature:** Kirtanaghosha features devotional songs composed by Shankara Deva and Madhavadeva.
- **A step back for the evil in society:** The Tamil poetsaints in south India started writing the Bhakti literature in the sixth century AD. The culture had many social oddities, including rigid caste systems, pointless rituals and religious activities, blind faiths, and social dogmas. The literature of the bhakti movement warned against all such sins.
- **Inclusivity:** Castes and outcasts were encouraged to be included, according to Bhakti literature.
- **Spirituality:** As a result of Bhakti literature, the Bhakticultwaspopularized.Itmarkedaconceptual leap from earlier devotional literature, which was based primarily in Sanskrit and centred on rites and rituals. Bhakti literature also popularised spirituality among the common people.
- **Harmonyamongstdifferentreligions:BabaFarid’s**Sufi poetry was assimilated into Sikh religious canon. Also, Kabir’s teachings were applied in the Guru Granth Sahib.**Bhakti Literature and South Indian Culture** - Bhakti literature started to be composed in the 6th Century AD in South India by the Tamil poetsaints.
- Through the Divya Prabandha, the devotional poetry of Vishnu devotees, the Alvars expanded Vaishnavism.
- Nayanars, who are devotees of Shiva, wrote the Thirumurai, a collection of devotional poetry called the Tamil Veda. **Bhakti Literature and North Indian Culture** - During the 12th century AD, the pioneer admirer of the Bhakti movement in North India was Ramananda. This resulted in huge growth in literature including in various languages such as Hindi, Marathi, Bengali, Gujarati, Punjabi, etc.
- ‘Ramcharitmanas’ and ‘Hanuman Chalisa’ are the famous works of Tulsidas.
- The main contribution of Tulsidas was that he made sacred texts and Ramayana accessible to every person.
- As an admired poet by Hindus, Muslims as well as Sikhs, Kabir wrote in his own vernacular.
- Mirabai was the most famous lady saint of the Bhakti movement. She composed various devotional songs, known as Bhajans, to express her love and devotion to Lord Krishna.
- Surdas was a blind poet who composed songs tributing to Lord Krishna. The language he used to compose was Braj Bhasha (Hindi dialect), which eventually gained the status of literary language because of his compositions.
- Jagadeva has written popular poetic texts during the Bhakti period in devotion to Lord Krishna and thus famous sacred text called “Gita Govinda”.
- This poetic text of Jagdeva celebrated the love between Radha and Krishna.  It is considered that this work laid the foundation for the evolution and development of Bengali literature.
- Vaishnava poets Chaitanya and Chandidasa were famous in Bengal. They produced eroticism combined with spiritual fervour in their poetry.
- Among the Vaishnava saints of Assam were Shankara Deva and Madhavadeva.
- Bhattadeva had translated the Bhagavad Gita into Assamese, which led to the enrichment of Assamese prose.
- Accordingly, the works of Jnanadeva (also known asJnaneshwari)andAmrutanubhavareconsidered sacred in Marathi. It was a significant shift from the earlier religious texts, which were primarily written in Sanskrit and focused on rituals and ceremonies. Their shared viewpoints not only prevented potential internal confrontations but also helped foster a more tolerant culture. Their messages were turned into songs, proverbs, and stories to appeal to the general populace, which helped **give** **rise to the regional languages.** ---

## Question 10 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2021-gs1-q10-unacademy**
**Q2. Trace the rise and growth of socio-religious reform movements with special reference to Young Bengal and Brahmo Samaj. (10 Marks, 150 Words) Tags: Modern Indian History from about the middle of the Eighteenth Century until the Present: Socio-Religious Reform Movements.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **Introduction: Give an overview of 19th** **century socio-religious reform movements.**

## **Body**

- **Mention a few examples of socio-** **religious reform movements.**-**Describe the Young Bengal Movement.**-**Describe the Brahmo Samaj.**-**Conclusion: Conclude with how these** **movements develop intellectual bent and** **rationalism in Indians.** **Answer:**India within the 19th century witnessed a series of reform movements undertaken in various parts of the country. These movements were oriented toward a restructuring of the Indian society along modern lines. Raja Ram Mohan Roy and Swami Dayanand Saraswati are among thepioneers who led theIndian renaissance and enlightened the society.**Socio-religious Reform Movements within the 19th**

## **Century**

- **Prarthana Samaj:**The Prarthana Samaj was established in Bombay by Dr. Atma Ram Pandurang in 1876 with**the target** of rational worship and social reform.
- **Satya Shodhak Samaj: it had been** started by Jyotiba Phule in Maharashtra, in 1873, to spread of education among women and lower caste people.
- **The Theosophical Society:**Madam H.P. Blavatsky laid**the inspiration**of the movement**within the** **US**in 1875 and later Colonel M.S. Olcott joined her. The members of this society believe that a special relationship**will be** established between a person’s soul and God by contemplation, prayer, revolution.
- **Self-Respect Movement (1925):**Led by EV Ramaswamy Naicker (Periyar) the movement**aimed toward**nothing**wanting** a rejection of Brahminical religion and culture which Naicker felt was the prime instrument of exploitation of the lower castes.

## **The Young Bengal Movement**

- Henry Louis Vivian Derozio started the Young Bengal Movement in Calcutta **within the** 1820s.
- Also, Derozio was perhaps **the primary**nationalist poet**of contemporary** India. Derozio was an Anglo-Indian college teacher in Calcutta, and he encouraged radical thinking among his students.
- His students came to be **called** Derozians.
- He also inspired free and rational thinking and propagated the spirit of liberty, equality and freedom.
- The movement attacked old traditions and decadent customs, advocating women’s rights and education and educating **the general public**on**the** **present** socio-economic and political questions through press and public associations.
- It inculcated spirit of **rationalism**and revolt against**the present** social & religious structure of the society.

## **The Brahmo Samaj**

- Raja Ram Mohan Roy was the **founding father of**this movement. He was called**the primary Homo** **sapiens sapiens** of India. He was the pioneer of socio religious reforms.
- The Brahmo Samaj was **the primary**intellectual**front** in modern India. It led to the emergence of rationalism and enlightenment in India which indirectlycontributedtothenationalistmovement.
- Themovementfoughtagainstworship,polytheism, caste oppression, and fought social evils like Sati, polygamy, purdah system, and child marriage.
- He rallied support to the efforts of William Bentinck (Governor General) for abolition of Sati and wrote extensively for the cause.
- In 1829, the custom of sati was formally abolished. He also condemned polygamy **and plenty of**other**kinds of**subjugation**of girls.**Roy was also an advocate**of contemporary** education.
- He opened an English school **moreover** as a Vedanta college (1825).
- The movement was dedicated towards promotion of western sciences and literature, etc. Thus, these movements helped in developing an intellectual class amongst Indians, formed **the amount**of India renaissance and contributed to**the concept** of India and values of tolerance, brotherhood, spirit of enquiry, secularism, rationalism and liberty.

## Question 11 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2020-gs1-q11-unacademy**
**Q1. The rock-cut architecture represents one of the mostimportantsourcesofourknowledgeofearly Indian art and history. Discuss. (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Indian culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, define what is a rock-cut** **architecture and the significance of it.**-**In Body, describe how it is a source of** **knowledge of early Indian art and history** **with examples.**-**Conclude with the insights that it projects** **even today and summarize it.** **Answer:**Rock-cut architecture is a practice of creating various structures out of the**natural solid rock**by the method of**either carving or cutting.**This is found in abundance in the Indian sub-continent than anywhere else in the world. Most of the rock-cut architectures found in India are**religious**in nature but they also give the economical, societal, political, and educational insights of the past.**Religious Knowledge of Early Indian Art and History**

## **from the Rock-Cut Architecture**

- The western Deccan area of India has many caves whichevendatestothebeforeChristera.Example: **Bhajacave,Kanhericave,AjantaCave.Thesewere**identified to be**Buddhist** caves.
- **Ellora cave and Mahabalipuram**having temples of Kailashnath, and Shore respectively signifies the**Hinduism** ideology being predominant in the region during the 6th century CE.
- The signs of **Jainism** can be found through the caves of Udaygiri and Khandagiri in the eastern part of India. These were built during the 1st century BCE.
- One of the **oldest rock-cut architecture**was found in the**Barabara caves in Bihar**which was built around the 3rd century BCE which depicts the**Ajivika sect.** **EconomicKnowledgeofEarlyIndianArtandHistory**

## **from the Rock-Cut Architecture**

- Commerce and religion complement each other in ancient India. This is further elaborated by the factthat **BhajaandKarlecavesweresituated** inthe busiest trade routes in the Western Ghats.
- Thistraderoutewasusedtoconnecttotheinterior **parts**with the seaports of the western coast.**Educational Knowledge of Early Indian Art and**

## **History from the Rock-Cut Architecture**

- The famous **Kanheri caves** in the western India was an educational hub where the evidence of water harvesting can be found.
- **Bhaja cave**has clusters of Stupas, where the relics of the teachers were obtained.**Political Knowledge of Early Indian Art and History**

## **from the Rock-Cut Architecture**

- Many political rulers **protected such architectures** under their patronage.
- For example, **Barabara caves by King Ashoka,** **Ellora by the Rashtrakuttas, and the Chalukyas** **protecting Badami cave**temples is a testimony of the political patronage in the ancient times. These findings clearly give an idea as to how the**rock-** **cut architectures have been an important source of** **information**regarding the various aspects of life in ancient India and help us understand their culture in a holisticway.Italsopaveswayforustounderstandhow**the evolution** in society has taken place.

## Question 12 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2020-gs1-q12-unacademy**
**Q2. Pala period is the most significant phase in the history of Buddhism in India. Enumerate. (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Indian culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, Define the timeline,** **geography of the Pala period and the** **stakeholders involved.**-**In Body,**-**Discuss how the patronization of the** **rulers help in flourishing the Buddhism** **ideology.**-**Discuss about the achievement of the** **Buddhism art, literature, architecture,** **and paintings during the era.**-**Conclude with remarks/opinions how the** **said period led to the reincarnation of** **Buddhism culture and briefly talk about** **what happened after the Pala period got** **over.** **Answer:**The foundation of the Pala dynasty was laid down when the people of Bengal elected a warrior named to be the ruler of the entire territory in .**Gopala** **750 CE**Before the formation of Pala Empire, the territory of Bengal was going through an anarchic period amidst chaos. It was Gopala, who brought back peace to the region, and he was known as the**first Buddhist king**. The Pala dynasty ruled for almost four**in the region**centuries.Theirterritoryconsistedof**Bengalandmost** **parts of Bihar.**A few of the major cities in the territory are Pataliputra, Gauda, Somapura, Monghyr, Ramvati, Vikramapur, and Jaggadala.**Contribution of the Pala rulers in promoting** **Buddhism:**As all the rulers in the Pala dynasty were all staunch followers of**Mahayana Buddhism,** each one of them contributed to the flourishing of the Buddhism culture through their patronage.
- By developing Buddhist centres of learning like **Vikramshila and Nalanda University,** which paved the way for scholars to propagate Buddhism to various regions.
- built the monastery in Odantapuri, **King Gopala** Bihar. This monastery was one of the most prominent Buddhist Mahaviharas and was considered to be the second oldest of its kind.
- **King Dharmapala** revived the Nalanda University which in turn helped in preaching the Buddhism ideologyandfoundedtheVikramshilaUniversity.
- **King Devapala**built many monasteries in Magadh (present day West Bengal and Bihar), where propagation of Buddhist ideology across different regions was promoted.**Signs of Buddhist art, literature, architecture, and**

## **paintings**

- Most of the architecture during the Pala era was **religious.** Many Mahaviharas, Stupas, Chaityas were built during this period.
- ApartfromNalandaandVikramshila,Mahaviharas of Somapura, Devikota, Panditta, Fulbadi, and Jagaddala also contributed immensely to the spread of Buddhism.
- The **scraps found in Bodhgaya and Nalanda** provide an excellent overview of the architectural advancements of the era. Nalanda was considered to be one of the finest institutions at the time for the study of Buddhist architectures.
- The **terracotta decoration** found in Paharpur suggests how the demonstration of art was used in propagating the religious ideas of Buddhism.
- Sculptures of Buddha in **‘Bhumisparsha Mudra’** werealsorecoveredfromtheMagadhaestablishing the practices of ‘Mudra’ during the Pala period.
- Several **manuscripts** which were written on palm leaves were recovered having the paintings which depicted the life of Buddha.
- Wall paintings were recovered in Saradh, and Sarai Sthal further reassured that the preaching of **‘Boddhisattva’** was practiced.
- Buddhist text of **‘Charyapada’**was compiled in Bangla language during the Pala rule. Buddhist**poet Vajradatta**who wrote the famous text of**Lokesvarashataka**prospered under King Devapala. The rulers of the Pala dynasty revived institutions,**promoted literature and religious poets,**safeguarded the sculpture, created artistic value enhancing Buddhism culture and sent poets, preachers to neighboring countries to propagate the Buddhist ideologies.**Right after the fall of the Pala Dynasty** **by the 12th Century,**the destruction of monasteries and other Buddhism scriptures is a testimony that**Buddhism would not have prospered** without the patronage of the Pala dynasty.

## Question 13 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2020-gs1-q13-unacademy**
**Q11. Indian Philosophy and tradition played a significant role in conceiving and shaping the monuments and their art in India. Discuss. (250 words, 15 marks) Tag: Indian culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to give a brief account of** **Indian Philosophy.**-**In Body, elaborate how Indian Philosophy** **impacted India’s Architecture and arts in** **different phases.**-**Conclude by writing how people from** **different phases were keen to manifest and** **impart their traditions, values, and beliefs of** **Indian Philosophy.** **Answer:**The Indian philosophy and tradition**represent the** **complete fusion and synthesis of the consciousness**for art and beauty that had been**expressed in the form** **of architecture, visual and performing arts.**In the Indian context, from the**Buddhist stupa and chaitya** **to the Hindu temple, and then to the Muslim Mosque** **or the Christian church,** philosophy integrated deeply with religion has stimulated the art and architecture in India. The impact of Indian philosophy and tradition can be categorized in the period of ancient, medieval, and modern.

## **Impact of Indian Philosophy in the Ancient Period**

## **Harappan**

- The origins of Indian philosophy can be traced back to 2500 BC or even earlier. The great bath and citadel suggest a number of the religious practices of the Indus valley civilization can be found in contemporary Indian religious life. For example, in south India, both males and females mark their foreheads with ashes, sandalwood paste, or other materials.
- The excavations at Harappa, Mohenjo-Daro and several other sites revealed the existence of a very modern urban civilization with expert town planning and engineering skills.
- The swastika has tremendous philosophical and spiritual significance in contemporary India. Swastika has also been found among the Indus valley diggings. According to many scholars, the popular Hindu god Siva had his origins as an Indus valley deity which later gave rise to the cult of Shaivism.

## **Vedic Period**

- In Rig Veda, while most of the texts deal with rites and rituals, and beliefs and practices other parts deal with abstract philosophical concepts.
- The Vedic people awed and overwhelmed by the forces of nature, ascribed divinity to fire, earth, sky, thunder, sun, dawn, dusk, etc. The mostimportantfeatureoftheVedicperiodwas the making of fire altars which soon became an important and integral part of the social and religious life of the people even today.
- Rig Veda provides an extensive list of such Gods and Goddesses that forms the basis for the temple and associated various architecture like Nagara style, Dravida style.
- In the Vedic age, the major contribution was towards the written epics of Ramayana and Mahabharata, apart from the Vedas and Upanishads. Vedic literature became the ideological foundations for the future generation’s art practices. In the epics there is a mention of grand painted halls and walls of palaces, which suggest that mural art was a common feature in those times.
- Narrative architectures have been widely adopted as a medium of storytelling sculpture like the Pandavas and Draupadi can be found at the Dashavatara Temple in Deogarh or the main panel at Mahabalipuram shows the descent of Ganga etc.
- The literature, epics god and goddess, share many concepts such as dharma, karma, samsara, reincarnation, dukkha, renunciation, meditation, with almost all of them focusing on the ultimate goal of liberation of the individual through diverse range of spiritual practices (moksha, nirvana).

## **Emergence of Buddhism and Jainism**

- Emergence of Buddhism and Jainism aided in the development of early architectural style.
- Stupa, originally the focus of a popular cult of the dead, celebrates the Buddha ‘s parinirvana (end of cycle of suffering), symbolizes his eternal body, and is an object of worship.
- For political unification, Ashoka institutionalized a common Dhamma, a common language, and one script (Brahmi). He shared his thoughts and philosophy by engraving them on stone pillars and rock surfaces which are extraordinary examples of Mauryan architecture and applying science.

## **South India**

- When the Pallavas and the Cholas reigned, there was a tradition where the temple used to be the central place in a village for societal communication showing the importance of Community.
- They extensively developed the tradition of building temples, for example, the ‘ratha’ at Mahabalipuram style of temples, structural templessuchastheKailashnathandVaikuntha Perumal temples by the Pallavas. The temple building process imparted unity in diversity.

## **Impact of Indian Philosophy in the Medieval Period**

- Mughal Emperor Akbar’s Dīn-i-Ilāhī known during its time as Tawḥīd-i-Ilāhī (“Oneness of God”) or Divine Faith, intending to merge some of the elements of the religions like Islam, Hinduism, and Zoroastrianism, Christianity, Jainism, and Buddhism. The synthesis between different cultures gave birth to new philosophical and religious traditions, ideas, forms and styles in almost all spheres of culture.
- The philosophy of Din-I-ilahi and Ṣulḥ-i-kul (Universal Peace) imparted in Indo-Islamic style of architecture. Fatehpur sikri, Tomb of salim Chishti, Tajmahal exhibits social, political and religious integration.
- In the realm of art, one witnesses the development of new styles of painting associated with the patronage of the Mughal and the Rajput style. The large number of forts, palaces, temples and Mosques that can be dated to this period are examples of the new styles.

## **Impact of Indian Philosophy in the Modern India**

- During the British colonial period, European styles including neoclassical, gothic revival, and baroque became prevalent across India.
- The amalgamation of Indo-Islamic and European styles led to a new style, known as the IndoSaracenic style.
- The economic reforms of 1991 further bolstered the urban architecture of India as the country becamemoreintegratedwiththeworld’seconomy. Traditional Indian philosophy of **Vastu Shastra**remains influential in India’s architecture during the contemporary era. The above examples show**how people from different** **phases were keen to manifest and impart their** **traditions, values, and beliefs of Indian philosophy** through art, monuments, which helped to pass the knowledge through generations and through different styles.
[Question ID: 2020-gs1-q13-unacademy]

## Question 14 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2020-gs1-q14-unacademy**
**Q12. Persian literary sources of medieval India reflect the spirit of the age. Comment. (250 words, 15 marks) Tag: The salient aspects of Art Forms, literature, and Architecture from ancient to modern times.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, write briefly about Persian** **language advent in Indian subcontinent.**-**In Body,**-**Write about the various Persian literary** **sources of medieval India.**-**Show how this literature reflects the** **spirit of the age, including the thought,** **social structure, culture, and political** **institutions.**-**In Conclusion, you write quotes suitable to** **intermingling culture and people.** **Answer:**The**Medieval period of 10th and 11th century is** **considered as an age of great cultural synthesis**in India. The Turks and Mughals introduced fresh ideas and helped in giving rise to new features in the areas of religion, philosophy and ideas, language, and literature. One of the hallmark features of this period was the**Persian language and its penetration into the** **literature and literary style.**The development of Persian literature on Indian SubContinent throws light on syncretic**intermixing and** **fusionofculture,religion,andthewayoflifeofpeople** of that time.

## **Birth of Sabaq-Hindi (Indian Style)**

- The development of Persian literature in the sub-continent entered a new era in the **writings of Amir Khusrau.**- His writing style represents the first instance of Persian styles being composed in the Indian context that is**Sabaq-i-Hindi (the Indian** **style).**-**Official language of Imperials and court**

## **chronicles**

- Like the Delhi sultanate, Persian continued as the official language of the Mughal court.
- Some of the important political and administrative works are reflected in the work of Abul Fazal’s Ain-e-Akbari which gives an account of the social, political, administrative and statistical record of Akbar’s reign.
- Thecourtchronicleswereanimportantfeature of the literature during this period. Some important of these were, the Qiran-us Sa’dain, Miftah-ul Futuh (dealing with the military success of Jalauddin Khalji), Tughlaqnama (describing Ghiyasuddin Tughluq’s rise to power).

## **Philosophy of Divinity in human beings**

- The Sufi literature which was in Persian developed a new form called the Malfuzat that was in the form of a dialogue of the Sufi saints. Example: Fawaid-ul Fu’ad written by Amir Hassan Sijzi. The sufi literature reflects ideas of Ruh(soul), Divine Proximity (Qurbat) and Hulul.
- Abu’l-Fazl, the author of the Akbar Nama, was a dynamic arguer and liberated thinker who constantly opposed the views of the conservative ulama and promoted the concept of questioning and liberal thinking.

## **Religious tolerance**

- During this period, there were many works that were translated into Persian from Sanskrit and Vice Versa. Example: The Tuti Nama (book of the parrot) by Zia Nakshabi was the first Persian translation of Sanskrit stories. The Mahabharatha and the Rajtarangini were translatedintoPersian.Thetranslationofwork resulted in Universal Peace, Brotherhood and Religious Tolerance and Respect for everyone.

## **Development of new culture and new language**

- The philosophical works of this period reflected the fusion of Persian and native Indian philosophies popularly known as Ganga-Jamuna Tehzeeb.
- The language Hindawi and Persian writing gave birth to the new language Urdu, which marked the new phase in Indian literature and language.

## **History/biography/memoirs**

- The Art of preserving Chronology, Historical events,memoirswasoneofthemostimportant features of Persian literature. Example the Tabaqat-I-Nasiri by Minaj-us Siraj, Futuhus Salatin by Isami and Ziauddin Barani’s Tarikh-I Firozshahi and the Fatwa-I Jahandari made the most important contribution to Persian literature during this period.

## **South India**

- Persian literature in the south received patronage from the Adil Shahi rulers of Bijapur and the Qutab Shahis of Golconda. Muhammad Hussain Tabrezi, Malik Qummi and Mulla Zuhuri were regarded as important Persian poets. This development gave the new feature of Urdu language integrated with the local language called Daskshini (Deccan) Urdu.

## **Growth of regional language**

- The development of Persian literature in the Mughal court played an important role and influenced the development and growth of regional literature. Languages like Punjabi, Pushtu, Sindhi and Kashmiri were strongly influenced by Persian. Persian literary sources **reflect religion, romance,** **and applaud struggle.**It avoids exaggeration, which is a form of distortion, and therefore of falsehood. But it is noteworthy that the Persian learning attracted both Hindus and Muslims all over the country. This according to**Sherwani “was a symbol of the general** **trend of the cultural goodwill which existed in the** **country.”** ---
[Question ID: 2020-gs1-q14-unacademy]

## Question 15 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2019-gs1-q15-unacademy**
**Q1. Highlight the Central Asian and Greco-Bactrian elements in the Gandhara art. (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Art and Culture.**

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to briefly write about the** **emergence of Gandhara art.**-**In Body,**-**Write various Central Asian and Greco-** **Bactrian elements in the Gandhara art.**-**In Conclusion, try to write about the overall** **significance and impact of Gandhara art.** **Answer:**Gandhara art flourished from 1st century BCE to 4th century CE in the North west part of India came under the political influence of the different kingdoms like Greco- Bactrians, Pratihars, Kushanas, Shakas etc. which resulted in the emergence of mixed school of arts. It developed in India during the**Kushana period** **and was**one of the major schools of art in the history of ancient India.**Central Asian and Greco-Bactrian elements in the**

## **Gandhara art**

- **Central Asian elements** - Halo behind the head of Buddha was inspired by ancient Persian solar deities.
- Curly hair of Buddha is similar to Parthian style.
- The drapery in the statue of Buddha was similar to Parthian drapery in Hellenistic cultures.
- The Buddhist inscription in the Kharosthi letters were found in the region.
- **Greco-Bactrian elements** - BuddhainGandharaarthasayouthful,Apollo (Greekgod)likeface.ManyBuddhasculptures include Greek god Hercules as protector.
- Buddha in Gandhara art is dressed like Roman imperial statue and for the first time Buddha is represented in a human-like figure.
- The decorative motifs like fruit garland, scrolls etc. resemble Greek tradition.
- There was massive **use of black stone and stucco,** whichwasatypeofplasterusedformanystructures including the freestanding ones under this style of art. The free-standing structures included many chaityas, Viharas and stupas.

- The striking feature of Gandhara School of art shows a **very realistic and natural depiction of** **features in perfection.**Though Gandhara art has been influenced by Central Asian and Greco Bactrian elements it emerged as a unique art form in India also. Because of its**strategic** **geographical position, the region became a meeting** **place of cultures.**These techniques were**endured till** **the epoch of Gupta.**Gupta’s realistic anthropomorphic representations of the Buddha**reflect the legacy of the** **Gandhara artistic influence.** ---

## Question 16 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2019-gs1-q16-unacademy**
**Q2. The 1857 Uprising was the culmination of the recurrent big and small local rebellions that had occurred in the preceding hundred years of British rule. Elucidate (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Modern History.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to briefly write about** **rebels and rebellions in the first 100 years of** **British rule in India.**-**In Body, write how the rebels and rebellions** **culminated in the great revolt of 1857.**-**In Conclusion, try to write overall reasons** **for rebellions and their culmination in the** **1857 uprising.** **Answer:** **AccordingtoC.A.Bayly,theIndianRebellionwasnot** **one movement, but many.**The first century of British rule has witnessed a series of uprisings which were termed as ‘restorative rebellions.’. The main objective behind the rebels and rebellions was to restore the old regime against the changes incorporated by British. The changes incorporated by British affected mostly the peasants, tribals, soldiers, local rulers, Mughal officials and disposed zamindars who started the uprisings.**Recurrent Rebellions Occurred in the Preceding**

## **Hundred Years of British Rule**

- **Sanyasi and Fakir Rebellion:** The sanyasi rebellion which was centered in Murshidabad and Baikunthapur took place in the 1770s. In 1771,150 unarmed Sanyasi were killed under the orders of Warren Hestings.Fakir uprising was started after the British annexation of Bengal by Fakirs or wonderingMuslimmendicants.Therewerealmost 50000 participants involved in those rebellions
- **Pagal panthi Revolt:** The revolt was started against the oppressive tax regime of the British.
- **Faraizi Revolt:** Led by Shariatullah Khan and Dadu Mian, Faraizi Movement (1838-1848) was the first ever no-tax campaign to support the cause of the tenants against the landholders and British government. It took place at Faridpur, Bakarganj and Mymensingh district of Bengal.
- **The Wahabi Movement (1830s-1860s)** was primarily religious in its origin, but became a class struggle in some places.
- The **peasant revolts**took place against aggressive and assertive economic policies which devastated the traditional agrarian system of India and deteriorated the condition of peasants.**Paika** **Rebellion (1817)**in Odisha took place against the Land revenue policy of British.**Moplah Uprisings** **(1836-1854)** also demanded reduction in revenue and oppression of officials.
- The British officials brought with them traders, moneylenders, zamindars and merchants in the tribal areas that resulted in the **Santhal Rebellion** **(1855-57).**-**Bhil uprising**in Khandesh (present day Maharashtra and Gujarat) during 1818-1831 and**Kol uprising** in Chhota Nagpur and Singhbhum region,Bihar,Odishaduring1831–1832 areother major uprising against the Britishers oppressive policies.
- **The Vellore mutiny (1806) and other such sepoy** **mutinies**were against the oppressive socioreligious policies of Britishers. The**colonial administration ended the relative** **isolation of tribes**and brought them fully within colonization which led the tribal uprising. There were**over forty major rebellions**excluding hundreds of minor ones from 1763 to 1856. The**economic** **exploitation, racial and discriminatory policies,** **religion interference, ruthless approach**to suppress the uprisings**finally culminated in the revolt of 1857** which provided a platform to all discontented sectors to raise their voice against company rule.

## Question 17 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2019-gs1-q17-unacademy**
**Q11. Many voices had strengthened and enriched the nationalist movement during the Gandhian phase. Elaborate. (15 Marks, 250 words) Tag: Modern India.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to briefly write about the** **Gandhian Phase.**-**In Body,**-**Write** **different** **ideologies** **and** **philosophies developed in the Gandhian** **phase which strengthen the nationalist** **movement.**-**In Conclusion, try to mention the overall** **importance of the Gandhian Phase and** **different voices.**
**Answer:** **The Gandhian phase (1917-47)**was the final phase of Indian National Movement, which was characterised by truth and non-violence and gave new orientation and perspective to Indian freedom struggle. Gandhiji remarkably guided the masses based on his experience from South Africa and tour of India. In this phase, there were many other ideas and personalities, which**strengthened Indian National movement to a great** **extent.** **Many Voices Strengthened and Enriched the National**

## **Movement**

- **Champaran Satyagraha: It is treated as the first** **Civil Disobedience Movement in India,** in which many popular leaders like Rajendra Prasad, Mazharul-Haq, Mahadeo Desai, Narhari Parekh, J.B. Kripalani etc. joined Gandhiji in the freedom struggle.
- **Ahmedabad Mill Strike and Kheda Satyagraha:** **Leaders like Anusuya Sarabhai, Sardar** **Vallabhbhai Patel etc.** organised the masses for struggle and politicised common people for putting pressure on the government.
- **Khilafat movement:**The movement was initially launched by Indian Muslims against British Government’sdecisiontodismembertheOttoman empire. Shaukat Ali and Muhammad Ali, Maulana Azad, Ajmal Khan and Hasrat Mohani etc. were main voices of Indian Muslim strengthened**Non-** **Cooperation movement and promoted Hindu** **Muslim unity.**-**During the Jallianwala Bagh Massacre (1919),** **Rabindranath Tagore renounced** Knighthood in reaction to the government’s action.
- **Swarajist and no changers:**After the NonCooperation Movement there were divided opinions in the Congress. A section of Indian National Congress led by Ballabhbhai Patel Rajendra Prasad carried out constructive programme of removal of untouchability, promotion of Hindu Muslim unity, promotion of women education, fight against caste system etc. Swarajist , other section of Indian National Congress,**led by C.R. Das and Motilal Nehru,** participated in the central legislative assembly to wreck the government from within.
- **Marxism and other Socialist Ideas:**From 1920s onwards the ideas of Marxism and socialism spread rapidly in India.**Communist party**was established in India in 1925. A section of Indian National Congress was also deeply moved by the idea of socialism.**Jawahar Lal Nehru, Subhash** **Chandra Bose**preached the idea of socialism. Congress Socialist party was established in 1934 under the leadership of**Jayaprakash Narayan,** **Ram Manohar Lohia and others.** The movement strengthened the voices of marginal classes and connected them with mainstream movements.
- **The idea of armed revolution:**The idea of armed revolution led by revolutionaries like**Chandra** **Shekhar Azad, Ram Prasad Bismil, Bhagat Singh**in Punjab and**Surya Sen in Bengal** strengthened national feelings. Their sacrifices, the idea of uprooting the British Government through armed revolution touched the heart of mass.
- Subhash Chandra Bose, Mohan Singh, Rashbehari Boseetc.strengthenedthemovementfromoutside **by organising Indian National Army (1942).**- During the Civil Disobedience Movement (1930) and**Dandi March leaders like K. Kelappan, C.** **Rajagopalachari** etc. popularised it in different parts of India.
- **Efforts of Dr. B R Ambedkar helped the people** **of depressed class and strengthen their voice for** **equality in the society.**-**Women participation:**The women participation in Indian freedom struggle during this phase was also remarkable.**Sorojini Naidu, Pritilata** **Waddedar, Aruna Asaf Ali, Usha Mehta, Vijay** **Laxmi Pandit** were some of the prominent women freedom fighters.
- Business class people organised the All **Indian** **Trade Union Congress** and contributed to the struggle for independence to a large extent.
- **Apart from the main leaders, farmers, students,** **workers, lawyers, soldiers and common people** **played a very important role in the nationalist** **movement during this phase.** The Gandhian phase many voices in the form of different ideologies, different ideas contributed to Indian freedom struggle to a large extent and strengthened nationalist movement. The collective efforts of people alone with different views and ideas played a very important role in politicizing every section of society and exerted pressure on the British government for independence.

## Question 18 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2019-gs1-q18-unacademy**
**Q12. Assess the role of British imperial power in complicating the process of transfer of power during the 1940s. (15 Marks, 250 words) Tag: Modern India.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to briefly write about** **transfer of power and British intentions.**-**In Body,**-**Write the role of British in complicating** **the process of transfer of power.**-**In Conclusion, try to mention the impact of** **complex transfer of power process on India-** **Pakistan relations.** **Answer:**British never wanted to leave a productive colony like India. Due to the impact of world war and intensity of national movement in 1940s. British tried to gain control back and suppress the demand for independence through various proposals. They complicated the process of transfer of power to further extend their rule in India.**RoleofBritishinComplicatingtheProcessofTransfer**

## **of Power**

- **August Offer (1940):** The Viceroy Linlithgow proposed creation of constituent assembly after war and the Dominion status created opposition among Indian leaders. Such provisions symbolised the unwillingness of British government to grant complete independence to India and launched just to gain support of Indians in world war.
- **Cripps Mission (1942):** Due to the threat of Japanese army and pressure from Allied countries to seek Indian cooperation, Cripps proposals were announced. But Provisions like the right to secede to the provinces threatened Indian unity and the takeitorleaveitattitudeofthegovernmentcreated deadlock between leaders and British government.
- **Wavell Plan (1945):** To solve the constitutional questionsinIndiatheseproposalswereannounced. It tried to reduce the status of the Congress as a Hindu party and provided a virtual veto power to strengthen their position, which exposed the real dividing nature of British and created drift among leaders and government.
- **Cabinet Mission (1946): A change in the attitude** **of the government was visible after the election** **of the Labour Party in Britain, which was** **sympathetic towards the Indian movement.** - It was also an extremely complicated system, with some uneasy proposals of creation of Dominion of India, groupings of provinces etc. which created conflict among leaders and political parties like the Congress and the Muslim League were opposing it.
- The Muslim League also called for the Direct Action Day, which resulted in communal riots betweenHindus&Muslimsinthecountryand created necessity for creating the constituent assembly.
- **Atlee’s statement (1947):** It fixed a deadline for British to leave Indian, without solving the existing issues in transfer of power. It reflected the intention of partition and Balkanisation of India.
- **Mountbatten plan (1947):** It developed a plan for freedom along with Partition of India. It allowed referendums in bordering provinces. It created a situationofturmoilandconfusionwhichledtothe large-scale migration of the people and intensified the communal riots in the country.
- **Boundary commission:** Cyril Radcliffe worked on the boundary line using census reports and some maps. He was not a professional and had no specialized knowledge needed for the task. In the short time duration, without gathering enough information and survey, they drew a line on the map declaring it as a new boundary. It further intensified the communal riots.
- **Princely states:** At the time of transfer of power, the princely states were made free and they were allowed the options either to join India or Pakistan or to remain independent. This decision of the British complicated the process of integration of princely states and created threat of Balkanisation of the country.
- Few princely states like Kashmir, Junagadh and Hyderabad posed some serious challenges. Due to the **complex process of transfer of power by** **British government,** India’s first years of freedom were plagued by the tragic legacy of partition. Refugee resettlement, economic disruption and inadequate resources for virtually every need, continuing communal conflicts. Since after gaining independence the border dispute, conflict at border and direct and indirect conflicts are common between both the countries.

## Question 19 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2018-gs1-q19-unacademy**
**Q1. Safeguarding the Indian art heritage is the need of the moment. Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words) Tag: Indian culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to define Indian art** **heritage. Also highlight the components of** **Indian Art heritage.**-**In Body, elaborate the need to safeguard the** **Indian art heritage.**-**Conclude by giving examples of schemes or** **Constitutional backing to safeguard Indian** **art heritage.** **Answer:**Cultural heritage is the legacy of cultural resources and intangible attributes of a group or society that is inherited from past generations. India’s cultural heritage is not only one of the most ancient, but it is also one of the most extensive and varied. The highlights of Indian heritage lie in the treasure of its art, architecture, classical dance, music, flora and fauna, and the innate secular philosophy of its people. The different components of heritage are:**Indian art heritage is “fragile wealth”, and they are** **required to be preserved and cherished because once** **lost, they are nonrenewable. Also, there are other** **factors for which this fragile wealth is needed to be**

## **safeguarded**

- According to IUCN, which helps the world find pragmatic solutions to the most pressing environmental and developmental challenges, **naturalworldheritagesitesarenotjustimportant** **environmentally, they also provide social and** **economic benefits.**-**WWFsurveyestimatesthat,“morethanhalfofthe** population depend directly on world heritage sites for food, water, shelter, and medicine. Harmful industrial development poses a threat to these ecosystem services and communities that depend on them.”
- **Many Indian art forms**are on the verge of extinction such as**Tikuli art and the Patna kalam** **Painting**of Bihar to**Parsi Ghara embroidery.**-**Around 197 languages in India are either** **vulnerable, endangered or extinct.** The extinct languages are Ahom, Andro, Rangkas, Sengmai, Tolcha -- all spoken in the Himalayan belt.
- **Large number of historic monuments and** **heritage buildings are in a pathetic state** which needs urgent attention like the mausoleum of Amanat Khan Shirazi and the historical site of Pul Kanjri in Amritsar.
- The traditional toys of India like **Channapatna,** **Kondapalli, or Galar putul** are on the verge of extinction due to inundation of Chinese toys in Indian market.
- Art heritage **represents “unity in diversity” of** **India**and builds a bridge between people living abroad to get connected with their native country. Preserving the heritage is enshrined as a Fundamental Duty (Article 51 A) in the Indian Constitution. Also, Article 49 makes it clear that the obligation to protect these sites are of the State. Institutions like, Archaeological Survey of India (ASI) are charged with preservation of**physical and tangible heritage**that is accumulated in the ancient monuments and archaeological sites. India has successfully inscribed 13 Intangible Cultural Heritage (ICH) elements in the UNESCO Representative List of Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity under the 2003 Convention. Schemes like ‘Adopt Heritage’ and**Heritage City Development** **and Augmentation Yojana (HRIDAY) will give more** **thrust in safeguarding Indian heritage.** <p align="center"><img src="images/unacademy_p536_img41.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>
