## Question 20 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2018-gs1-q20-unacademy**
**Q2. Assess the importance of the accounts of the Chinese and Arab travelers in the reconstruction of the history of India. (10 marks, 150 words) Tag: Indian culture. (The salient aspects of Art Forms, Literature and Architecture from ancient to modern times).**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to start by giving a brief** **introduction and importance of Chinese** **and Arab travelers.**-**In Body, provide description and importance** **of the Chinese and Arab travelers.**-**Conclude, stating how the details gathered** **from the travellers, helped in linking the** **history of India.** **Answer:**The Chinese and Arab travellers came from vastly**different social and cultural environments,**they were often more attentive to everyday activities and practices which were taken for granted by indigenous writers, for whom these were routine matters and not worthy of being recorded. They left elaborate records of their travels often mentioning facts which**helped** **historians to construct the past.**Chinese travelers mostly**visited India during the** **Gupta and Post-Gupta period while notable Arab** **travelers can be seen during the medieval period.**The importance of their accounts of travelling in India are as follows:**ImportanceofChinesetravelersinthereconstruction** **of the history of India:** **FaxianorFaHien:ABuddhistmonkwhosepilgrimage** in India between 402 AD to 405 AD during Chandra Gupta Maurya time.

## **Importance**

- His writings attest that **intermingling of people,** **monks, and students** were there in ancient times. He also contributed to initiate Sino-Indian relations.
- After his return to China, he translated many Sanskrit Buddhist texts in Chinese which he had carried with him and **hence, contributed to** **propagate Buddhism and Sanskrit literature to** **the Indian Subcontinent.**-**His accounts are filled with geographical** **references as he had mentioned about the Silk** **Route.** - He travelled through the Gupta Empire and wrote

## **down his impressions about India**

- **Political Condition and Administration:** The administration of the Guptas was liberal, the people enjoyed economic prosperity and the burden of taxes on them was not heavy.
- **The Social Condition:** The people were prosperous and content with their lives. Public morality was high.
- **The Religious Condition:** Buddhism and Hinduism were the most popular religions at that time.
- **Pataliputra:**He extensively wrote about Pataliputra, the Capital city. He described that there were separate Sangha’s, both Hinayana**and Mahayana**sects, which provided education to students gathered from all parts of India. The Palace of Emperor Asoka also existed at that time and provides us some usefulinformationaboutthesocial,economic, religious, and moral conditions of the Indian people of the Gupta age.**Hieun Tsang:**The**Chinese traveler Hiuen Tsang**visited India during**the period of Emperor Harsha** **during 629 AD.**

## **Importance**

- He wrote a detailed description of his tour in his book **‘Si-yu-ki’ or ‘Record of the Western** **Countries’.**- His description has been accepted as**the best** **available source** of knowing the administrative, social, and cultural condition of India at that time.

## **His books gave the glimpse of following things**

- **City-life of India.** The information gathered from his account is that the houses were of varied types andwereconstructedwithwood,bricks,anddung.
- The city-streets were circular and dirty. Many old cities were in ruins while new cities had grown up. Prayag was an important city while the importance of Pataliputra was replaced by Kannauj.
- Sravasti and Kapilvastu had lost their religious importance. Instead, Nalanda and Valabhi were the centres of Buddhist learning.
- He described that Indians used cotton, silk and wool for their garments and these were of varied types. He described Indians as lovers of education, literature, and fine arts.
- **Source of income:**Harsha divided his income into four parts, of which, the major source was landrevenue which formed 1/6th the produce of the land.**Importance of Arab travelers in the reconstruction of** **the history of India:** **Al-Biruni and the Kitab-ul-Hind: (973 AD – 1048** **AD): He came during Mahmud Ghazni’s reign.**His book gives a glimpse of India during**Medieval** **times.** It talks about religion and philosophy, festivals, astronomy, alchemy, manners and customs, social life, weights and measures, iconography, laws and metrology.

## **Importance**

- **Destructive effects of Mahmud’s Indian invasion:** Alberuni wrote that, “Mahmud ended the prosperity of India and so cruelly exploited and penalized the people that the Hindus became disgruntled like the dust particles”.
- Hinduism became a matter of history, “It means that there **came a downfall of Hinduism”.** The feeling of hatred prevailed in the hearts of remaining Hindus.
- **Feeling of Hindu about Muslim:** Alberuni wrote that Hindus suffer from some defects such as they desire to live in isolation from other countries.
- They considered the Muslims as ‘Mleccha’, as untouchables and boycotted them.
- **Political Situation:** He mentioned that the entire country was divided into small states which occasionally used to quarrel among themselves.
- Malwa, Sindh, Kannauj and Kashmir were prominent states among them.
- **Social System:** The caste system prevailed, and the spirit of seclusion and difference existed.
- Early marriages were largely in vogue.
- Parents arranged the marriages.
- He did not mention the system of dowry, but he wrote about the Streedhan which the relatives presented to her. **Ibn Battuta’s Rihla: He came in 1334 AD, the time** **when the Tughlaq dynasty was at its height in India.**

## **Importance**

- It is Battuta’s chronicles which give an **insight into** **the postal courier system** that was operational in India.
- Ibn Battuta’s account also discussed Jogis, **who** **would perform magic**tricks on the streets and notes of the**practice of Sati.**- His descriptions of trees and fruits are most interesting, with an adoration for the jackfruit, which he termed the**‘Loveliest of all fruits in** **Hindustan’.**The description, certainly, helps historians in assessing the conditions of India during Ancient and medieval times. However, historians do not accept these descriptionsasentirelydependable.Therefore,itneeds to be corroborated and checked with the help of other contemporary sources but still they**are key sources for** **finding out the missing links in the reconstruction of** **Indian history.** ---

## Question 21 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2018-gs1-q21-unacademy**
**Q11. The Bhakti Movement received a remarkable re-orientation with the advent of Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu. Discuss. (15 marks, 250 words) Tag: Indian culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, define Bhakti Movement** **andgivebriefintroductionofShriChaitanya** **Mahaprabhu.**-**In Body, elaborate Teachings of Shri** **Chaitanya, Contribution and Impact of his** **teachings and ideas.**-**Conclude with Salimullah Khan’s, a noted** **Bangladeshi linguist, quote on Shri** **Chaitanya Mahaprabhu.** **Answer:**The Bhakti Movement attributes to the trend brought forward by several Hindu saints in medieval Hinduism that endeavoured to**begin religious reforms by** **adopting devotion to achieve salvation.**It originated in the eighth century in south India and swept over east and north India from the 15th century onwards, reaching its zenith between the 15th and 17th century CE. The greatest saint of the Bhakti movement in the early 16th century was Shri Chaitanya, popularly known as**Gouranga Mahaprabhu.**His mode of worshipping Krishna with ecstatic song and dance had a profound effect on**Vaishnavism.**

## **Teachings of Shri Chaitanya**

- Chaitanya was a supporter of the **Radha-Krishna** **cult.** - His teachings focused on ‘love’ from intense human love to divine love.
- He unlocked the doors of divine love to all by chanting and singing the glories of Krishna in the form of **Kirtans.** - Kirtan is not merely a religious song but a feeling of happiness emanating from love and devotion, accompanied by singing and dancing of the highest spiritual order when one can sense the presence of Almighty.
- He assumed that by singing kirtan alone, one could realize God because it carries the mind from the material world to the divine world.
- True to the Vaishnava way of life, Chaitanya stood for truth and non-violence.
- His teachings were simple and, therefore, had a universal appeal.

## **Contribution of Shri Chaitanya**

- He was the **chief proponent**of the Vedantic philosophy of**Achintya Bheda Abheda,** a Vedanta school representing the philosophy of inconceivable one-ness and difference.
- He founded **Gaudiya Vaishnavism,** i.e., Brahma Madhva Gaudiya Sampradaya.
- He presented **Bhakti yoga**and popularized the chanting of the**Hare Krishna Maha-mantra.**- He composed the**Shikshashtakam** (eight devotional prayers).
- His exposition of Rasalila is one of his most profound contributions to Indian philosophy.
- He was a champion of social liberation.
- He **denounced the caste system** and stood for the universal brotherhood of man.
- He was very much opposed to the domination of the priestly class and superfluous rituals and ceremonies.
- It was due to his attitude of social liberation that people of socially oppressed classes became his disciples.
- Indeed, one of his most favourite disciples was a Muslim named Haridas.

## **Impact of his teachings and ideas**

- Chaitanya died in 1533 in the premises of Jagannath temple at Puri while singing kirtan.
- His followers considered that **Shri Chaitanya** **merged with Lord Jagannath, whom he** **considered to be the embodiment of Krishna.** - After his death, his followers set his teachings together, collected his religious songs and organized themselves into a separate sect.
- The **six Goswamis of Vrindavan** were his prominent disciples who carried forward their master’s philosophy.
- Chaitanya’s teachings and message of love remain a great source of spiritual inspiration to the people of eastern India in particular.
- TheRadha-KrishnacultandChaitanya’spreaching through singing Kirtans generated remarkable impact in Bengal and Odisha, and the effect continues to be felt even today. Thus, by **universalizing**the Bhakti Movement in the Hare Krishna movement, he made a remarkable reorientation and contribution to the Bhakti Movement. SomeattributetohimaRenaissanceinBengal,different from the more well-known 19th-century Bengal Renaissance.**Salimullah Khan,**a noted Bangladeshi linguist, affirms, “Sixteenth-century is the time of Chaitanya, and it is the beginning of Modernism in Bengal.**The concept of ‘humanity’ that came into** **fruition is contemporaneous with that of Europe”.** ---
[Question ID: 2018-gs1-q21-unacademy]

## Question 22 (Year: 2017 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2017-gs1-q22-unacademy**
**Q1. How do you justify the view that the level of excellence of the Gupta numismatic art is not at all noticeable in later times? (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Gupta numismatic art, Art and culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to write the status of art** **and culture in the Gupta age briefly. Why is** **it called the ‘golden age’.**-**In Body,**-**Write briefly about Coinage and its** **features.**-**Also, write Coinage of post-Gupta age.**-**In Conclusion, try to write an assessment of** **Coinage of Gupta age and Post Gupta age.** **Answer:**The Gupta dynasty ruled around the th**early 4 century**th . Renowned for their gold coins, and**to 6 century C.E**the Gupta numismatic art has a plethora of coins in an array of designs. They also distributed silver coins. However, coins made of copper, bronze or any other alloy metals were limited. This plethora of gold coins from the Gupta era has led some scholars to regard their reign as the ‘**rain of gold.**

## **Excellence of the Gupta Numismatic Art**

- The Gupta gold coins are acknowledged **as Dinars,**they are the most extraordinary examples of**and** numismatic and artistic excellence.
- Gupta coinage attained the height of **metallurgy** **and iconography.** Being indigenous, they portrayed the king, queen, and deities with Indian attire and with lots of grace.

## **The coins had the following features and themes**

- **Royal themes:** Image of king and queen involved in various ceremonies. For example, coin depicting Chandragupta I and queen Kumaradevi etc.
- **Themes depicting valour:** Samudragupta carrying an axe, bow and arrow, standing with a dagger, Skanda Gupta sitting on an elephant and hunting a tiger in one of the coins.
- **Religious themes:** Image of a female goddess in almost every coin, an image of the king holding an animal at the altar for sacrifice, ruler performing Ashwamedha Yajna.
- **Cultural themes:** King Samudragupta playing the Veena.

## **Coinage of Post Gupta age**

- Post Gupta age, we find a decline in numismatic art. **Coins were rare during the period 900 A.D-** **1200 A.D.**- It was emphasized that the early Medieval age was**inflicted with monetary anaemia, and from the** **side of Coinage, it was a dark age.**- The recent Historical researchers had brought to light that monetization was never wholly absent in India & its level was reasonably**high during 900** **A.D-1200 A.D.**- TherewasalsoageneralpaucityofCoinagecreated by the**declining internal trade and weakening of** **a powerful centre.**- The newly**emerging independent and self-** **sufficient local units** (Shrenis) added to the sharp decline in the number and purity of later Gupta coins.
- The level of excellence of the Gupta numismatic art declined in later times. **Islamic rulers like the** **Delhi sultanate and Mughals did not feature on** **the coins** and preferred minting teachings from the Quran instead.
- The Post-Gupta coins became monotonous with slight or no creative changes at all. A general atmosphere of peace and prosperity assisted in creating a civilization conducive to cultural advent and social growth. **The Gupta period is treated as the** **“Golden Age” of classical India.** The flourishing state of the economy was ascertained by a large number of gold coins circulated by different Gupta rulers. In the post-Gupta period, numismatics quality declined due to reduction in trade and frequent conflict for power.

## Question 23 (Year: 2016 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2016-gs1-q23-unacademy**
**Q1. Early Buddhist Stupa-art, while depicting folk motifs and narratives, successfully expounds Buddhist ideals. Elucidate. (12.5 marks, 200 words)**

#

## Answer

- **Tag:** Indian culture.

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to start with the** **definition of Stupa and give examples of it** **(like Sanchi, Amravati, etc.).**-**In Body, mention how Stupa depicts ideas** **of Buddha like his birth, mahaparinirvana,** **three jewels of Buddhism. Elaborate them** **briefly.**-**Conclude with the statement like ‘early Stupa** **arts expound Buddhist’s ideals. Here one** **can also quote Art-historian Vidya Dehejia** **on relics.** **Answer:**The most fundamental of Buddhist architectural monuments, the**Buddhist Stupa**serves as a marker for a sacred space, and it is a**symbolic representation** **of the Buddha’s burial mound.**A**Stupa** is a moundlike structure containing relics that is used as a place of meditation. They were built of huge mounds of mud, enclosed in carefully burnt small standard bricks. Buddha’s life and his ideas are depicted on various panels of Stupas. Sanchi, Sarnath, Amaravati and Bharhut Stupas are the oldest examples of early Buddhist Stupa art.

## **Buddhist Stupa-art, depicting Buddhist ideals**

- The great Stupa at Sanchi, **is one of the earliest** **Stupas** which was first constructed by the 3rdcentury-BCE Mauryan ruler Ashoka and later expanded to twice its original size in stone.
- The great Stupa at Sanchi shows **Buddha’s birth by** **motifs** of Maya being flanked by two elephants.
- InSanchiStupa,thecrossbars,andtheintervening **square between them are covered with relief** **sculptures depicting the events of the Buddha’s** **life.**Here, Buddha is depicted throughout in symbolic form,**by a wheel, an empty throne, or a** **pair of footprints.**- The hemispherical mound at Stupas represents**symbolic association i.e., the mountain home of** **the gods at the center of the universe.**- A central pillar supporting a triple-umbrella form (chattra)**represents the three Jewels** of Buddhism,

## **which are the keys to a true perceptive of the faith**

(a) Buddha; (b) Dharma (Buddhist teachings or religious law); and (c) Sangha (monastic community).

- Most of the relics at Stupas represent **Jataka tales** **narrating the stories and ideals of Buddhism**such as**ahimsa or non-violence and nature** **conservation** are also shown through the animal motifs like lion, bull, horse, stag etc.
- Thecarvingsportrayscenesinwhichobeisance **is being paid,**for example, to the footprints of the Buddha. One scene, considered**to depict** **the Buddha’s departure from the palace,** shows a riderless horse.
- Such works show that **early Buddhism** **prohibited depiction of the Buddha in bodily** form but allowed representation by certain symbols.
- In **Bharhut Stupa,**the medallions on the railing portray various Jatakas, or stories from the life and conception of the Buddha, and images of devotees and donors along with fertility nymphs (shalabhanjikas), yakshas, lions, floral motifs and pictures of many personages including what looks like a Yavana (Greek) warrior. The early Stupa arts expound Buddhist’s ideals, and, in this regard,**Art-historian Vidya Dehejia**is of the view that**the Buddha’s relics are not intended for public** **viewing.**They are**interned in a Stupa and devotees** **visit the Stupas to “experience proximity to the** **Buddha”.**The relics are believed to contain Buddha’s living essence. They**are thought to retain and be** **infused with the quality that animated and defined** **the living Buddha.** ---
[Question ID: 2016-gs1-q23-unacademy]

## Question 24 (Year: 2016 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2016-gs1-q24-unacademy**
**Q2. Krishnadeva Raya, the king of Vijayanagar, was not only an accomplished scholar himself but was also a great patron of learning and literature. Discuss. (12.5 marks, 200 words) Tag: Indian culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to mention about** **Krishnadeva Raya.**-**In Body, elaborate on Krishnadeva Raya’s** **contribution in literature.**-**Conclude with the statement like** **Krishnadevaraya of Vijayanagara was at the** **apex of a south Indian cultural renaissance,** **etc.** **Answer:**One of the most important rulers of the Vijayanagar empire was Krishnadeva Raya who reigned from 1509 AD to 1529 AD. He was a great commander and an efficient administrator who is attributed to be the founder of the Tuluva dynasty. According to many historians,**Krishnadeva Raya was an accomplished** **scholar, and a great patron of art and literature.**His reignisregardedasagoldenageofKannadaliterature,**but he also patronized Telugu, Sanskrit, and Tamil** **poets in his court.**

## **Krishnadeva Raya’s contribution in literature**

## **Kannada Literature**

- **Krishna Deva Rayana Dinachari** in Kannada highlights contemporary society during Krishna Deva Raya’s time.
- **It is still debatable among historians whether** **the record was written by the king himself,** **but undoubtedly it is said to be one of the** **most authentic sources to interpret social life** **during his time.**- Kannada poet**Mallanarya, Satyendra Chola-** **kathe, Chatu Vittal-anatha** who wrote Veerasaivamruta, Bhava-chinta-ratna and Bhaga-vatha respectively, was patronized by Krishnadeva Raya.

## **Telugu Literature**

- His contribution in art and literature marked a zenith in vernacular literature as he composed a work on statecraft in Telugu known as the **Amuktamalyada.** - In his court, eight Telugu poets were regarded as the eight pillars of the literary assembly.
- Mythologically, it was believed that eight elephants were holding the earth in eight different directions.
- Therefore, the title **‘Ashtadiggajas’**was used to celebrate this belief and hence the court was also called**Bhuvana Vijayam (Conquest of the** **World).**- The most celebrated poets in his court were**AllasaniPeddanaandNandiTimmahawhowrote**the**Parijatapaharana.**- The period of the Krishnadeva Raya Empire is known as**Prabandha Period**i.e.,**semi-** **historical anecdotes about famous persons.**-**AllasaniPeddanawasgiventhetitleofAndhra** **KavitaPitamaha**(thefatherofTelugupoetry).**Svarocisha Sambhava**or**Manucharita** is his popular prabandha work and was dedicated to Krishnadevaraya.

## **Sanskrit Literature**

- Sanskrit literature was given patronage by the Krishnadeva Raya.
- **Vallabhacharya**who was the great poetphilosopher wrote**Madhurastakam.**- He has written many other works like**Vyasa** **Sutra Bhashya, Jaimini Sutra Bhasya,** **Bhagavata Tika Subodhini, Pushti Pravala** **Maryada**and**Siddhanta Rahasya**in Sanskrit. ItcanbesaidthatKrishnadevarayaofVijayanagarawas**at the apex of a south Indian cultural renaissance.**His**reign was a vibrant period of vernacularization**when old Sanskrit texts and traditions were being revived, translated and reformulated for new and broader audiences. It is still believed that**Krishnadeva Raya** **continues to hold sway over the public imagination** **in the southern states** as well as other parts of the country, even after the lapse of five centuries, for the great services he rendered to the Indian culture and literature in general and Telugu, Kannada, Sanskrit literature in particular.

## Question 25 (Year: 2015 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2015-gs1-q25-unacademy**
**Q1.The ancient civilization in Indian sub-continent differed from those of Egypt, Mesopotamia and Greece in that its culture and traditions have been preserved without breakdown to the present day. Comment. (12.5 marks, 200 words) Tag: Indian Culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, start your answer with a** **suitable introduction that describes the** **uniqueness of Indian Culture.**-**In Body, discuss the path of history and how** **Indian culture evolved with time.**-**Conclude answer with briefly analysing the** **character of Indian culture.** **Answer:** Many great civilizations have been developed all over the world during the ancient age. Some of them are contemporary. Many such civilizations have perished and have been replaced by different cultures. But the uniqueness of Indian culture is that its culture and traditions have been preserved without breakdown to the present day. Indian culture has had an enduring character. Despite major changes and upheavals significant threads of continuity can be traced throughout the course of Indian history right upto the present day. Harappan civilization flourished in the Indian subcontinent over 4500 years ago. Archaeologists have found evidence to showthatculturesexistedhereevenbeforethematurity phase of the Harappan civilization. This tells that India has a very long history. Even today the pattern of a house in an Indian village is not very different from that of a Harappan house. Some aspects of Harappan culture are still practised, such as, the worshipping of Mother Goddess and Pashupati. Similarly, Vedic, Buddhist, Jain and many other traditions continue to be followed even today. At the same time changes are evident in the multistoried buildings in the metropolitan cities like Mumbai and Delhi, quite unlike the Harappan houses that had only one storey. Continuity and change in Indian civilization has gone hand in hand. Remarkable feature of Indian culture is that along with continuity it has kept on changing, whereas the basic spirit of our culture continued. It has kept on discarding what was becoming irrelevant in the modern age. In the long history of India, there have been periods of ups and downs.Asaresult,movementshavegrownandreforms brought about. The reform movements in the Vedic religion brought about by Jainism and Buddhism in sixthcenturyBCandthereligiousandsocialawakening in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries in modern India are a few examples when Revolutionary changes were brought about in Indian thought and practices. Yet the thread of basic philosophy of Indian culture continued and still persists. Thus, a process of continuity and change has always been a feature of Indian culture. This shows the dynamic character of our culture. This has provided enduring character to Indian culture
[Question ID: 2015-gs1-q25-unacademy]

## Question 26 (Year: 2015 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2015-gs1-q26-unacademy**
**Q2. Mesolithic rock cut architecture of India not only reflects the cultural life of the times but also a fine aesthetic sense comparable to modern painting. Critically evaluate this comment. (12.5 marks, 200 words) Tag: Indian Culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the question**

- **In Introduction, try to start with the time** **period of the Mesolithic age.**-**In Body, discuss how rock cut architecture** **of the Mesolithic age reflected both cultural** **and aesthetic sense with their features.**-**Concludewiththecomparisontothemodern** **paintings with respect to the aesthetic sense.** **Answer:**The Mesolithic Age is the**period between the end** **of the Palaeolithic Age and the Neolithic Age.**The MesolithicAgeinIndialiesroughlyinbetween10,000**– 2,000 BCE.**The art and architecture conceived during this period is referred to as**Mesolithic art.** The art during this period mainly depicted man’s immediatesurroundings,thingsonwhichhesubsisted whereas the classical art essentially depicted what man saw and the modern art illustrated what man thought. Some of the Mesolithic sites in India are situated in Uttar Pradesh (e.g., Sarai Nahar Rai, Morhana Pahar), Madhya Pradesh (Bhimbetka), Rajasthan (Bagor), Gujarat (Alhaj, Valsana), Odisha, Tamil Nadu, and Andhra Pradesh. Some features of this era are-
- Microliths or small stone tools were used by the people in this Age which were made by blunting one or more sides with steep retouch. Examples include obliquely truncated blades, crescents, triangles, and trapezes. The art and architecture were limited to the rock and cave shelters.
- The Rock art is in the form of petroglyphs (rock paintings) and petroglyphs (rock engravings) of which Bhimbetka is a classic example. The art in these natural cave shelters depict the social life in those times especially related to hunting, sexual activities, childbirth, burial, etc.
- People in this age had, thus developed a clear aesthetic sense as most of the paintings portrayed the nature around them. The modern paintings on the other hand before the 19th century focussed more on ornate presentations than capturing the aesthetic value as evident by the bazaar paintings during the Company rule. Even contemporary Indian paintings of today exhibit a fine aesthetic sense, as they are mostly based on European neo-classical traditions. Whereas the abstract modern art incidentally lacks the aesthetics of classical and stone age art and appeal to only a selected class of people.

## Question 27 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2014-gs1-q27-unacademy**
**Q1.To what extent has the urban planning and culture of the Indus Valley Civilization provided inputs to the present-day urbanization? Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words) Tag: Indian culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to write briefly about** **Indus Valley Civilization and emphasize its** **Urban architecture.**-**In Body, elaborate on the town planning** **structure and its features.**-**In Conclusion, mention how the present** **urbanization planning in Indian Sub-** **continent can be traced back to IVC.** **Answer:** **Indus Valley Civilization (IVC) (2500–1700 BCE),**also called**Harappan Civilization,**the earliest known urban culture of the Indian subcontinent. The**most** **unique feature**of the Indus Valley Civilization was the**development of urban centers.**One of the**most** **outstanding features**of the Indus Valley Civilization**was their city planning.**The**excavations at Harappa** **and Mohenjo-Daro**have shown a lot of evidence of this.**Inputs Provided to the Present-Day Urbanization by** **The Urban Planning and Culture of the Indus Valley**

## **Civilization**

- **Wide range of urban and non-urban rural sites:** The urban or the ‘mature phase’ of Indus Valley Civilization includes a wide range of urban and non-urban rural sites.
- They varied in size and function but are inherently known for **several features**like the**town planning with defensive walls with** **impressive gates around the site, two or more** **divisions of the settlement at the site, drains,** **baked brick structures, brick sizes, etc.**-**Town Planning:**The Indus Valley Civilization possessed a flourishing urban architecture laid out on a**grid pattern**with provisions for an**advanced** **drainage system.**- The most important**innovation**at the time was the**standardization of the bricks in a size** **ratio very close to 4:2:1.**- The citadel, defense walls, dams, etc., prove the**existence of monumental architecture.**-**Mohenjo-Daro, Harappa, Rakhigarhi and** **Dholavirawerebyfarthelargesturbancenters** **of the IVC** evidently as important political and administrative regional centers.
- **CitadelandLowertown:Themetropolitancenters**were**internally divided into two or more parts:** **The Citadel for rulers and the Lower Town for the** **common people.**-**Private houses and Water resources:**The private houses were**oriented towards a central space,** with access from the street by an entrance that blocks the view of the interior of the house.
- The number of wells and their association with neighborhoods could indicate a need for discrete and relatively private water sources.
- **Great Bath and Granaries:**The large public structures have open access or provide a thoroughfare from one area of the site to another like the**“Great Bath” of Mohenjo-Daro,** and the “granaries” at Mohenjo-Daro and Harappa.
- **Drainage System:**One of the**most distinctive** **features of Indus Valley Civilization**was the**carefully designed and well-maintained drainage** **systems.**-**Wells and bathing platforms were lined with** **bricks,** and small drains carried water away from the wells or living area to larger street drains.
- The **street drains were equipped** with sumppits and the streets had bins for non-liquid waste, which was presumably collected and dumped outside the settlement.
- **Rectangular grid pattern:**The sites were laid out on**a rectangular grid of main streets** and smaller lanes with an efficient drainage system.
- The **grid-like arrangement**of the streets and the stark uniformity of the houses suggest rigid state control, the**first instance of town** **planning in the world.**The Indus Valley Civilization indicates a sufficiently advanced urban civilization, socio-economic and technological fabric capable of developing a complex urban infrastructure and political organization. The people of Indus Valley Civilization were traders par excellence, which to a certain**extent formed the basis** **of their urbanized status through trade contacts.**The presenturbanizationplanninginIndianSub-continent**can be traced back to IVC.** ---
[Question ID: 2014-gs1-q27-unacademy]

## Question 28 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2014-gs1-q28-unacademy**
**Q2. Gandhara sculpture owed as much to the Romans as to the Greeks. Explain. (10 marks, 150 words) Tag: Indian culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, write briefly about** **Gandhara sculpture and influence of foreign** **elements.**-**In Body, you can bring out the Roman** **Characteristics/Influence in Gandhara** **sculpture.**-**Conclude by stating that Gandhara** **School was heavily influenced by Greek** **methodologies, and at same time this school** **is also influenced by the Roman techniques** **to a greater extent.** **Answer:**Gandhara art, style, of Greco-Roman origin, seems to have**flourished largely**during the**Kushan dynasty.**Gandhara art presents some of the**earliest images of** **Buddha.**The Gandhara school incorporated many motifs and techniques from**Classical Roman and** **Greek art,**including vine scrolls, cherubs bearing garlands etc. The**basic iconography, however,** **remained Indian.**According to Historian V. A. Smith, “the Gandhara style of art that developed in sculpture was a**fusion of Greco-Roman and Indian styles”.**

## **Roman Influence on Gandhara Sculpture**

- The Gandhara region was open to **foreign** **influences**and one such influence from the west was**the Roman and Greek realism in art.**- The region came under the political influence of a variety of kingdoms like Greek, Mauryan, Parthian, Kushan, Synthian and this resulted in**the emergence of a mixed school of art,** whichborrowedfromvarioustraditionsofart.
- The Gandhara school **drew upon the** **anthropomorphic traditions of Roman religion** **and represented the Buddha with a youthful** **Apollo-like face,** dressed in garments resembling those seen on the Roman imperial statues.
- Standing figures of Buddha, deceived the **elusive** **stylistic actions of the Roman sculptor,** seeking to express majesties.
- The drapery was fundamentally Western, the folds and volume of dangling garments were carved with realness and gusto which earmarked the Gandhara sculpture **as based** **onaRomanandGreekclassicalvisualimpact.**- In Gandhara art, the**Hellenistic-Roman elements** are proposed to form the basis for the artist’s conceptions and design.
- The **Roman influence is also seen**in the**relief** **carvings which,**both in their artistic style and in much of their iconographical detail, are closely related to**Roman relief sculptures of imperial** **times.**- The**position of the figures, the treatment of** **the body and the architectural forms**used are all clearly derived from Roman models. It is said that Gandhara School was heavily influenced by Greek methodologies, the figures were more spiritual and sculpted mainly in grey, and detail was paid to have the exact depiction of body parts. But, at same time this school is**also influenced by the Roman** **techniques to a greater extent.** ---

## Question 29 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2014-gs1-q29-unacademy**
**Q3.Taxilauniversitywasoneoftheoldestuniversities of the world with which were associated a number of renowned learned personalities of different disciplines. Its strategic location caused its fame to flourish, but unlike Nalanda, it is not considered as a university in the modern sense. Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words) Tag: Indian culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to describe briefly about** **Taxila university.**-**In Body, substantiate why Taxila university** **is not considered as a university in the same** **modern sense as Nalanda university.**-**Conclude that despite not being considered** **as a university in the modern sense, many** **renowned learned personalities of different** **disciplines were associated with it.** **Answer:**Taxila university was a center of its own kind which is frequently referred**to as a seat of higher learning in** **Buddhistliterature.TaxilawasthecapitalofGandhara**in northwest India.**Taxila is perhaps the best known** **because of its association with Chanakya.**The famous treatise**Arthashastra by Chanakya, is said to have** **been composed in Taxila**itself. By some accounts,**Taxila university is one of the earliest universities**in the world, while**many do not consider it a university** **in the modern sense.** **Reasons Why Taxila University is not Considered** **as a University in the Modern Sense as Nalanda**

## **University**

- According to some scholars, **Taxila was not a** **university town**with lecture halls and residential quarters, which**can be seen at ancient Nalanda** **university.**- At Taxila,**the preceptor housed his own pupils,** who paid for their board and lodging in cash or in the form of service to the teacher and his family.
- TheBuddhistmonasteriesalsocateredtotheneeds **of the students and monks**at Taxila university. Whereas Nalanda university functioned from**the** **revenue collected from the surrounding villages.**- In Taxila university**the teachers living there may** **not have had official membership** of colleges, in contrast to the Nalanda university.
- The **centralized system of schooling or syllabus** **in Taxila was absent** as compared to Nalanda university.
- Theconceptoftheexaminationsystemanddegree **certificate** was absent in Taxila university.
- The education system was **not as systematic as in** **Nalanda university** and was modified according to a student’s Caliber.
- The teachers of Taxila had **complete autonomy,**and they designed their**own school of learning,**rules, and regulation, while in Nalanda university a well-defined structure of administration was followed. Still, Taxila university became famous as**a place of** **learninginancientIndia.Amongitsnotedpupilswere**the legendary Indian grammarian,**Panini.**He was an expert in language and grammar and authored one of the greatest works on**grammar called Ashtadhyayi.** **Jivaka,oneofthemostrenownedphysiciansinancient** **India,**studied at Taxila university. Students came to Taxila from Kashi, Kosala, Magadha and from other countries despite the long and arduous journey they had to undertake. Even though Taxila university is not seen in the same modern sense as Nalanda university, its**contribution in academics cannot be disputed.** ---

## Question 30 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

The ideas of Sufi and other saints were of great importance in the history of medieval India.

## **Reasons of their failure**

- The Sufi and other saints were nomadic in nature. They propagated their ideas by singing. People also received their messages. When the saints had left the place, people forgot the messages.
- Bhakti, Sufi movement lacked proper organizational structure. So, their messages failed to bring any change in the society.
- Sufi and other Saints failed to provide any effective alternative to the existing social customs and system. In absence of a proper alternative their messages fell on deaf ears.
- Since most of the saints came from the lower strata of the society. Society didn’t accept them readily. So, it was difficult for them to bring effective changes by uprooting the existing systems. Though they could not bring any effective changes the Sufi and other saints made a valuable contribution to Indiansociety.LiketheBhaktisaintswhowereengaged in breaking down the barriers within Hinduism, the Sufis too infused a new liberal outlook within Islam. The interaction between early Bhakti and Sufi ideas laid the foundation for more liberal movements of the fifteenth century

## Question 31 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2013-gs1-q31-unacademy**
**Q1.Though not very useful from the point of view of a connected political history of South India, the Sangam literature portrays the social and economic conditions of its time with remarkable vividness. Comment. (10 marks, 200 words) Tag: Literature.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- In Introduction, try to start with a brief introduction on the Sangam age.
- In Body, show how Sangam literature portrays the social and economic conditions of its time with remarkable vividness.
- Conclude by mentioning the significance of the Sangam literature. **Answer:**The Sangam age refers to that period in the early history of South India**(3rd Century BC to 3rd Century** **AD)**when large numbers of poems in Tamil were composed by several authors. The term**Sangam refers** **to an assembly or “meeting together” of Tamil poets.**The Sangam literature**portrays the economic life and** **social life of South India** of that age.

## **Economic Life of South India**

- The people of the Sangam age were engaged in various economic activities such as agriculture, crafts, and trade. Paddy was the most important crop.
- Among the crafts, the most important was spinning and weaving textiles, cotton as well as silk. Salt manufacture was another critical activity.
- ThemostimportantaspectoftheSangameconomy was flourishing trade with the Roman world.
- The growth of trade led to the rise of essential towns and craft centres in the Tamil region.
- Madurai,thecapitalofthePandyas,ischaracterized in the Sangam poems as a large city.
- It was an important centre of delicate textile and ivory working. Korkai, in the Tirunelveli district of Tamil Nadu, was a critical Pandya port. It was famous for its pearls.

## **Social Life of South India**

- The whole Tamilaham in this era was divided into five tinais or eco-zones, i.e., zones based on their economic resources. These were: Kurinji (hilly region); Palai (arid zone); Mullai (pastoral tracts); Marudam (wetlands); and Neital (seacoast). These zones were not demarcated and were scattered all around the region.
- Though the concept of Varna was known, social classes in the Sangam period were not pronounced by higher or lower rankings as in North India.
- Based on their occupation, people were known, such as artisans, salt merchants, textile merchants, etc.
- The rich lived in well-decorated brick houses and wore costly clothes, whereas the poor lived in mud huts and had scanty attire to wear.
- Women in the Sangam period appear to have been educated. Women are also depicted as engaged in various economic activities such as paddy plantation, cattle rearing, basket-making, spinning, etc.

## **Fails to Portray Political life of South India**

- Though there were three Sangams, only the last one consists of the elements related to their polity.
- War heroes occupied a unique position in society, and memorial stones called nadukal or virukkal were raised in honour.
- Most political events were exaggerated. Names of the territories and dynasties mentioned were imaginary and had no archaeological evidence supporting them. Even though the Sangam literature **fails to provide any** **factual information regarding the political history**of South India, it**portrays the social and economic** **conditions** of its time with remarkable vividness.
[Question ID: 2013-gs1-q31-unacademy]
---

---

## Question 33 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2013-gs1-q33-unacademy**
**Q2b. Chola architecture represents a high watermark in the evolution of temple architecture. Discuss. (5 marks, 100 words) Tags: Indian Culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- In Introduction, start with how temple architecture flourished under the Chola kings.
- In Body, discuss the features of Chola temple architecture.
- Conclude with mentioning their status and recognition in the current era. **Answer:** ThroughthepatronageoftheCholakings,theevolution of Dravidian temple architecture was artistically significant. The Chola temples like Brihadeshwar Temple of Thanjavur, Gangaikonda Cholapuram took the Dravidian style of temple architecture to a new

## **height. Some important features of these temples are**

- Temple is surrounded by a compound wall.
- The front wall has an entrance gateway in its centre, which is known as a gopuram.
- The shape of the main temple tower known as vimana is like a stepped pyramid that rises geometrically rather than the curving shikhara of North India.
- The word ‘shikhara’ is used only for the crowning element at the top of the temple which is
- usually shaped like a small stupika or an octagonal cupola— this is equivalent to the amlak and kalasha of North Indian temples.
- At the entrance to the South Indian temple’s garbhagriha, it would be usual to find sculptures such as of fierce dvarapalas or the doorkeepers guarding the temple.
- It is common to get a large water reservoir or a temple tank enclosed within the complex.
- Subsidiary shrines are either combined within the main temple tower or located as distinct, small shrines beside the main temple. Temple building was prolific under the Chola regime. Currently, over a hundred important temples of the Chola period are in a good state of preservation, and many more are still active shrines. Due to their artistic sophistication, many temples have been globally recognized and have been designated as UNESCO World Heritage Sites.

## Question 34 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q34-unacademy**
**Q3.What were the events that led to the Quit India Movement? Point out its results. (150 Words 10 Marks)**

#

## Answer

**Answer:** **Introduction:** TheQuitIndiaMovementwasstartedon8August1942 at Gowalia Tank, Maharashtra, during a time when the Indian freedom struggle was gaining momentum in World War II. It was a major event in India’s fight for independence, with many people, including women, joining in large numbers. Mahatma Gandhi’s slogan “Do or Die” became a powerful symbol of the fight against British rule.

## **Events that led to the Quit India Movement**

- **Failure of the Cripps Mission:** The British sent Sir StaffordCrippstoIndiawhichwasexpectedtogive India a constitutional solution but the proposal offered limited self-rule in the form of Dominion Status which disheartened the Indian masses.
- **Involvement of Indians in World War II:** Britain’s decision to involve India in World War II without consulting Indian leaders sparked widespread resentment and dissatisfaction among the Indian populace. Earlier, the Congress had proposed that India would support the war effort only if granted complete independence, a condition the British refused to meet.
- **Japanese Invasion:** The fear of a Japanese invasion during World War II in Southeast Asia caused widespread panic in India, leading many people to flee cities like Madras and Calcutta. This chaos fueled nationalist feelings and prompted leaders like Gandhi to take more aggressive actions, resulting in the Quit India Movement in 1942, as many believed that if the British left, Japan would have no reason to invade.
- **Economic Condition due to the War:** The economic conditions in India worsened during World War II, due to food shortage and the redirection of food supplies to Britain for soldiers, leading to severe shortages that affected millions.
- **Do or Die Speech of Mahatma Gandhi:**Mahatma Gandhi’s “Do or Die” speech on 8 August 1942 at GowaliaTankduringthismovementurgedIndians to take immediate action against British rule.**Results of the events which led to the Quit India**

## **Movement**

- **Widespread Participation:** The events united the Indian people, with participation from students, women, and workers in the Quit India Movement, strengthening the fight for freedom.
- **Mass participation:** Including that of women, was significantly influenced by the deteriorating economic conditions, which also affected women. Motivated by Mahatma Gandhi’s inspiring speeches, many women were encouraged to actively participate in the struggle against British rule.
- **Underground** **Movement/Activities:** The underground movement included activities such as secret radio channels and underground press aimed at spreading the ideas of Mahatma Gandhi and other initiatives, all carried out without the knowledge of the British authorities.
- **Parallel Government:** Post Movement several regions in India saw the establishment of parallel governments, set up by nationalist leaders to assert Indian authority and provide local administration independent of British rule. Such efforts reflected the growing resolve for self-governance among the Indian populace despite British repression.
- **Unity at the National Level:** This movement demonstrated a united approach, with participation from various segments of society, including women and students. Even Muslims, who did not participate directly, were noted for providing shelter to Indians fleeing British actions.
- **Leaderless Movement:**As a result of the movement, key Congress leaders were arrested; however, it continued to have a significant impact despite being leaderless, owing to widespread mass participation that resonated deeply with the Indian populace.**Conclusion:** The Quit India Movement was not an immediate response; it resulted from the culmination of unfulfilled promises by the British regarding complete independence or self-governance for India. This movement revived revolutionary sentiments and led to the establishment of parallel governments and underground activities. Despite the arrest of key leaders, the collective resolve of the Indian populace ensured that the movement left a lasting impact. Ultimately, it demonstrated India’s determination to resistanyformofdominationwhetheritisofBritishers or Japanese.
[Question ID: 2024-gs1-q34-unacademy]
---

---

## Question 35 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2023-gs1-q35-unacademy**
**Q2. What was the difference between Mahatma Gandhi and Rabindranath Tagore in their approach towards education and nationalism? (10 Marks, 150 Words)**

#

## Answer

## **Approach**

- **Introduction:** Define nationalism briefly in the early 20th century context.
- **Body:** Highlight contrasts between Gandhi and Tagore on nationalism and education, while noting areas of convergence.
- **Conclusion:** Conclude by showing how their diverse visions collectively enriched India’s national movement.

## **Answer**

## **Introduction**

- Nationalism in the early 20th century was both a unifying and contested idea, shaping India’s freedom struggle. Thinkers like Mahatma Gandhi and Rabindranath Tagore engaged with nationalism differently, and their educational philosophies mirrored these distinctions. **Body:** **Differences on Nationalism**

## **Mahatma Gandhi**

- Saw nationalism as rooted in **self-sufficiency** **and decentralization,** with villages as the foundation of nation-building.
- Advocated **compulsory nationalism,** linked to service and sacrifice.
- Criticized colonial nationalism as pugnacious, butbelievedIndia’snationalismshouldremain non-imperialist and people-centric.

## **Rabindranath Tagore**

- Considered nationalism a **“Maya” or mirage,** dangerous when elevated above humanity, especiallyafterwitnessingtheFirstWorldWar.
- Opposedanyformofcompulsorynationalism, seeingitasdestructivetouniversalhumanism.
- Warned that narrow nationalism could hinder peaceful coexistence with other nations. **Differences on Education**

## **Mahatma Gandhi**

- Criticized colonial education for fostering inferiority and ignoring skill-based learning.
- Proposed **Nai Talim,** emphasizing manual work, moral development, and holistic growth of the child.
- Education was a means to social reform, especially eradication of caste barriers.

## **Rabindranath Tagore**

- Believed education should promote **self-** **realization and creativity.**- Founded**Shantiniketan**(inspired by the Gurukul tradition) and**Visva-Bharati University** (drawing on Western liberal models).
- Advocated blending Western pedagogy with Indian traditions to nurture universalism.

## **Similarities**

- Both valued **non-violence, tolerance, and cultural** **pride.**- Shared love for**nature-based learning.**- Envisioned a**classless and humane society,**though Gandhi leaned towards decentralization and Tagore towards universal humanism.**Conclusion:** Despite their divergent approaches, Gandhi’s practical, community-driven model and Tagore’s universalist, humanist outlook enriched India’s struggle for freedom. Together, their philosophies provided complementary visions—one rooted in grassroots empowerment, the other in cultural and intellectual globalism—strengthening India’s quest for identity and independence.
[Question ID: 2023-gs1-q35-unacademy]

## Question 36 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2023-gs1-q36-unacademy**
**Q13. How did colonial rule affect the tribals in India and what was the tribal response to the colonial oppression? (15 Marks, 250 Words)**

#

## Answer

## **Approach**

- **Introduction:** Briefly state how colonialism disrupted tribal life, economy, and culture.
- **Body:** Explain colonial impacts (land alienation, exploitation, cultural disruption, missionary activities) and highlight tribal responses through major uprisings.
- **Conclusion:**Conclude by linking recurrent tribal resistance to the broader context of the 1857 revolt.**Answer:** **Introduction:**Colonial rule in India deeply impacted tribal communities by disrupting their socio-economic and cultural fabric. Policies on land, forests, revenue, and labor exploitation altered their traditional way of life, while external pressures from traders, moneylenders, and missionaries created further distress.**Body:** **Impact of Colonial Rule on Tribals**-**Displacement and Land Alienation:** British land settlements undermined the joint ownership traditions of tribals. The expansion of settled agriculture and influx of outsiders led to loss of land. Forest laws curtailed shifting cultivation, worsening displacement.
- **Economic Exploitation:** Tribals were forced into labor in plantations, mines, and industries under exploitative conditions. Police, traders, and moneylenders (mostly outsiders) added to their hardships.
- **Social and Cultural Disruption:** Tribal chiefs lost authority as British laws weakened traditional governance. Customary practices and cultural autonomy were undermined.
- **Missionary Activities:**Conversion drives and interference in customs disrupted tribal cultural and religious identity.**Tribal Response – Rebellions and Uprisings**

## **Early Uprisings**

- Paharia Rebellion (1778) – Rajmahal Hills.
- Chuar Uprisings (1766–72, 1795–1816) – Midnapore (Sham Ganjan, Durjan Singh).

## **Nineteenth-Century Major Revolts**

- Kol Mutiny (1831) – Ranchi, Singhbhum (Buddho Bhagat).
- Ho & Munda Uprisings (1820–22, 1831–37, 1899–1900) – led by Birsa Munda.
- Santhal Rebellion (1855–56) – Rajmahal Hills, led by Sidhu and Kanhu.
- Khond Uprisings (1837–56) – led by Chakra Bisoi.
- Koya Revolts (1803, 1840–62, 1879–80) – led by Tomma Sora.

## **Other Regional Movements**

- Bhil Revolts (1817–19, 1913).
- Khasi Uprising (1829–33) – under Tirath Singh.
- Singhphos’ Rebellion (1830–31, 1843) – led by Nirang Phidu.
- Ramosi and Koli Risings in Western Ghats. **Impact of Tribal Resistance** - Rebellions highlighted opposition to exploitation, land alienation, and cultural loss.
- These localized uprisings weakened colonial authority and built the spirit of resistance.
- Theycreatedatraditionofdefiancethatcontributed to the broader Revolt of 1857. **Conclusion:** Throughout the 18th and 19th centuries, tribal communities consistently resisted colonial exploitation. Their uprisings against land alienation, cultural erosion, and economic exploitation culminated in the Revolt of 1857, which drew strength from this century-long tradition of tribal defiance. Tribal struggles thus represent an inseparable part of India’s broader freedom movement.

## Question 37 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2022-gs1-q37-unacademy**
**Q2. Why did the armies of the British East India Company – mostly comprising of Indian soldiers – win consistently against the more numerous and better equipped armies of the Indian rulers? Give reasons. (150 words, 10 Marks) Approach**

#

## Answer

- **Introduction:** Give a brief about recruitment of Indian soldiers by the British East India Company.
- **Body:** Give the reasons why British East India Company’s army achieved consistent victories against the larger and betterequipped armies of the Indian rulers.
- **Conclusion:**conclude the answer with impacts of the British East India Company and how it shaped the course of Indian history .**Answer:** **Introduction:** Indians were recruited for the British East India Company’s army due to their familiarity with Indian conditions and willingness to accept lower wages. The armies of the British East India Company, primarily composed of Indian soldiers, achieved consistent victories against the larger and betterequipped armies of the Indian rulers due to several

## **reasons**

## **Body**

- **Superior Military Tactics:** The British East India Company employed more advanced military tactics and strategies compared to the traditional methods followed by the Indian rulers. They utilized disciplined formations, artillery, and firearms effectively, giving them an edge on the battlefield.
- **Technological Advancements:** The British had access to modern weaponry, including muskets, cannons, and rifles, which provided them with a significant technological advantage over the Indian rulers who relied largely on traditional weapons like swords, spears,and bowsand arrows.
- **Well-Organized and Trained Forces:** The British East India Company maintained disciplined and professionally trained armies. They had a wellestablished command structure, proper training, and efficient logistical support, which contributed to their success in battles.
- **Divide and Conquer Strategy:** The British employed a policy of exploiting existing divisions and rivalries among the Indian rulers. They formed alliances with certain Indian princes and played them against each other, weakening their collective resistance and making it easier to conquer them individually.
- **Economic and Political Factors:** The British East India Company had a strong financial base and the support of the British government. They could mobilize resources more effectively and sustain their military campaigns over extended periods. Moreover, they exploited political instabilities within Indian kingdoms and took advantage of the weakened central authority to expand their control.
- **Naval Superiority:** The British possessed a powerful navy, which enabled them to secure coastal areas and establish control over key ports and trade routes. This naval dominance allowed them to receive reinforcements, supplies, and reinforcements from Britain, giving them a strategic advantage in their military campaigns.
- **Effective Leadership:**Prominent British leaders like Clive, Hastings, Elphinstone, Munro, and Dalhousie demonstrated exceptional leadership skills. They were supported by capable secondary leaders such as Coote, Lake, and Wellesley, who fought for the cause and glory of their country. On the Indian side, leaders like Haidar Ali, Tipu Sultan,ChinQilichKhan,MadhuRaoScindia,and Yashwant Rao Holkar were also skilled, but lacked a well-trained team of secondary personnel.**Conclusion:** The Company became the dominant power in India after winning battles like Plassey, Wandewash, and Buxar.The consistent victories of the British East India Company’s armies over the more numerous and better-equipped armies of Indian rulers were a result of their military training, modern technology, political acumen, and skilled leadership. ThislaidthefoundationforBritishcolonialdominance in India, eventually shaping the course of Indian history and its relationship with the British Empire.

## Question 38 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2022-gs1-q38-unacademy**
**Q3 Why was there a sudden spurt in famines in colonial India since the mid-eighteenth century? Give reasons. Approach y Introduction: Write in brief about the numerous famines during the British era. y Body: Highlight the reasons responsible for a sudden spurt in famines in colonial India since the mid-eighteenth century. y Conclusion: Conclude the answer with the combination effect of man made policies.**

#

## Answer

**Answer:** **Introduction:**India experienced numerous famines during the British era, spanning from 1760 AD to 1943 AD. These famines resulted in a significant loss of life, and British policies contributed to their severity. The Great Bengal famine of 1770 caused a mortality rate of one to 10 million, the Chalisa famine of 1783-1784 , the Doji bara famine of 1791-1792, and the Agra famine of 1837-1838 were prominent ones. Overall, more than 85 million Indians perished in these famines, which can be seen as genocidal acts perpetrated by the British Raj.**Body: There was a sudden increase in famines in** **colonial India since the mid-eighteenth century due**

## **to several reasons**

- **British policies:** The British implemented exploitative economic policies, such as the extraction of agricultural resources and high land taxes, which resulted in the impoverishment of the Indian population and limited their ability to cope with food shortages.
- During the Great Bengal famine of 1770, The British authorities increased the land tax to 60 percent as a means to compensate themselves for the loss of lives suffered by numerous peasants.
- Fundsthatwererequiredtoaddressthefamine situation were redirected towards financing the British military campaign in Afghanistan during 1878-1880.
- During1943,adevastatingfamineoccurredin which three million people lost their lives and others were compelled to consume grass and human flesh in order to sustain themselves due to inflation engineered by the British Raj.
- The large-scale export of agricultural goods to England by the British resulted in a scarcity of food supply in India, eventually leading to a severe famine.
- **Land Revenue System:** The British introduced a permanent settlement that imposed heavy taxes on farmers, leaving them with limited resources to invest in agriculture and withstand crop failures. This system contributed to the vulnerability of the agricultural sector and increased the likelihood of famine.
- **Natural Causes:** The Bengal famine of 1769-70 occurred after two years of unpredictable rainfall that led to the drought and was further aggravated by a smallpox outbreak. Similarly, the famine of 1783-84 followed a widespread crop failure.
- **Commercialization of Agriculture:** The British colonial administration prioritized the commercialization of agriculture, focusing on cash crops for export rather than food crops for local consumption. This shift led to a decline in food production and a greater reliance on imports, making the population more susceptible to food shortages during times of crop failure.
- **Disruption of Traditional Systems:** The British policies disrupted traditional agricultural practices and systems that had previously helped mitigate the impact of natural disasters. These disruptions, such as the dismantling of irrigation systems and the conversion of agricultural land for other purposes, weakened the resilience of rural communities and their ability to withstand famines.
- **Lack of Investment in Infrastructure:**The British administration neglected to invest adequately in infrastructure, such as irrigation systems and transportation networks, that could have improved agricultural productivity and facilitated the distribution of food during times of scarcity. This lack of investment further exacerbated the impact of famines.**Conclusion:** The devastating famines of 1770, 1866, 1874, 1897, and 1943 had a manmade origin, resulting from colonial politics. Overall, the combination of exploitative policies, disruptions to traditional systems, and inadequate infrastructure investment under British colonial rule contributed to the sudden increase in famines in colonial India from the mideighteenth century onwards. These factors highlight the devastating impact of colonial rule on India’s agrarian economy and its people, leaving a lasting legacy on the nation’s history.
[Question ID: 2022-gs1-q38-unacademy]

## Question 39 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2021-gs1-q39-unacademy**
**Q11. To what extent did the role of the moderates prepare a base for the wider freedom movement? Comment. (15 Marks, 250 Words) Tags: The Freedom Struggle — its various stages and important contributors/contributions from different parts of the country**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **Introduction: Write about the background** **of Indian National Congress and the** **‘Moderates’.**

## **Body**

- **Mention the ideology of Moderates** **along with the challenges to mobilise** **the general populace in support of the** **cause.**-**Conclusion: Comment on the situation for** **the wider base for the freedom movement in** **reference to Moderates.** **Answer:**In the second half of the nineteenth century, Indian nationalism developed as a result of a number of factors, including western education, socio religious changes,andBritishpolicies,amongothers.TheIndian**National Congress was established in 1885**and was a key player in the effort to win India’s independence. The**years 1885 through 1905 are known as “The** **Moderate Phase”.**The leaders of this phase are the**‘Moderates’.**

## **Ideology of Moderates**

- Moderates expressed India’s national subjugation and the necessity for its amelioration via **petitioning,** **questioning,** **and** **debating** **techniques.**- Some of the methods employed by moderates to spread their ideas include**prayer, petition,** **persuasion, and charitable giving.**- Their demands were constitutional, as was their agitation.**“Passive Resistance”** was the name given to their tactics.
- Their strategy was to conduct a **boycott.**- The Moderates’ major**goal was to establish self-** **government within the British Empire.**They preferred to achieve their objectives through**legal** **and nonviolent means,** placing a higher value on patience and reconciliation than on violence and conflict.
- They embodied the most progressive forces of the day. They were able to elicit a widespread national awakeningamongallIndianswhosharedcommon interests and saw the need to **unite behind a** **common cause,** as well as a sense of belonging to a single nation.
- They provided political training and popularised modern ideals.
- **Theyhighlightedcolonialrule’sfundamentally** **exploitative nature,** eroding its moral basis.
- The freedom movement’s ideological foundation was provided by moderates.
- For instance, Dada Bhai Naoroji’s economic critiqueofcolonialcontrolinspiredconviction over British rule in India’s exploitative nature.
- The oppressive British government in India frequently attempted to restrict Indians’ civil liberties by enacting harsh legislation.
- The **moderates pushed for the protection of** **rightssuchasthefreedomofpress,association,** and free speech.
- In the years that followed, they solidified the groundwork for a more ferocious, militant, and broadly based national movement. **Challenges to Mobilise the General Populace in** **Support of the Cause:** Although moderates contributed positively to the cause of India’s independence, their ability to mobilise the general populace in support of the cause was

## **limited because of the following reasons**

- The moderate phase’s leaders received **criticism** **for their strategies** (which included petitioning, protesting, and praying) as these weren’t thought to be strong enough to stand up to the British power in India.
- Furthermore,thelackofwidespreadparticipation in the movement has been seen as a fundamental weakness because the organisers were primarily educated, middle-class professionals including lawyers, teachers, journalists, and government employees. During the moderate period of the national movement, the **masses largely took a** **passive role.** - This was due to the early nationalists’ absence of political faith in the general populace; they believed that Indian society was divided and subdivided, and that the populace as a whole was uninformed and held traditional beliefs. Although moderates contributed positively to the cause of India’s independence, their ability to mobilise the general populace in support of the cause was limited which proved to be a major reason that the Indian masses did not have a voice or a face in the liberation struggle until Mahatma Gandhi happened.

## Question 40 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2021-gs1-q40-unacademy**
**Q12. Bring out the constructive programmes of Mahatma Gandhi during Non-Cooperation Movement and Civil Disobedience Movement. (15 Marks, 250 Words) Tags: Modern Indian history from about the middle of the eighteenth century until the present- significant events, personalities, issues.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **Introduction: Write about the conception of** **the idea of constructive programmes.**-**Body: Mention the constructive programmes** **of Mahatma Gandhi.**-**Conclusion: Conclude suitably.** **Answer:**Gandhi’sideaofapeacefulsocietybythewayofnational renewal gave rise to the constructive programme. It arose out of the understanding of the bare realities of our country at the time that he had gained via his extensivetravelsacrossthecountry.Theseprogrammes were not the result of a fleeting thought, but rather of his mind’s careful planning and thought process. Gandhi’s constructive programme was founded on the notion that**foreign dominance in India thrived** **because we were negligent in performing our basic** **national obligations.** **Constructive Programmes of Mahatma Gandhi:**Gandhi introduced his constructive programmes through the Congress in 1920. In order to empower the public via training and discipline in constructive programmes and attain this goal, Gandhi emphasised the necessity of any Satyagrahi working on this programmemoreandmore.Hisgoalwastore-generate**a new civilisation on a non-violent foundation** by the

## **way of following measures**

- **Satyagraha Sabha:** Prior to the Non-Cooperation campaign, the Home Rule Leagues’ members created the Satyagraha Sabha. Strike tactics were connected to prayer and fasting.
- **Promoting indigenous sentiment:**Gandhi presented**Khadi**as a**symbol of nationalism,** economic freedom, equality and self-reliance. He thought that Khadi was the only means that could effectively lead a Satyagraha against foreign authority and rebuild society. Khadi was at the centre of the positive activities that he suggests.
- Through the use of a spinning wheel, khadi bhandars, and a boycott of imported clothing, Khadi and local industries were promoted.
- **Communal unity:**Communal disunity and untouchability were the major hurdles in the way of establishing social unity in our society that created discrimination in the minds of the people. These social evils were age old and deep rooted in Indian social structure and the Indian psyche. In order to maintain and expand their power and politics, the British government, taking advantage of our societal structure, chose the**“divide and** **conquer”** strategy and made an effort to foster and foment racial and ethnic divisions.
- **Gandhi,** through a network of Ashrams spread throughout villages, the promotion of Hindu-Muslim brotherhood was emphasised in language and symbols.
- **Boycotting Evils in society:**He founded**‘Harijan** **Sevak Sangh’ in 1932** after his ‘Yeravada Pact Fast’. Its goal was to completely eradicate untouchability from Hindu society, both in theory and in reality.
- **NeworBasicEducation:Gandhiwasclearthatthe** education of citizens is a backbone of any society. So, he envisaged a new educational system for the non-violentsocietyofhisdreamandexperimented with it for many years.
- **Upliftment of Women:**In his mission of Swaraj, Gandhi needed the cooperation of women, kisans, labourers and students. He therefore intended to interact with them through a positive programme, which was a deliberate strategy to raise their consciousness and win their support for the freedom movement. Without women’s active involvement, in his opinion, the movement could not prosper. People’s spirits were kept up by constructive programming during the non-cooperation movement as a result of their discontentment at the movement’s suspension. The national movement’s reach was widened by constructive effort by**bridging caste and** **classbarriers.Gandhiwasconvincedthatindependent** villages provide a solid foundation for a just, equitable, and non-violent social order. This commitment can serve as a compass for all Indian people, productive workers, and policymakers.

## Question 41 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2020-gs1-q41-unacademy**
**Q3. Evaluate the policies of Lord Curzon and their long-term implications on the national movements. (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Modern Indian history.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, write about the timeline** **and brief about his personal overview and** **circumstances when he took over the reign.**-**In Body,**-**Discuss about various policies that he** **implemented.**-**Elaborate on the effects of those policies** **on Indian people socially, politically,** **and economically.**-**In Conclusion, mention the opinions/** **remarks as to why the policies were far-** **reaching and how it galvanized and unified** **the Indian public.** **Answer:**Lord Curzon was the**youngest Viceroy**of India who took over the reign in 1899 and governed till 1905. He was considered to be racist and a believer of the**‘civilizing mission’** of Indian people by the British. He was an authoritarian, ruthless ruler who wanted to achieve too much in a short span of time. He detested the aspirations of the Indian people to be included in the political spectrum.

## **Policies of Lord Curzon**

-

## **Calcutta Corporation Act, 1899**

- This Act further **reduced** the numbers of the Indian people who were to be elected in the corporation.
- By doing so, the majority shifted towards the European members in the corporation which monopolized the entire corporation.
- Lord Curzon deemed Indians to be unfit for ruling.
-

## **Indian Universities Act, 1904**

- This Act fixed the tenures and the numbers of fellows in the University.
- The provision of this Act introduced the structureofelectiontotheSenateintheIndian Universities.
- The govt kept the veto and the overriding powers to itself in the matters of all the Universities in India, which **increased the** **govt control** over it.
-

## **Ancient Monuments Preservation Act, 1904**

- This Act was passed to preserve the ancient monuments, gain control over traffic in antiquities, and to protect ancient monuments of historical importance.
- Through this Act, the **government gained** **the power** to acquire protected areas, and to regulate archaeological excavation anywhere in India.

## **Partition of Bengal, 1905**

- Lord Curzon divided Bengal into **two parts.** The provinces of western part of Bengal, Odisha, and Bihar were in one region and eastern part of Bengal and Assam were in another region.
- The justification given for this drastic step was that the size of Bengal being the hurdle **for achieving smooth administration** in the region.

## **Implication of Lord Curzon’s Policies**

- The partition of Bengal paved the way for the **rise** **of nationalism,** and it garnered a mass movement in the country.
- Especially the **Swadeshi movement and the Vande** **Mataram** movement was due to the ill-thought policies of Lord Curzon.
- **Communal politics** had started to take deep roots in the Indian society. Pitting Muslims and Hindus against each other to divide the unity.
- The manufactured communal hatred only resulted in the **unity of Hindu-Muslim** brotherhood.
- TheinclusionoftheEuropeansinkeycorporations and overriding powers to the government resulted in **lack of trust among the people** of the British rulers.
- The sympathetic people of Indian origin who were working under the British government started to alienate themselves and formed a **strong diaspora.**As the**policies of Lord Curzon were half baked and** **mostly reactionary,**they in turn, helped the cause of the people of India to unite and fight against the imperialist forces. The long-term effect of his policies was the**subsequent withdrawal** of the British government years later.
