## Question 42 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2020-gs1-q42-unacademy**
**Q13. Since the decade of the 1920s, the national movement acquired various ideological strands and thereby expanded its social base. Discuss. (250 words, 15 marks) Tag: The Freedom Struggle- Its various stages and important contributors/contributions from different parts of the country.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, cite briefly how movements** **were different from post-1920’s – different** **ideological strands.**-**In Body,**-**Elaborate new ideologies and mass** **penetration.**-**Discuss about Marxist ideology, RSS and** **CPI emergence, participation of labor** **class, peasantry, women, and backward** **classes to attest the expanding social** **base.**-**Conclude by comparing the earlier social** **base and change in the ideology of Indian** **National Congress.** **Answer:**The National freedom struggle activity in the 1920’s decade got new elements. The Non-Cooperation Movement gained momentum through 1921-22. But all this was merely the tip of the iceberg. Large parts of the country were on the brink of a formidable revolt. Several different ideological elements which the nationalmovementdevelopedwithanexpandedsocial base from simply disorganized and irregular protests of a small number of people to peasants, workers and women having focus on national liberation objectives nationwide.**VariousIdeologicalstrandswhichcameduring1920’s**

## **decade are as follows**

## **Marxist Ideology**

- The Marxist paradigm movement during the 1920s,placedtheIndiannationalistmovement within a universalistic framework.
- MN Roy was the driving force behind the ideology of the communist movement which attracted many Indian intellectuals.
- TheclassbaseoftheCongressandthenational movement changed over the period. Thus, in the years preceding the First World War, the urban petty bourgeois class became more influential. After the War, the Indian masses like peasantry and the industrial working classes made their presence felt through class struggle.
- The working class participated in the movement with a definite intention to realize social equality.
- **Two important developments of the mid-1920s** **weretheformationoftheRashtriyaSwayamsevak** **Sangh (RSS), a Hindu organization, and the** **Communist Party of India.** - These parties have held very different ideas about the kind of country India should be.
- Revolutionary nationalists such as Bhagat Singh, Chandra Shekhar Azad, Sukhdev and others wanted to fight against colonial rule and the rich exploiting classes through a revolution of workers and peasants.
- For this purpose, they founded the Hindustan Socialist Republican Association (HSRA) in 1928 at Feroz Shah Kotla in Delhi.

## **The Labour Ideology**

- People belonging to this ideology participated in the Non-Cooperation movement through the Cotton Mill workers strike in Bombay during 1919 and 1920.
- ThelegalizationoflabourunionsbytheIndian Trade Union Act in 1926 gave thrust to their activity.
- The Labour strike during the Civil Disobedience Movement and strike of Great IndianPeninsularRailwayin1930wasamajor blow to the British.

## **Caste based movement**

- Another prominent movement was the Dalit movementunderB.R.Ambedkar,whichbegan between the 1920s and 1930s. He campaigned for greater rights for Dalits in British India and continued the same even after independence.
- From the 1920s, a number of organizations united around the issue of caste sprang up in different parts of the country. These included the United Provinces Hindu Backward Classes League, All-India Backward Classes Federation, All India Backward Classes League.

## **Peasant Movements**

- Between1920and1940,peasantorganizations arose.
- The first organization to be founded was the Bihar Provincial Kisan Sabha (1929) and in 1936 the All-India Kisan Sabha.
- ThepeasantsorganizedbytheSabhademanded freedom from economic exploitation for peasants, workers, and all other exploited classes.
- At the time of Independence, the two most classical cases of peasant movements, namely the **Tebhaga movement** (1946-47) and the Telangana movement (1946-51).  The first was a struggle of sharecroppers in Bengal in North Bihar for two thirds share of their produce instead of the customary half. It had the support of the Kisan Sabha and the Communist Party of India (CPI).  The second, directed against the feudal conditions in the princely state of Hyderabad and was led by the CPI.

## **Women Participation**

- LargenumberofwomenparticipatedinIndia’s struggle for independence.
- Women could take part in the movement, and were in fact encouraged to do so, since the methods for struggle were mainly noncooperation and non-violence.
- They were active in participating in the Swadeshi movement, or the boycott of foreign goods, non-payment of taxes, picketing of liquor shops, and so on.
- There was mass participation of women in the non-cooperation movement of 1921 and the civil disobedience movement of 1930.
- A number of women activists also gained prominence were Kamaladevi Ghattopadhyaya, Kalpana Dutt, and Madame Bhikaji Cama. These **different ideological strands**didn’t behave exclusively in their individual capacities,**rather they** **complemented and supplemented**each other one way and another, because of which the social base stood out as togetherness of different masses. The**decade of** **1920’s closed with the Congress resolving to fight for** **Purna Swaraj**(complete independence) in 1929 under the presidency of Jawaharlal Nehru. Consequently,**“Independence Day” was observed on 26 January** **1930** all over the country.

## Question 43 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2019-gs1-q43-unacademy**
**Q3. Examine the linkages between 19th centuries ‘Indian renaissance’ and emergence of national identity. (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Modern History.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to briefly write about the** **Indian Renaissance.**-**In Body, write how was the renaissance of the** **19th century responsible for the emergence** **of national identity.**-**In Conclusion, try to write the overall impact** **of renaissance on nationalism.** **Answer:**The renaissance was not an event, it was a process and its attributes underwent changes whenever major shifts took place in society and the economy. The Indian renaissance is generally marked as the**pre-** **political phase of the anti-colonial struggle,**in which Indians were mainly**engaged in social and cultural** **preparation**for participation in the more progressive and radical, political programme.**Renaissance Responsible for Emergence of National**

## **Identity**

- Introduction of western education in IndianWestern science, philosophy etc., which led to development of rationalism, scientific temper among people. They started questioning the existing norms and practices of society.
- Publication of many newspapers and Magazine promoted rationalism, Humanism (love everyone, no discrimination), Religious universalism etc. which strongly influenced philosophic, social and political outlook of the time. For example: Idea **of** **Vasudhaiv Kutumbkam of Swami Vivekanand,** **Universal theism of Raja Ram Mohan Roy etc.**- The reform movements aimed for modernization rather than westernization. The intellectuals attacked the existing socio-cultural evils and malpractices such as obscurantism, superstitions and irrationality embedded in the Society. The**focus was given on the individual rights of people** **like equality, freedom, education, women’s rights** **etc.** - Through a liberal critique of colonial rule during the 19th century, the Indian intelligentsia was able to overcome this delusion, which led to the emergence of nationalism.
- The Indian intelligentsia was able to understand the true character of British rule, which led to the emergence of nationalism. **The rise of the middle** **class** led to a disjunction between the social and the political movements, which had long-term implications.
- The traditional institutions were regenerated and revolutionised & reconstruction of the social sphere brought a sense **of togetherness**among people which helped in fighting the common enemy. The**political awakening among people** **mobilised communities** to fight against the oppressive policies of the government.
- With the development of the organisations like **Servants of India, Indian Social Conference,** **Bombay Association etc.**spread awareness among people and trained them for political participation and developed sense of nationalism. The Indian renaissance realised the**importance of** **combining political and cultural activities.**This connection led to the**internalisation of nationalism.**Nationalism is conceptualised as a natural outcome of the renaissance. The**social and religious movements,**whichprecededthepoliticalstruggles,wereanecessary**precursor to the coming of nationalism.** ---

## Question 44 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2018-gs1-q44-unacademy**
**Q13. Why was indentured labour taken by the British from India to their colonies? Have they been able to preserve their cultural identity over there? (15 marks, 250 words) Tag: Colonization and Decolonization.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to define an indentured** **labor system.**-**In Body, elaborate reasons for indentured** **labour were taken by the British from India** **to their colonies and their impact especially** **on cultural identity.**-**Concluding with remarks on preservation** **of cultural identity, noted economist and** **playwright, Dr. Satnarine Balkaransingh** **can be quoted.**The indentured labour system was a system of indentured servitude, by which more than one million Indians were transported to labour in European colonies, as a substitute for slave labour, following the abolition of slavery in the British Empire. This system**required Indians to sign a legal agreement stating the** **consent to move abroad for a minimum of five years**.**to work mainly on sugar estates** **Reasons for indentured labour was taken by the**

## **British from India to their colonies**

- After the Slave **Emancipation**Act was passed, which gave all slaves in**the British**empire their freedom, in most colonies,**a universal shortage** **of cheap labour,** particularly for agricultural work was created.
- The colonial government devised a scheme to send, under a system of indentureship, Indians to those colonies which were **experiencing a labour** **shortage.**-**The impact of the Industrial Revolution** was huge in Britain followed by the other European countries which accelerated the flow of trade, labour, and capital across the world.
- The **growing urbanism** in Britain increased the demand for food and agricultural goods.
- Colonies in Africa and Asia became **the** **lucrative destinations** for investing in agricultural and raw commodities.
- Since **immigration of the Chinese indentured**because transport**labour was not very successful**was very expensive and Chinese indentured labour were prone to illness which resulted in a high mortality, and the**Africans would not work** because they thought that Indentureship was just like slavery, therefore Britishers preferred Indian indentured labour over these two.
- The **rural economy of India**frequently fluctuated and the peasants lost their land or fell into debt or disaster. Many Indians agreed to become indentured labourers**to escape the widespread** **poverty and famine in the 19th century.**- On the other side, there was the inbuilt hardships endured by**some disadvantaged** **groups,** and the inevitable occurrence of periodic drought or famine.
- The **caste system** was a very oppressive and inflexible social system. For instance, in Bihar, the poor sold their services and their children’sservices,knownaskamiuti.InSouth India, pariahs (untouchables), pannaiyals and padiyals (lower castes of agricultural labourers) commonly sold themselves and their children into lifelong debts.
- In times of drought, poor harvests and a succession of famines, **there was intensified** **distress.**-**Emigration from India depended more on** **the threat of starvation than on the attraction** **of higher wages in the colonies.**The main destinations of Indian indentured migrants were the Caribbean islands (mainly Trinidad, Guyana, and Surinam), Mauritius and Fiji. Closer home, Tamil migrants went to Ceylon and Malaya.**These laborers** **took their culture with them through their language,** **food, and music.** **Impact of Indian Indentured laborer’s cultural**

## **identity**

- The creation of a **multi-cultural identity** among the descendants who are a product of the cultural integration of the Indians, British and the natives of their host country.
- Once they reached these colonies, **they created** **their unique socio-cultural ecosystems** while they were limited to living in the confines of the large plantations’ owners.
- The new forms of individual and collective selfexpression, blending with different cultural forms, old and new was formed. For instance,
- InTrinidad,theannualMuharramprocession **was remodelled into a riotous carnival**named**‘Hosay’** (for Imam Hussain), in which workers of all religions and races participated.
- The protest religion of **Rastafarianism** (made famous by the Jamaican reggae star Bob Marley) is also supposed to speculate social and cultural links with Indian migrants to the Caribbean.
- **‘Chutney music’,** popular in Trinidad and Guyana, is another creative contemporary expression of the post-indenture experience.
- Most indentured workers stayed on after their contracts expired or returned to their new homes after a short spell in India. Consequently, there are large communities of people of Indian descent in these countries, creating a **substantial Indian** **Diaspora worldwide.**- This system resulted in the development of a large Indian diaspora in many parts of the world, such as the Caribbean, South Africa, Mauritius, to Fiji, and the growth of IndoCaribbean, Indo-African, Indo-Fijian, IndoMalaysian,andIndo-Singaporeanpopulations. These kinds of cultural fusion are part of the composition of the global world, where things from different areas get mixed, lose their original characteristics, and**become something entirely new.**As noted by economist and playwright**Dr Satnarine** **Balkaransingh,** “Whenever we talk about indenture, we talk about loss. But it is also a discourse of a new and changed identity.”
[Question ID: 2018-gs1-q44-unacademy]

## Question 45 (Year: 2017 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2017-gs1-q45-unacademy**
**Q2. Clarify how mid-eighteenth-century India was beset with the spectre of a fragmented polity. (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Indian History.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to briefly write about the** **Mughal administration in the 18th century.**-**In Body,**-**Write briefly about the emergence of** **regional States and rebels during that** **time.**-**In Conclusion, try to write consequences of** **the fragmented polity.** **Answer:**The Mughal Empire had a centralized administration.**After the death of Aurangzeb in 1707, the war** **of succession broke out, and the Mughal empire** **started fragmenting into small independent, semi-** **independent and autonomous states due to lack of** **strong central authority, frequent rebellions by local** **rulers, etc.**

## **Emergence of Regional States**

- The empire was divided into several provinces headed by subedar. The provincial administration had a check and balance system. There were three officers-subedar: Head executive (known as Nizam or Subedar), Diwan (in charge of revenue department) and Bakshi (in charge of military department).
- After the death of Aurangzeb, the weaknesses of the Mughal empire enabled the **local powers** **to assert autonomy. For example, the Successor** **States like Hyderabad, Bengal and Awadh.**- The second group of regional states was the new states or insurgent states set up by rebels against the Mughals, Marathas, Sikhs, Jats,**and Afghans.**

## **Successor States**

- **The successor states** were founded by Mughal provincial governor, who exercised autonomy and never formally severed their links with the Centre. For example, Bengal, Awadh and Hyderabad.
- After the death of Aurangzeb, Farruksiyar appointed **Murshid Quli Khan as the** **governor of Bengal.** After that, Bengal became independent of Mughal control. He gradually became too powerful and ruled like an independent ruler, all primary appointments were made without any reference to the Centre, and revenue payment stopped.
- Burhan-ul-Mulk Sa‘adat Khan was appointed subadar of **Awadh in** 1722, a prosperous region, controlling the rich alluvial Ganga plain and the main trade route between North India and Bengal. He also held the combined offices of Subadari, Diwani and Faujdari.
- Governor of Deccan Chinquilich Khan had founded the **Hyderabad state in1724.**

## **Rebel states**

- Maratha state began as a small kingdom in western India in the seventeenth century, founded by the legendary Maratha chief Shivaji. They captured vast territory in northern and central India.
- **Mysore state** gained prominence as Haidar Ali trained and modernized its troop with French help in 1755.
- **Nadir Shah invaded India in 1739 and** **plundered Delhi several times.** - The Mughal empire’s influence was not as widespread or deep as was believed in the mid-18th century. Mughal Empire gradually fragmented into several independent, regional states due to a lack of strong central power and ruler. The British took advantage of the situation and expanded their rule in India during this time.
[Question ID: 2017-gs1-q45-unacademy]

## Question 46 (Year: 2017 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2017-gs1-q46-unacademy**
**Q3.Whydidthe‘Moderates’failedtocarryconviction with the nation about their proclaimed ideology and political goals by the end of the nineteenth century? (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Modern India.**

## Answer

- **In Introduction, try to write about the** **Moderates briefly.**-**In Body,**-**Write briefly about their ideologies and** **political goals and the reason behind the** **failure of their political goals.**-**In Conclusion, try to write the overall** **contributions of Moderates in the Indian** **freedom struggle.** **Answer:**The Moderates leader**believed in the British justice** **system and was loyal to them.**The Moderate phase of Congress existed**from 1885 to 1905.** They demanded a greater voice for Indians in the government and administration. Dada Bhai Naoroji, W. C. Bannerjee, Gopal Krishna Gokhale etc., were some prominent Moderate leaders.

## **The ideologies and political goals of the moderates**

- **Utilitarian theories**influenced moderates. They believed that**British rule is required for the** **modernisation and betterment of India.**They used**peaceful and constitutional agitation to** **opposed popular means of agitation.**- TheywantedLegislativeCouncilstobemademore**representative,** given more power and introduced in provinces where none existed.
- They used methods like **‘created awareness’**and politicised people by exposing real British intentions through newspapers, public speech, etc., and persuaded Britisher to introduce reforms through petition, prayers,**and protest (3ps).** **Why did Moderates Fail?** - The Moderates did not understand the true nature of the British government.
- **They were alienated from the greater mass**of the Indian population.**Moderates**mostly**belonged** **to propertied classes:** Mainly lawyers, bankers, landowners, medical men, journalist educationist. About 18.99% (1892-1909)-landlords, Lawyers-39.22%, Traders-15.10%, Journalists-3.18%, Doctors-2.94%.
- **Thepeacefulandconstitutionalmethodswerenot** **very effective;** they did not bring any significant change in the policies of the British government.
- They **failed to realise the potential of the masses** for the long and sustained struggle. They could not dispense with landed aristocracy and could not take a logical stand on peasant questions.
- The Moderate leaders **wanted to develop public** **awareness**about the unjust nature of British rule. They had a broad social base and connected with the masses and hence attacked colonialism and demanded similar rights and self-rule for Indian people. Though**Moderates had certain limitations,**they**gained certain political rights and represented** **Indians in legislative councils**and influenced laws and policies of the government. They also**initiated the** **people’s political awakening** and worked as a beacon light for upcoming leaders and successive struggles for independence.

## Question 47 (Year: 2017 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2017-gs1-q47-unacademy**
**Q12.Examine how the decline of traditional artisanal industry in colonial India crippled the rural economy. (250 words, 15 marks) Tag: Modern India.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to briefly write about the** **decline of the traditional artisanal industry** **after the Industrial revolution.**-**In Body,**-**Write how British economic policy** **accelerated the decline of traditional** **artisanal industry.**-**Also, decline of traditional artisanal** **industry in India crippled rural** **economy.**-**In Conclusion, try to mention the overall** **impact of decline of traditional artisanal** **work.** **Answer:** At the beginning of British rule in the mid18th century Indian handicraft industries used to supply about a quarter of all manufactured goods produced in the world and constituted chief export items of European trades. But after the industrial revolution this export demand gradually evaporated.

## **Decline of Traditional Artisanal Industry**

- Earlier, Indian handlooms had a big market in Europe. Indian textiles such as cotton, linen, silk, jute goods etc. already had markets in Asia and Africa. With the coming of industrialisation in England,thetextileindustrytheremadeimportant headway.
- There was **a massive import of machine-made** **clothes**from English factories to Indian markets. This import of a large number of products manufactured by mechanical looms in England led to increased threat forthehandicraftindustries as the British goods were sold at a much**cheaper** **price.**- There**was a one-way free trade policy** between IndiaandEngland.TheBritishsucceededinselling their goods at a cheap price as foreign goods were given free entry in India without paying any duty.
- On the other hand, Indian handicrafts were **taxed** **heavily** when they were sent out of the country.
- Besides, under the pressure of its industrialists, British government often **imposed a protective** **tariff on Indian textiles.** - Therefore, within a few years, India from being an exporter of clothes became an exporter of raw cotton and an importer of British clothes.
- The **decline of royal courts,** reduced the local demand of traditional industries. They also lost patronage from kings.
- The influence of **western education** created a new class of people, who have a taste for European goods. It reduced the demand of traditional industries.
- The loss of guilds led to decline in the quality of products, workmanship etc. which affected the demand of products of traditional industries. **Decline of Traditional Artisanal Industry in India**

## **Crippled Rural Economy**

- It also generated **unemployment**for a large community of weavers. Most of them**migrated** **to rural areas to sustain their life by working on** **their lands as agricultural labourers.** This, in turn, put extended pressure on the rural economy and livelihood.
- **The Indian nationalist leaders later called this** **unevencompetitionfacedbytheIndianhandloom** **industry de-industrialization. The foremost** **intention of the British was to transform India** **into a consumer of British goods. It destroyed the** **self-sufficient village economy.**- As a result, textile, metalwork, glass and paper industries were soon out of work. Indian goods could not contend with the British factory-made products made by machines. It**intensified poverty** in India drastically.
- They extricated money from the Indian rulers, merchants, landlords, and even the common people. Added to this drain was the profit made through trade and the British officials’ salaries. It is clear that their economic policies were intended to serve the interests of the East India Company and, later, the British Empire. By 1813, the Indian handicrafts lost domestic and foreign markets and were captured and monopolized by Britain through war and colonization. From an exporter, India became an importer of goods. British economic policy led to de-industrialization and deurbanization, increasing pressure on land, increased poverty&unemployment,andcrippledruraleconomy.
[Question ID: 2017-gs1-q47-unacademy]

## Question 48 (Year: 2017 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to briefly write about** **the origin of the national movement and** **subsequent development.**-**In Body,**-**Write various new objectives to the** **vision of Indian Independence in 1920s.**-**In Conclusion, try to mention overall change** **in the vision.** **Answer:** Since ‘twenties of the last century new objectives, ideas imparted a new orientation to the Indian freedom struggle. The approach of non-violence, civil disobedience, mass participation etc. for the Indian Independence.

## **NewObjectivestotheVisionofIndianIndependence**

- Gandhiji came back to India in 1915. He provided a new orientation and perspective to Indian freedomstrugglebasedontruthandnon-violence and guided the masses and the freedom fighters with his idea of **Satyagraha.**- Motilal Nehru report recommended Nineteen Fundamental Rights to uplift the vulnerable section, including the**right to vote**for both men and women above 21 years of age unless disqualified equal rights for men and women as citizens and no state religion. These ideas later became the fulcrum in**Indian Constitution** making.
- From the 1920s onwards **the ideas of Marxism** **and socialism** spread rapidly in India. Communist party was established in India in 1925. A section of Indian National Congress was also deeply moved by the idea of socialism. JL Nehru, Subhash Chandra Bose preached the idea of socialism.
- The **peasant’s movements** like Eka movement, Kisan sabha movement etc. were integrated with the Congress and national movements.
- The left- wing movement did not remain confined to Indian freedom struggle only but raised **their** **voicesagainsttheclassoppressionbythecapitalist** class. The movement strengthened the voices of marginal classes and connected them with the mainstream movement. In this regard, Karachi

## **Resolution is noteworthy because**

- Itreiteratedthegoalof“PoornaSwaraj”:Thus, the goal of complete independence was made a definite goal. Until then, Swaraj was not defined and was of abstract nature.
- “Poorna Swaraj” did not mean only political independence: The concept included both political and economic freedom.
- Accordingly, the resolution called for equality irrespective of sex, language, caste and creed, universal suffrage, secularism, freedom of speech and expression, freedom to form associations etc.
- Theresolutionalsocalledforlandreforms,free primary education, healthy work conditions for workers, minimum wages, etc.
- Protection of language, culture and script of minorities including linguistic minorities.
- The **women’s participation** in Indian freedom struggle during this phase was also remarkable. Sorojini Naidu, Pritilata Waddedar, Vijay Laxmi Pandit were some of the prominent women freedom fighters.
- Besides **Indian business class, the All Indian** **Trade Union Congress** contributed to the struggle for independence to a large extent. Business class cooperated by giving financial assistance.
- **Dr. B R Ambedkar and Mahatma Gandhi**organised and politicised the depressed class, through various organisations. Thus, the**new objectives and ideas broadened the** **vision of Indian independence** scale and scope after the twenties of last century. It strengthened the National movements and increased the participation of masses and pressured the government.

## Question 49 (Year: 2016 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2016-gs1-q49-unacademy**
**Q3. Explain how the uprising of 1857 constitutes an important watershed in the evolution of British policies towards colonial India. (12.5 marks, 200 words)**

#

## Answer

**Tag:** Modern Indian History - Significant events.

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to begin by describing** **the 1857 revolt.**-**In Body, mention the evolution of British** **policies towards colonial India after the** **1857 Revolt, such as crown rule, military** **setup, etc.**-**Conclude by showing how the 1857 revolt** **paved the way for national movement or** **freedom struggle.** **Answer:** **The uprising of 1857**was a watershed moment in the history of India. The British East India Company suppressed the revolt with great force, thus**paving** **the way for India to become a British colony.**But nevertheless, the uprising was a big blow for the imperial intentions of the British and resulted in many decisions that changed the fate of India.**Evolution of British policies towards colonial** **India after the 1857 Revolt occurred through many** **instances discussed below.**

## **Transfer of power from Company to the Crown**

- The British Parliament enacted the Government of India Act of 1858 to transfer the powers of the East India Company to the British Crown to ensure more responsible management of Indian affairs.
- A **member of the British cabinet**was appointed**Secretary of State for India** and made responsible for all matters related to the governance of India.
- He was given a **council to advise him, called** **the India Council.**- The**Governor-General of India was given the title** **of Viceroy,** that is, a personal representative of the Crown.

## **Changes in Military setup**

- It was decided that the proportion of Indians in the British Indian army would be reduced, and the number of European soldiers would be increased.
- The ratio of British to Indian troops was fixed at roughly 1:2 instead of 1:5, one British and two Indian battalions were formed into brigades so that no sizable station should be without British troops.
- The effective Indian artillery, except for a few mountain batteries, was abolished, while the Brahmans and Rajputs of Avadh were reduced in favor of other groups.
- Instead of recruiting soldiers from Awadh, Bihar, central India and south India, they decided to recruit more soldiers from among the Gurkhas, Sikhs and Pathans

## **Leniency towards Princely states and zamindars**

- British fears of another mutiny and consequent determination to bolster Indian states as **“natural** **breakwaters”** against any future tidal wave of revolt thus left autocratic princely rule to survive.
- All ruling chiefs of the country were assured that their territory would never be annexed in future.
- They could **pass on their kingdoms** to their heirs, including adopted sons.
- However, they were **made to acknowledge the** **British Queen as their Sovereign Paramount.** Thus, the Indian rulers were to hold their kingdoms as subordinates of the British Crown.
- Policies were made **to protect landlords and** **zamindars** and provide them security of rights over their land.

## **Divide and Rule**

- The land and property of Muslims was confiscated on a large scale and they were treated with suspicion and hostility. The British believed that theywereresponsiblefortherebellioninabigway.

## **No interference in indigenous social belief**

- The British decided to respect the customary religious and social practices of the people in India. They decided nonintervention in matters of religious belief or worship within British India.

## **Economic policy and development**

- The total cost of the mutiny of 1857–59, which was equivalent to a normal year’s revenue, was **charged** **to India and paid off from increased revenue** **resources** in four years.
- An **individual income tax** was introduced for five years to pay off the war deficit, but urban personal income was not added as a regular source of Indian revenue until 1886.
- Britain’s **major contribution to India’s** **economic development**throughout the era of crown rule was the railroad network but the spread of railroads**also accelerated the** **destruction of India’s indigenous handicraft** **industries.** Within the constraints of British Rule, a modern India was emerging by the end of the 19th century. British rule of course had an important role in this process, their oppressed regime gave India a sense of nationalism and united everyone to drive them out of the Indian subcontinent.
[Question ID: 2016-gs1-q49-unacademy]

## Question 50 (Year: 2016 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2016-gs1-q50-unacademy**
**Q4. Discuss the role of women in the freedom struggle especially during the Gandhian phase. (12.5 marks, 200 words) Tag: The Freedom struggle.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to write the importance** **of women’s participation in the National** **Movement.**-**In Body, emphasis the role of women in the** **various freedom struggles especially during** **the Gandhian phase.**-**Conclude with the how the role of women in** **the national movement was an important** **part of it.** **Answer:** **Women participated in the Indian anti-imperialist** **struggle in large numbers.**There were several women leaders who emerged as a leader during national movementslikeSarojiniNaidu,VijayalakshmiPandit,**Annie Mascarene, A.V. Kuttimaluamma, Durgabai** **Deshmukh,**and many more. While these women facilitated the growth of the women’s movement in India and their active involvement in public life, it was the new direction and focus given to the Indian National Movement by Mahatma Gandhi that enabled Indian women to come out of their homes.**The role of women in the freedom struggle especially**

## **during the Gandhian phase**

- It was during the Gandhian movement that the single woman acquired a sense of dignity and came to be respected for her political work.
- Women’s participation in the first major Gandhian phase, **the Khilafat and Non-cooperation** **Movement in 1920-22,** was limited but there were some important developments.
- The Khilafat Movement in the U.P. was marked by the energetic efforts of Bi Amman. A courageous woman who did not allow patriarchy and the pardah to come in her way.
- Another important development was that **women went to jail for the first time in the Non-** **Cooperation Movement.** - During this phase, Gandhi actively drew women into the mainstream of politics, asking them to attend the Ahmedabad session of the Indian National Congress, held at the end of 1921, in large numbers.
- Most of the leaders had been arrested during the Non-Cooperation Movement.  The women took upon themselves the responsibility of keeping the movement alive by holding meetings, often in defiance of prohibitory orders, addressing large crowds, usually for the first time in their lives, and if need be, even going to jail.
- **The Civil Disobedience Movement of 1930-33** was undoubtedly a major landmark in women’s participation in political activity.
- The **Civil Disobedience Movement of 1930**showed that “**The veil of centuries was torn** **asunder.** Women, young and old, rich and poor came tumbling out in their hundreds and thousands, shaking off the traditional shackles that had held them so long”.
- **The 1942 Quit India Movement** was one in which women’s participation was even more significant.
- Women took the initiative in a wide variety of waysfromproducingpamphlets,tocirculating undergroundliterature,torunningaCongress radio.
- Usha Mehta, along with Vithalbhai Jhaveri, Babubhai Khakar and Rammanohar Lohia **set** **up Freedom Radio in Bombay.**The role of these courageous women in the national movement was immense and they**were not mere add-** **onstotheGandhianmovementbutwereanimportant** **part of it.**The story of women’s participation in India’s freedom struggle is the**story of making bold choices,** **finding themselves on the streets, inside jails and in** **legislatures,**all of which empowered them in a whole varietyofways.Thenon-violentmovementthatgained Indiaherfreedomnotonlytookwomenalongbutwas**dependent for its success on the active participation** **of women.** ---

## Question 51 (Year: 2016 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2016-gs1-q51-unacademy**
**Q5. Highlight the differences in the approach of Subhash Chandra Bose and Mahatma Gandhi in thestruggleforfreedom. (12.5marks,200words) Tag: The Freedom struggle.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to write briefly about** **Mahatma Gandhi and Subhash Chandra** **Bose.**-**In Body, mention the differences in the** **approach of Subhash Chandra Bose and** **Mahatma Gandhi in the struggle for** **freedom.**-**Conclude with the fact that, despite all the** **differences in ideologies, both these great** **men admired and respected each other.** **Answer:**Mahatma Gandhi and Subhas Chandra Bose were two legendary**personalities, gigantic in their political** **moral and ethical stature.**In 1915, soon after his return from South Africa, Gandhi became the unquestioned leader of India’s freedom movement and Indian National Congress. Subhas Chandra Bose, younger to Gandhi by 28 years who had resigned from the coveted Indian Civil Service with the resolute aim and determination to devote himself entirely to the fight for India’s freedom.**The differences in the approach of Subhash Chandra** **Bose and Mahatma Gandhi in the struggle for**

## **freedom**

- Although **Subhas Chandra was a follower of** **Gandhi** during the initial days, the later part of the 1930s witnessed a growing radicalization of his thoughts and Bose became increasingly frustrated with the lack of momentum in the independence movement.
- As Bose started to assert his bold stance in various party forums, it led to a polarization in the Congress party ranks.
- **Subhash Chandra Bose** differed from Mahatma Gandhi’s approach of non-violent movement to oust the British.
- He believed that non-violence could be an ideology but not a creed.
- The national movement should be free from violence but, if need be, people could resort to arms.
- In protest against the decision of Gandhi in calling off the Non-cooperation Movement as a sequence to the Chauri Chaura incident in 1922, Bose felt highly dejected.
- Bose found himself frequently at loggerheads with Gandhi and their differences often came out in the public.
- All these bickering reached a climax when Subhas Chandra Bose became Congress President for a second term in 1939 defeating Gandhi-nominated candidate Pattabhi Sitaramayya.
- Unable to hide his displeasure, Mahatma commented “Subhas’ victory is my defeat.”
- This unhealthy environment within the party made Bose’s tasks more difficult and soon he resigned from his post.
- Subhas Chandra Bose and Gandhi **also disagreed** **over their visions** for the post-Independence Indian state.
- Bose was influenced by the success of the five-year plans in the Soviet Union, and he advocated for a socialist nation with an industrialized economy.
- Gandhi was opposed to the very concept of industrialization. Despite all the differences in ideologies, both these great men admired and respected each other. In 1942 Gandhi called Subhash Bose the **“Prince among** **the Patriots”**for his great love for the country. Bose too admired Gandhi and in a radio broadcast from Rangoon in 1944, he called Mahatma Gandhi**“The** **Father of Our Nation.”** ---
[Question ID: 2016-gs1-q51-unacademy]

## Question 52 (Year: 2015 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2015-gs1-q52-unacademy**
**Q3.Howdifficultwouldhavebeentheachievementof Indian independence without Mahatma Gandhi? Discuss. (12.5 marks, 200 words) Tag: The Freedom Struggle.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In introduction, start the answer with some** **suitable introduction as per the question.**-**In Body, describe the role of M K Gandhi in** **the freedom struggle.**-**Conclude the answer with mentioning the** **importance of Gandhi.** **Answer:** One of the greatest men in the history of India is unarguably Mahatma Gandhi. The way he gave shape and character to India’s freedom struggle was extraordinary. Gandhiji arrived in India in 1915 from South Africa. Before the arrival of Gandhi, the fight against colonial British was sporadic and there was no leader at the national level to unite the struggle against the British. The concept of Indian nationalism had not taken a concrete shape before the emergence of Gandhi as a national leader. After arrival, he spent a year visiting various places in India to understand the situation. His political engagement started in the 1917-18 period when he took up the issues of Champaran indigo farmers,theAhmedabadtextileworkersandtheKheda peasants. The struggles demonstrated to the Indian people, Gandhi’s method of political struggle and his principles of non-violence and non-cooperation. One of the first series of non-violent protests nationwide was the non-cooperation movement started by Mahatma Gandhi. This movement officially started the Gandhian era in India. In this freedom struggle, the non-cooperation movement was basically aimed at making the Indians aware of the fact that the British governmentcanbeopposed,andifdoneactively,itwill keep a check on them. Though the movement failed, Indians awakened to the concept of going against the British. Gandhiji made mass mobilization at such a wide scale, throughout the events that occurred in India in 1920s and 1930s. After the non- cooperation movement, the freedom struggle got even more intense and passionate. Entire India was united in the movement for freedom. Everyone contributed what they could, in the freedom struggle. The cry of Purna Swaraj for complete independence was raised. After much sacrifice and efforts, India gained its independence on the 15th August, 1947. Gandhiji made social reports a part of the programme of the nationalist movement. His greatest achievement in the field of social reform was the campaign against the inhuman institution of untouchability which had degraded millions of Indians. His other achievement was in the field of cottage industries. He saw in the charkha,thespinningwheel,thesalvationofthevillage people and its promotion became part of the congress programme. In addition to infusing people with the spirit of nationalism it provided employment to millions and created a large group of people who were ready to throw themselves into the struggle and court imprisonment. The charkha became so important that it eventually became a part of the flag of the Indian National Congress. Gandhiji devoted himself to the cause of HinduMuslim unity. He regarded communalism as antinational and inhuman. Under his leadership the unity of the nationalist movement was secured and the people worked hard for independence. Thus, Mahatma Gandhi played a pivotal role in the freedom struggle of India. Without Gandhiji, it would have taken a longer time for attaining independence.
[Question ID: 2015-gs1-q52-unacademy]

## Question 53 (Year: 2015 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2015-gs1-q53-unacademy**
**Q4. Mahatma Gandhi and Dr. B R Ambedkar, despite having divergent approaches and strategies, had a common goal of amelioration of the downtrodden. Elucidate. (12.5 marks, 200 words) Tag: The Freedom Struggle.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to give a brief idea** **about Gandhi and Ambedkar’s divergent** **approaches.**-**InBody,discussdissimilaritiesinapproaches** **and similarities in ambition.**-**Conclude your answer balancing both** **dissimilarities and similarities.** **Answer:** Gandhiji and Ambedkar are among the major makers of modern India. Despite having dissimilarities, the common ground between the two great personalities has been their goal of upliftment of the downtrodden.

## **Dissimilarities in approaches**

- Gandhi had a profound faith in the Varna system, though it further stretched into the caste system. He believed that a person born into a particular caste had a divine dispensation. However, Ambedkar was wholly against this system. He aspired to abolish casteism to improve the status of the oppressed people.
- Ambedkar’s reading of caste, pivoted on seeing the Dalit question as a political issue, and not only a social one, as Gandhi did. In his writing and speeches, Ambedkar insisted that a political democracy was meaningless if the so-called oppressed classes were not equal participants in it.
- Gandhi’s reading of caste fundamentally differed from Ambedkar. While Ambedkar preferred a rights-based approach, Gandhi’s approach was through faith and spirituality.
- UnlikeAmbedkar,Gandhifeltthatanyexploitative relationship could be rectified only when the exploiter hada change of heart. So, heworked with upper castes to change their mindset. Ramsay MacDonald announced a separate electorate for the downtrodden which was demanded by Ambedkar to protect their interests. Gandhi was against this secret electorate as he thought the mechanism would further increase the division in society. Therefore, he proposed for a greater number of reserved seats in the Legislature to protect the rights of these people. As a result, the Poona Pact was signed in 1932.

## **Similarities in approaches**

- Both Gandhiji and Ambedkar worked for the upliftment of oppressed classes throughout their lives.
- They maintained the momentum in their struggles for justice and equality until the very end of their lives. Though Gandhi and B.R. Ambedkar had different approaches, but both the leaders wanted to change the condition of downtrodden. Their contribution to social justice is unparalleled.
[Question ID: 2015-gs1-q53-unacademy]
---

---

## Question 54 (Year: 2015 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2015-gs1-q54-unacademy**
**Q5. It would have been difficult for the Constituent Assembly to complete its historic task of drafting the Constitution for Independent India in just three years, but its experience gained with the Government of India Act, 1935. Discuss. (12.5 marks, 200 words) Tag: The Freedom Struggle.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, start with a meeting of the** **Constituent Assembly.**-**In Body, discuss features of Indian** **Constitution taken from GOI 1935 & other** **sources.**-**Conclude the answer with a suitable** **conclusion balancing GOI 1935 & other** **sources.** **Answer:** The Constituent Assembly of India assembled for the first time on December 9, 1946. It took nearly three years (2 years, 11 months, and 17 days to be precise) to complete its historic task of drafting the Constitution for Independent India. It would have been difficult to draft the Constitution in just three years without a comprehensive legal document like the GOI 1935 Act. The Constitution of India was the residue of the legacy started by the Government of India Act, 1935. Some features of the Government of India Act that suited well for free India were taken into consideration by the drafters of the Indian Constitution.
- Features of Federal Legislature and Provincial autonomy was taken from the Government of India Act, 1935. The 1935 Act divided powers between the Centre and the Province which was better for the administration at ground level as well.
- Anotherfeaturethathadbeentakenwasprovincial autonomy. This autonomy worked better for regulating the states or provinces without the interference of externalauthorities.Also, the states or the provinces knew their people’s condition better than any other external authority.
- A Federal Court that was established in 1937 through the Government of India Act, 1935 was considered as the Supreme Court by independent India.
- The Constitution of India also borrowed the feature that every state should have a Governor who would be elected by the Central Government.
- Public Service Commission that we see in Article 315 of the Indian Constitution had also been taken from the Government of India Act, 1935 **HoweverapartfromGOIact1935IndianConstitution** **has been amalgamation of experiences from freedom** **struggleandothersourcesaswell.Manynewadditions**were made in the Indian constitution from the myriad experiences from other countries such as Britain, the U.S.A, Irish, Canadian, etc. Undoubtedly GOI act 1935 laid down a strong foundation in the making of Indian Constitution. But the Govt**of India act was created to manage** the affairs of a colony upon vested commercial interests of The Great Britain. The basic ideas of the Constitution were also laid down throughout the course of the freedom struggle by freedom fighters.
[Question ID: 2015-gs1-q54-unacademy]

## Question 55 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2014-gs1-q55-unacademy**
**Q4. The third battle of Panipat was fought in 1761. Why were so many empire-shaking battles fought at Panipat? (10 marks, 150 words) Tag: Indian Culture.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to start your answer** **with the location of Panipat.**-**In Body, write the reasons why Panipat was** **preferred for the battle.**-**Conclude the overall significance of Panipat** **as a battle ground.** **Answer:** Panipat is located in present-day Haryana, on the bank of the river Yamuna. The region is fertile plain land located between the Ganges and Indus River. Panipat has witnessed three empire shaking battles, the first battle of Panipat in 1526, the second battle of Panipat in 1556 and third battle of Panipat in 1761. Empire shaking battles which took place at Panipat

## **were**

- First battle of Panipat led to the establishment of Mughal dynasty
- Second battle of Panipat laid the real foundation of Mughal empire
- Third battle of Panipat ruined the Maratha ambition to rule India. Panipat had immense strategic importance in the medieval era. India was invaded multiple times from Northwest through Khyber Pass. Both the parties, the rulers of Delhi and the invaders preferred Panipat as strategic ground to confront.

## **The reasons why Panipat was preferred are**

- It was close to Delhi. The location was preferable fortherulersofDelhiastheycouldeasilytransport their weapons, military, food etc. and the rulers of Delhi could keep their capital protected.
- Panipatwasalargeplainlandwhichwassuitableas a battle ground, easy for the movement of cavalry.
- Panipat fell on the Grand Trunk Road which was built by Sher Shah Suri. So, the invaders from northwest easily used to reach the large plain land, Panipat. Thus, for its strategic importance Panipat has always been the preferable zone as a battlefield.

## Question 56 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2014-gs1-q56-unacademy**
**Q6. Examine critically the various facets of economic policies of the British in India from the mid- eighteenth century till independence. (10 marks, 150 words) Tag: Modern Indian History.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to mention about the** **Advent of British.**-**In Body, discuss significant changes of** **economic policies of the British in India.**-**Conclude your answer dragging the question** **outline and stating the economic impact.** **Answer:** British East India company came to India as a trading company. Gradually they conquered India. British economic policy in India underwent significant changes in different phases of British rule in India such

## **as**

- Before the Industrial Revolution British followed the policy of mercantilism. In the 17th and first half of 18th century the East India Company’s role in India was that of a trading corporation which brought goods or precious metals into India and exchanged them for Indian goods like textiles and spices, which it sold abroad. Its profit came primarily from the sale of Indian goods abroad. Naturally, it tried constantly to open new markets for Indian goods in Britain and other countries. Thereby, it increased the export of Indian goods and thus encouraged their production.
- After 1757, the pattern of the Company’s commercial or economic relation with India underwent a qualitative change. After defeating the Nawab of Bengal in the Battle of Plassey in 1757 and after the Battle of Buxar the Company could now use its political control over Bengal to acquiremonopolisticcontroloverIndiantradeand productionandtopushitsIndiantrade.Moreover, it began to utilize the revenues of Bengal to finance its export of Indian goods.
- The Indian merchants were gradually squeezed out, while the weavers and other craftsmen were compelled either to sell their products at uneconomic rates or to hire themselves out to the Company at low wages.
- At the beginning of British rule in the mid18th century Indian handicraft industries used to supply about a quarter of all manufactured goods produced in the world and constituted chief export items of European trades. The scenario changed after the industrial revolution. After the industrial revolution this export demand gradually evaporated. British started importing raw materials from India and opened the Indian market for the British manufactured goods led to deindustrialization. It Reduced the number of people dependent on secondary industries. Deindustrialization also led to deurbanization which resulted in increasing pressure on land and pauperization.
- After 1850, a very large amount of British capital was invested in railways, loans to the Indian Government, and to a smaller extent in tea plantations, coal mining, jute mills, shipping industry, trade and banking. India now serves as an area for investment of foreign capital. Thus, British economic policy was always against India’s interest. It seriously affected Indian economy to a large extent.
[Question ID: 2014-gs1-q56-unacademy]

## Question 57 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2014-gs1-q57-unacademy**
**Q7. What ways did the naval mutiny prove to be the lastnailinthecoffinofBritishcolonialaspirations in India? (10 marks, 150 words) Tag: The Freedom Struggle.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, start your answer with a** **brief introduction on naval mutiny.**-**In Body,**-**Discuss the reasons behind naval** **mutiny.**-**Mention the impact of naval mutiny.**-**Conclude the answer with the statement that** **it was the last nail in the coffin of British** **colonial aspirations in India.** **Answer:** The Royal Indian Navy mutiny was arguably the single most important event in convincing the British government that it could no longer hold on to India. The Royal naval mutiny started on 18 February when 1100 naval ratings of HMIS Talwar went on strike protesting the treatment meted out to them. This included racial humiliation and unpalatable food. The protesting sailors demanded the release of all political prisoners including those from Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose’s Indian National Army (INA), action against the commander for ill-treatment and using insulting language, revision of pay and allowances to put RIN employees on a par with their counterparts in the Royal Navy, demobilization of RIN personnel with provisions for peacetime employment, release of Indian forces stationed in Indonesia, and better treatment of subordinates by their officers. The RIN strike came at a time when the Indian nationalist sentiment had reached fever pitch across the country. The winter of 1945-46 saw three violent upsurges: in Calcutta in November 1945 over the INA trials; in February 1946, also in Calcutta, over the sentencing of INA officer Rashid Ali; and, in that same month,theratings’uprisinginBombay.Soon,ordinary people joined the ratings, and life came to a virtual standstill in both Bombay and Calcutta. There were meetings, processions, strikes, and hartals. In Bombay, labourers participated in a general strike called by the Communist Party of India and the Bombay Students’ Union. In many cities across India, students boycott classes in solidarity. It was an event that strengthened further the determination among all sections of the Indian people to see the end of British rule. Though the revolt was short-lived, British realized it would be difficult for them to continue their rule in India. The army which was one of the major sources of power of British rule had turned against them. Thus, royal naval mutiny became the last nail in the coffin of British rule in India.
[Question ID: 2014-gs1-q57-unacademy]
---

---

## Question 59 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2013-gs1-q59-unacademy**
**Q3. Defying the barriers of age, gender and religion, the Indian women became the torch-bearer during the struggle for freedom in India. Discuss. (10 marks, 200 words) Tags: The freedom struggle of India.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- In Introduction, try to give a brief introduction regarding women participation in the freedom struggle.
- In Body, discuss the role of women in Indian freedom struggle in detail.
- In Conclusion, try to project the positive aspect of women’s involvement and its impact in current times. **Answer:**Conditions during the**pre-colonial period in India**confined the women in India to the**domestic space** **only.**The freedom struggle was an**opportunity for** **women**from different backgrounds, defying age, gender, and religion to unite and lead the fight against the British rule. The**educational reforms**during these times garnered an awareness among masses which strived**to achieve equality and empowerment of** **women.**Hence, women played a very important role during the struggle for freedom in India.**Roles of Women in the Indian Freedom Struggle**

## **Defying Barriers of Age, Gender and Religion**

- **It all started with**Rani of Jhansi,**Lakshmi Bai,**participating in the great Revolt of 1857**breaking** **many stereotypes. Hazrat Mahal Begum,**wife of the deposed ruler of Lucknow, also actively took part in the Revolt of 1857 against the**Doctrine** **of Lapse under which Dalhousie** wanted her to surrender Lucknow.
- **The Quit India Movement presented an excellent** **opportunity**for the women in India to**overcome** **the gender barrier**and lead the struggle,**since** **mostofthemaleleaderswereinjail. Kasturba,the**wife of Mahatma Gandhi, was one of the foremost supporters of Gandhi’s programmes. One of the**first women to be imprisoned in Transvaal,** she took part in the Quit India Movement (1942) and was arrested.
- **Aruna Asaf Ali**played a leading role during the Quit Indian Movement. She unfurled the National Flag at the Gowalia Tank maidan in Bombay to signify the commencement of the Quit India Movement**and became a legend for thousands of** **youths that rose to emulate her.**-**Sarojini Naidu**holds pride of place among women freedom fighters of India. She was responsible for awakening the women of India. Sarojini Naidu led the Civil Disobedience Movement. In 1942, she was arrested during the ‘Quit India’ protest and stayed in jail for 21 months.**Sucheta Kriplani, an** **ardent nationalist,** also actively participated in the Quit India Movement.
- **Rani Gaidinliu**was a prominent Naga nationalist woman leader from Manipur. She participated in the Civil Disobedience Movement to oust the foreigners from Manipur. She was jailed at the age of 16, which goes on to show that**women’s** **participation had defied the barriers of age.**- Another revolutionary**Matangini Hazra at the** **age of 73**participated in the Quit India movement**defying the barriers of age.**-**Annie Besant, Irish by birth,**was a staunch supporter of India’s struggle for freedom. She founded the Home Rule League in Madras in**1916.KamalaNehru,marriedtoJawaharlalNehru** in 1916, participated in various movements, led the Civil Disobedience Movement. She played a prominent part in organizing the No Tax Campaign in the United Provinces.
- **Vijay Laxmi Pandit** entered the Non-cooperation Movement. She was imprisoned thrice in connectionwiththeCivilDisobedienceMovement in 1932, 1941 and 1942.
- **Pritilata Waddedar, Kalpana Dutta** were also prominentwomenrevolutionaryleaderswhowere influenced by the revolutionary idea of Surya Sen.
- **Defying the religion barrier,** many women leader from royal Muslim families took part in the freedom struggle. Begum Safia Wajid, Rehana Tyabjee and Rajkumari Amrita Kaur and many others are among the few of the names that come to mind who have come out of their royal lineage and led the mass.
- **SmtKamaladeviChattopadhyay,SmtKamaladevi** **Chattopadhyay, Meera Behn and Sarla Behn**also contributed to Indian freedom struggle. This is a testimonythatwomenfromdifferentbackgrounds (rural/ urban) came out and actively took part in the freedom struggle. The role of women in the entire freedom struggle, starting from 1857 till the independence of India in 1947, always occupies a special place. In the different phases of Indian freedom struggle women came forward and took the charge of the struggle**not only** **as the foot soldiers but also as leaders**being the**torch-bearers defying the barriers of age, gender, and** **religion.** ---

## Question 61 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2013-gs1-q61-unacademy**
**Q5. “In many ways, Lord Dalhousie was the founder of modern India.” Elaborate. (10 marks, 200 words) Tag: Modern Indian History.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to start your answer** **with a brief introduction on Lord Dalhousie.**-**In Body, discuss Lord Dalhousie’s major** **reforms in detail.**-**InConclusion,showthatthereformsbrought** **by him led to the modernisation of India.** **Answer:**Lord Dalhousie became the**Governor General of** **India in 1848 and continued till 1856.**The most important aspect of Dalhousie was his**policy of** **annexation.**He adopted the policy of**Doctrine of** **Lapse.**His annexations include the Punjab, Lower Burma, most of the Central Provinces and Oudh. Dalhousie’s territorial**acquisition transformed the** **map of India.**He introduced a number of**reforms**in the educational,**administrative, military, transport** **and communication, public work, and social sector** in the country.

## **Administrative Reforms**

- Heintroducedacentralisedadministrativesetup. Many **Commissions were formed** to consolidate the British rule.
- During his tenure, he **delegated the powers**pertaining to**Justice, Revenue, and Law** **Enforcement**to the**District Magistrates,**which is**still the case in modern India** through the All India Services.

## **Educational Reforms**

- Dalhousie had evinced in the development of **education.TheeducationaldespatchofSirCharles** **Wood (1854)**was considered as the**“Intellectual** **Charter of India”.**It presented an outline for the comprehensive scheme of education at**primary,** **secondary and collegiate levels.**- Departments of Public Instructions were established.**The Universities of Calcutta, Bombay** **and Madras were founded in 1857,**which are still very**relevant in modern India.**

## **Military Reforms**

- He shifted the **artillery Headquarters from** **Calcutta to Meerut. Shimla**was made the new**army HQ.**-**TheGurkhaRegiment,whichisoneofthemodern** forces in the country, was his creation.

## **Transportation and Communicational Reforms**

- **TheintroductionofrailwaysinIndiabyDalhousie**inaugurated a new economic era. He envisaged a network of railways**connecting the main places** **with the ports**and providing both for strategic needs and commercial development. Besides facilitating trade and commerce,**minimizing** **distances the railways have gone a long way in** **uniting India.** - The use of telegraphs brought significant changes in the communication system. Main cities of the country viz., Calcutta, Peshawar, Bombay and Madras were telegraphically connected. About 4000 miles long telegraph lines were laid before the departure of Dalhousie.
- Lord Dalhousie laid down the foundation of the modern postal system. A new Post Office Act was passed in 1854, and Postage stamps were introduced for the first time

## **Public Work and Social Sector Reforms**

- Dalhousie created a separate Public Works Department and allocated more funds for cutting canals and roads. The Upper Ganges Canal was completedin1854.Manybridgeswereconstructed.
- By modernizing the Public Works Department, he laid the foundations of the engineering service in India.
- Social evil practices like female infanticides, etc., were abolished by him. The Religion Disability Act and Hindu Widow Remarriage Act were passed during his tenure. Though the reforms were carried out to consolidate BritishEmpireinIndia,thereformsintroducedbyhim are **still relevant in various sectors in modern India.**Hence, he is**hailed as “the founder of modern India”.** ---
[Question ID: 2013-gs1-q61-unacademy]

## Question 62 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2013-gs1-q62-unacademy**
**Q6. Critically discuss the objectives of Bhoodan and Gramdan Movements initiated by Acharya Vinoba Bhave and their success. (10 marks, 200 words) Tags: Post-independence consolidation and reorganization within the country.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to begin with the** **backgrounds of the Movements briefly.**-**In Body,**-**DiscusstheobjectivesoftheMovements.**-**Discuss the success of the Movements.**-**Conclude with its real progress, as felt by the** **mass.** **Answer:**The**demand for land reforms,**through distribution of land to the tillers, and doing away with multiple layers of intermediaries, particularly in those parts of the country where the**Zamindari system**was prevalent, was an important part of the thinking of the leaders of our freedom movement. In this regard**Bhoodan and** **Gramdan movements played a very crucial role** in the post-independent period.

## **ObjectivesoftheBhoodanandGramdanMovements**

- **Bhoodanwasanattemptatlandreform,atbringing**about**institutional changes in agriculture, like** **land redistribution**through a movement and not simply through government legislation but also through voluntary donation. This was aimed to**reduce inequality** in the society.
- Eminent Gandhian constructive worker **Acharya** **Vinoba Bhave drew upon Gandhian techniques** **and ideas such as constructive work and** **trusteeship to launch the Bhoodan Movement in** **the early 1950s.**-**The Gramdan Movement is the latest phase of** **the Bhoodan movement.** It is the most significant development of the Bhoodan movement. This movement, at first grew spontaneously in 1952 in the village Mangroth in U.P.
- The **objectives of the Gramdan Movement were-** Social reconstruction, Economic upliftment, building a new social order based on equality and cooperation, spirit of renunciation of private ownership, and collective ownership and communism.
- In the Gramdan Movement, an **entire village** **wouldpooltheirshareforthelandtobecultivated** cumulatively and everyone would get their share on a need basis.

## **Success of the Bhoodan and Gramdan Movements**

- Initially, the **Bhoodan Movement achieved** **massive success.**Even without being compelled to donate the land,**more than 4 million acres of land** **were donated** by the land owners.
- Since the land was donated, it helped **reduce** **inequality in the society.** This movement established an idea among the masses that land is a source of nature and it must belong to all equally.
- **Gramdan movement brought more inclusiveness** in the society as there would be no land owners and the community would allocate resources to everyone as per the need.
- The slogan popularised by **Karl Marx**which says, “From each according to his ability, to each according to his needs”, was aptly implemented by the Gramdan movement.**Althoughcorruption,bribery,landlitigation,political**gimmickshadowedboththemovementsandhampered the desired progress, it**helped in ending illiteracy**and mutual quarrel among people to some extent. The problem of**rural indebtedness was reduced.**Rural economy and agriculture improved. The**standard of** **morality increased** among people. The movements have had more success among the Adivasis, who have a strong tradition of social cohesiveness Thus, both these movements, despite having setbacks in achieving the full potential in land reform, provided much needed ground for land reform awareness.

**Q11. The political and administrative reorganization of states and territories has been a continuous ongoing process since the mid-nineteenth century. Discuss with examples. (250 Words, 15 Marks) Approach**

-

## **Body**

- Mention the reorganization of states and territories in India starting from the colonial era to recent developments.
- **Conclusion:Concludetheanswermentioning**factors of reorganisation and its contribution in the evolving political and administrative landscape of India.**Answer:** **Introduction:**The political and administrative reorganization of states and territories in India has been an ongoing processsincethemid-nineteenthcentury.Thisprocess involves the creation of new states, the reorganization of existing states, and the adjustment of administrative boundaries.**Body:** **Colonial Era:**British India: During the colonial era, British India witnessed the reorganization of administrative units for better governance and control. The Bengal Presidency was divided in 1905 into East Bengal and Assam, and the rest of Bengal, based on religious and administrative considerations. However, this partition was reversed in 1911 due to widespread protests. Princely States: The consolidation and reorganization of princely states were also carried out by the British. The merger of multiple small princely states into larger administrative units, such as the integration of various princely states into the Central Provinces and Berar, aimed to streamline administration.**Post-Independence Era:** **Linguistic Reorganization:**After India’s independence in 1947, there was a significant focus on linguistic reorganization to create states based on linguistic and cultural identities. The States Reorganization Commissionin1956ledtotheformationofnewstates, such as Andhra Pradesh, Maharashtra, and Gujarat, along linguistic lines.**Reorganization of Northeast:**The northeastern region witnessed multiple reorganizations to address ethnic and regional aspirations. The creation of new states like Meghalaya, Manipur, Mizoram, and Arunachal Pradesh aimed to accommodate cultural and tribal diversity.**Recent Reorganizations:** **Uttarakhand and Jharkhand:**In 2000, Uttarakhand was carved out of Uttar Pradesh, and Jharkhand was separated from Bihar to create new states. This reorganization aimed to address regional imbalances and provide better governance to the people.**Telangana:In2014,Telanganawasformedasaseparate**state from Andhra Pradesh, driven by demands for regional development and identity.**Jammu and kashmir:**In August 2019, the Indian government abrogated Article 370 of the Constitution, revoking the special status of Jammu and Kashmir. The state was bifurcated into two separate Union Territories – Jammu and Kashmir, and Ladakh. This reorganization aimed to bring about administrative and governance changes in the region.**Conclusion:** The political and administrative reorganization of statesandterritorieshasbeenshapedbyvariousfactors such as linguistic considerations, regional aspirations, ethnic diversity, and administrative efficiency. The reorganization aims to ensure effective governance, address regional aspirations, and accommodate diverse identities,contributing to theevolving political and administrative landscape of India.
