## Question 64 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2021-gs1-q64-unacademy**
**Q3. Assess the main administrative issues and socio- cultural problems in the integration process of Indian Princely States. (10 Marks, 150 Words) Tags: Post-independence consolidation and reorganization within the country.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **Introduction: Briefly inform about Princely** **States and their status.**-**Body: Mention administrative issues and** **socio-cultural problems in the integration** **process of Indian Princely States.**-**Conclusion: Write about the government’s** **efforts to control the situation.** **Answer:** The history of the Princely States dates from the classical period of Indian history, the predominant usage of the term Princely state refers explicitly to a semi-sovereign principality that was not directly governed by the British but rather by a local ruler, subject to a form of an indirect rule on some matters.
- **Princely States accounted for about 40% of the** **colonial territory** and numbered more than 500. They enjoyed supremacy and were protected from both external assault and their own people under British patronage during the British administration.
- After independence, many of them began to consider an independent life, despite British Prime Minister Clement Atlee’s vague assertion that paramountcy could not be transferred to either India or Pakistan. However, the British administration later clarified the situation and encouraged the States to join one of the dominions when a few States had made their minds up. **Administrative Issues and Socio-Cultural Problems** **in the Integration Process of Indian Princely States:** The presence of such autonomous entities within India may have created substantial political, security, and administrative issues, jeopardising the country’s hardwon independence and integrity. The most difficult step of Princely state unification with India began in December 1947.
- The accession regions to the Dominion of India (or Pakistan) was permitted by the **Instruments of** **Accession**signed by the rulers on three grounds:**defence, external affairs, and communications.** - The prospect of ceding their status and prestige did not sit well with the Princely States.
- Before independence, these States included Jodhpur, Bhopal, and Travancore, while afterward Junagarh, Hyderabad, and Kashmir all presented issues.
- The departure of the British was seen by many of the monarchs as the perfect **opportunity to** **declare autonomy** and establish their own States as nations on the international map.
- Some Princely states desired to maintain their independence because they had sufficient natural resource reserves and were thought to be selfsufficient.
- The **Telangana Rebellion of 1946–1951** was a peasant uprising led by communists against the Princely state of Hyderabad in the Telangana region that sprang out of protests.
- Despite having a Hindu king and a sizable Hindu population, the Rajput Princely realm had a leaning towards Pakistan.

## **Government’s Efforts to Control the Situation**

- **Raja Hari Singh was a Hindu king of Kashmir,** which had a Muslim population of around 75%. Due to his reservations about both democracy and communalism, he was opposed to joining either India or Pakistan.
- However,SheikhAbdullah’spopularpoliticalforces sought to join India. India, on the other hand, stayed calm and, like in Junagadh and Hyderabad, left the decision to the people of Kashmir.
- Pakistan, on the other hand, despised the idea of a vote and launched an offensive stance in October 1947, killing numerous Pathan tribesmen, prompting Hari Singh to seek Indian military assistance.
- Maharaja Hari Singh signed an ‘Instrument of Accession’ with the Indian Government after some time. India demanded that Pakistan leave the valley and that peace be restored.
- The United Kingdom and the United States dominated the United Nations Security Council and sided with Pakistan in a highly partisan manner. On 31st December 1948, a ceasefire was proclaimed, effectively dividing Kashmir along the ceasefire line, which is now known as the **‘LoC’ or Line of Control.** - The United Nations approved a Resolution in 1951 asking for a referendum on the condition thatPakistanremovesitssoldiers,whichnever happened, and therefore the vote could not take place.
- Later, elections were held in Kashmir, and the ConstituentAssemblywasformedinKashmir, which confirmed Kashmir’s accession to India and Pakistan continues to refer to Kashmir as a part of its territory.
- **Junagadh was a minor state in modern day** **Gujarat** on the coast of Saurashtra, surrounded by Indian territory and so having no geographical ties to Pakistan. Even so, on 15th August 1947, the Nawab of Junagadh declared his state’s accession to Pakistan.
- The majority of the state’s residents, who were Hindus, wanted to join India. Pakistan even agreed to Junagadh’s admission. The residents of the state, on the other hand, would not accept the king’s choice.
- As a result of the public rebellion, Nawab was forced to flee to Pakistan, where he established a provisional administration. Dewan of JunagadhlaterurgedtheIndianGovernmentto interfere, and the Indian army was dispatched. A poll was held, and overwhelming support for joining India was seen.
- **Although the Nizam had legal right** to maintain Hyderabad’s independence in post-partition India, India never accepted the idea of a sovereign state in its centre and with the onset of partition violence and subsequent issues with Pakistan involving Kashmir, increasingly prepared to bring Hyderabadintoaccessioninwhateverwayneeded.
- So, the Nizam-ruled Princely state was invaded by the Indian Armed Forces following Operation Polo in September 1948, a military operation.
- Since then, Hyderabad became a part of India.
- Many small States were merged to establish five new unions: **Rajasthan, PEPSU (Patiala and** **East Punjab States Union), Madhya Bharat,** **Saurashtra, and Travancore-Cochin.** Clearly, the merger of the States compensated for the loss of land due to the founding of Pakistan in terms of both area and population.
- It undoubtedly aided in the healing of ‘the partition wounds.’
- The **existence of French and Portuguese** **settlements**along the coast posed another integration problem. Two of the most important were**Pondicherry, a French settlement,**and**Goa,** **a Portuguese town.**- After years of discussions with the Indian Government, the French Government decided to hand over**Pondicherry** and other French colonies to India in 1954. Goa, as well as Daman and Diu and Dadra and Nagar Haveli, served as the capital of the Portuguese territories.
- The Portuguese were insistent on staying in India, and because Portugal was a NATO member, Britain and the US were willing to support them. The Indian Government, which wasdevotedtoresolvinginternationalconflicts through peace and dialogue, had **little interest** **in taking direct military action to liberate** **Goa**and other Portuguese possessions. India was born in very difficult circumstances. Freedom came with the partition of the country and one associated turmoil regarding Princely States. The interim government took a firm stance against the possible division of India into small principalities of different sizes.**Sardar Patel played a historic role** in negotiating with the rulers of Princely States firmly but diplomatically and bringing most of them into the Indian Union.
[Question ID: 2021-gs1-q64-unacademy]

## Question 65 (Year: 2018 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2018-gs1-q65-unacademy**
**Q12. Discuss whether the formation of new states in recent times is beneficial or not for the economy of India. (15 marks, 250 words) Tag: Post-independence consolidation and reorganization within the country.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, briefly write about the need** **to form new states.**-**In Body, mention the challenges and** **opportunities in the creation and post-** **formation of new states, focusing mainly on** **the economic aspect.**-**Conclude with, how economic prosperity** **can be achieved with good governance, in** **both, smaller and bigger states.** **Answer:**Formationofthenewstatesofferpossibilitiesofhaving better governance structure, greater participation for people, administrative convenience for the State and equitable distribution of resources. There are also intellectual debates between those who see smaller states as a boon for development, while others say that dividing existing states can breed animosity to an extent that national political stability comes under threat.**The formation of new states in recent times provide**

## **opportunity for boosting the economy of India**

- The University of Oxford provides the first systematic evidence of the **impact of redrawing** **boundaries of states.**- Examining outcomes 12 years after the formation of the new states, they have marked an**increase in economic activity** immediately across the border in the new states.
- The **Eleventh Plan document**provides some data which support that the smaller newly carved states specifically Uttarakhand and Chhattisgarh**grew** **at a rate faster** (at 9.31% and 7.35% respectively during the period 2004-05 to 2008-09) than their parent states (Uttar Pradesh which grew at 6.29% and Madhya Pradesh which grew at 4.89% during the same five-year period).
- The existence of new states would mean the **creation of new capital cities** (such as Ranchi, Raipur, and Dehradun) and the associated infrastructure.
- Itiswidelyunderstoodthatcapitalcitiesattract firms for various services and agglomeration economies like:  Infrastructure of national /international standards such as highways,  Mass transport,  High tele density,  International airport,  Relatively large international community and culture, and  World-renowned cultural institutions and universities.
- **School enrolment**also**increases,** suggesting greater investment in human capital.
- Durable goods remain comparable across the two sides of the state border, suggesting that free movement of labour and capital can mitigate differences in economic opportunities across proximate geographies.
- Local expertise and technology can be harnessed better through local government. **The formation of new states in recent times pose**

## **challenges for the economy of India**

## **Claims on central finances**

- In the Thirteenth Finance Commission devolutionformula,thefiscalcapacitydistance has the greatest weightage (47.5%) in the devolution formula, followed by population (25%).
- Which means that the **new states may not** **always get additional claims on central** **finances.**- A larger state, by virtue of having a larger market, can generate financial resources more efficiently to supply public goods to its citizens.**However, when** **there are too many diverse groups in a sprawling** **state, conflict emerges.**- Instead of public-goods provisioning, redistribution of resources among regions becomes the central political issue. In other words,**when the diversity effect becomes greater than** **the scale effect, there is an economic case for a** **smaller state.**- Newstatesaresuccessfulindevelopingtheirregions depending**on the extent of decentralization.** - For instance, if a small state is unable to devolve enough funds and physical resources to a far-flung area of the state to maintain its roads, the result would be inadequate quality of public services (such as schools, colleges, roads, or irrigation) with the population in the remote area feeling as neglected as before
- **Small states do not generate enough revenue for** **the state,** thus are heavily dependent on the central assistance.
- Creation of new states means **establishing** **new administrative machineries**and new institutions which leads to**increased revenue** **expenditure,**in turn puts pressure on fiscal pressures for the government.**Evidence shows that both large and small states** **have fared well, and that poor performance is not** **necessarily linked to size.** The more long-lasting solution to regional and intra-state disparities is to create viable proposals for reducing them within the existing framework of governance rather than create a new state. Unless there is substantially better governance, there is no guarantee that a new state will lead to better economic performance.
[Question ID: 2018-gs1-q65-unacademy]

## Question 66 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2013-gs1-q66-unacademy**
**Q7. Write a critical note on the evolution and significance of the slogan, ‘Jai Jawan Jai Kisan’. (10 marks, 200 words) Tags: Post-independence consolidation and reorganization within the country.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, start with who gave the** **slogan and its meaning.**-**In Body, discuss the backdrop and** **significance of Jai Jawan and Jai Kishan.**-**Conclude with a demonstration, how the** **slogans have evolved with time.** **Answer:** The slogan ‘Jai Jawan Jai Kishan’ was given by Prime Minister Lal Bahadur Shastri in the mid-1960s, at a time when India was battling dual threats of hunger and war. The slogans identify with our agriculture sector and national defense.

## **Evolution of Jai Jawan**

- In the 1960s, India was facing military aggression from both China and Pakistan.
- The **Indo-China war took place in 1962** and IndoPak war took place in 1965. India was surrounded by hostile neighbors.
- Even the cold war politics was complicating the situation as Pakistan was an ally of the USA. So, India needed a strong army to secure her from foreign aggression. **To boost up the morale of our soldiers** and to make it stronger, our Prime Minister Lal Bahadur Shastri gave the slogan Jai Jawan.

## **Evolution of Jai Kisan**

- In the 1960s, India was facing a serious economic crisis.
- India experienced twin draughts. Our economy was mostly dependent on agriculture and in agriculture, we were not self-sufficient.
- We were largely dependent on import of food grains. We had to import PL 480 from the USA.
- The Green Revolution had not come in yet. So, under the circumstances, to strengthen the hand of the farmers and boost agricultural production the slogan ‘Jai Kisan’ was given by prime Minister Lal Bahadur Shastri.

## **Significance of ‘Jai Jawan Jai Kisan’**

- The Green Revolution was initiated during the trying times, and it helped India in achieving selfsufficiency in the agricultural sector.
- The Indian Army’s morale was restored and the results of the 1965 and the 1971 war, against our arch-rival Pakistan, are a testimony to it.
- As a result of which, India emerged as a global power in the sub-continent. The slogans have evolved over the years. Our ex-Prime Minister Atal Bihari Vajpayee added the slogan **‘Jai** **Vigyan’**to**‘Jai Jawan Jai Kisan’,** which underlines the importance of science and technology, along with agriculture and security, to make the country prosperous.
[Question ID: 2013-gs1-q66-unacademy]

## Answer

Maulana Azad had**immense contribution**in the educational and other sectors in pre and post independent India.**Contribution of Maulana Abul Kalam Azad to pre-**

- Azad was a very active leader of the Indian independencemovement.Hewasabriefbutactive participant in the **Khilafat Movement (1920-24),**during which he held the post of the**President of** **the All-India Khilafat Committee.**- After the Khilafat movement Azad actively participated in the**Civil Disobedience**initiatives namely the**Dandi March (1930) and Quit India** **Movement (1942).**- He was**imprisoned** at several instances during the period of 1920-1945.

Maulana Abul Kalam Azad was an**ardent follower of** **Gandhian philosophy,**his contribution**in promoting** **the integrity of the nation, reforming education** **sector,creatingbrotherhoodamongthecommunities,**etc., remain the highest point of his life. ## Question 68 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2013-gs1-q68-unacademy**
**Q10. Critically examine the compulsions which prompted India to play a decisive role in the emergence of Bangladesh. (10 marks, 200 words)**

#

## Answer

**Tags:** Post-independence consolidation and reorganization within the country.

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to write briefly about the** **background of the Bangladesh war.**-**In Body, discuss the compulsions which** **prompted India to play a decisive role in the** **emergence of Bangladesh.**-**Conclude the answer by stating the role** **played by India in creating an autonomous** **Bangladesh.** **Answer:**The 1947 partition drew**two independent nations**- India and Pakistan. During 1947 - 1971, Pakistan experienced prolonged phases of military rule, which made it more difficult for**ethnic minorities**residing in the eastern wing to gain access to**political power,** **social prestige,** and economic development, which were enjoyed in West Pakistan.

## **Oppression of East Pakistan by West Pakistan**

- The tyrannical attitude destroyed **unique Bengali** **culture,** and to some extent, opposed the thought of secularism. The Pakistani government aspired to turn Bangladeshis into exemplary Muslims by alienating them from their Hindu background.
- Since the formation of Pakistan, the Western part **branded the Eastern as inferior** because it considered the Muslims in the eastern wing subordinate due to their social and cultural affiliation with the Hindu population, powerful, rich, and dominating in East Pakistan before the partition of the subcontinent.
- **Operation Searchlight**was the planned genocide on the 25th of March**1971** and was undertaken by the West Pakistani government against its own citizens of the eastern wing.
- TheWestPakistaniArmyconferrednocompassion for Bengalis, and the rules of engagement were at no time adhered to. The West Pakistani Army would kill civilians without any mercy.

## **Compulsions Which Prompted India to Join the War**

- India had a strained relationship with Pakistan, and having both East and West Pakistan in its border was a major security concern.
- The influx of refugees in India from East Pakistan was creating an economic burden, as well as law and order problem.
- The development of the northeast sector in India was facing multiple challenges due to the influx of refugees, and its ethnic identity was under threat.
- The atrocities committed on the people of East Pakistan was the final straw, which forced India to intervene on humanitarian grounds.

## **India’s role**

- Bangladesh got **continuous moral support** from India since when the Liberation war began.
- Indian Prime Minister Indira Gandhi was able to **acquire support from the Soviet Union, the** **United Kingdom and France** to assure that there would be no directives favouring Pakistan in the United Nations Security Council.
- Despite the protection and encouragement from the United States, Pakistan did not have high chances of winning the war as the Soviet Union played against the United States’ efforts during the war. **Russians encircled the US fleet in the Bay of** **Bengal** region, not allowing the fleet to close in on Chittagong or Dhaka, securing the independence of Bangladesh.
- Under the assistance of Sheikh Muijib-ur-Rahman Mukti Fauj, the **Mukti Bahini**was born soon after the crackdown by the Pakistani Army on the 25th of March, 1971. The**Mukti Bahini** played a key role in the Liberation War of 1971.
- After Pakistan began several attacks on Indian territory on the 3rd of December 1971, India joined Bangladesh in its military efforts against Pakistani aggression. With the Indian support and commands from the military officers of East Pakistan, the **West Pakistani military was** **defeated.**- Pakistan**eventually surrendered to the joint** **forces of Bangladesh and India,**leading to victory for Bangladesh on the 16th of December 1971 While many Bengalis sought the guerrilla force favouring independence, many others, particularly women and children, fled the country and took refuge in India’s closest neighbouring country. It is estimated that the**number of refugees taking shelter in India** **during the liberation war was about 10 million.** Thus, India’s role in the liberation of Bangladesh and its strategic decisions at that time pay dividends even today.
[Question ID: 2013-gs1-q68-unacademy]

## Question 69 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q69-unacademy**
**Q12.How far is it correct to say that the First World War was fought essentially for the preservation of balance of power? (250 Words 15 Marks)**

#

## Answer

**Answer:** **Introduction:**World War I, also known as the**Great War,**started in 1914 after the assassination of**Archduke Franz** **Ferdinand**of**Austria.**His murder catapulted into a war across Europe that lasted until 1918. Concept of Balance of Power in International relations describes the distribution of power among actors, and the ways in which that power is maintained. The concept gained prominence in Post- Napoleanic Europe. European powers like**Britain**and**France,** often sceptical and confrontational regarding their colonies, were nonetheless compelled to cooperate in thefirsthalfofthe20thcenturytomaintainthebalance of power in Europe and address common threats.

## **World War I as means to preserve balance of Power**

- By 1900, several European nations had empires across the globe with Britain and France holding the beacon of Imperialism. Tensions rose between opposing empires, including Germany, AustriaHungary and the Ottoman Empire, leading to the creation of the **Allied Powers (Britain and France)**and**Central Powers (Germany, Austria-Hungary** **and the Ottoman Empire)**during**World War I.**- Britain wanted to secure the existing**Balance of** **Power**by forging alliance with France (erstwhile enemy) against Germany, for as long as Europe was divided,**Britain’s hegemony** was secured.
- **France**was determined to restore its prestige and power and to regain the provinces of Alsace and Lorraine, while**Germany**implemented**‘Weltpolitik’,** i.e., world policy to transform Germany into a global power through aggressive diplomacyandtheacquisitionofoverseascolonies.
- **“Blank check assurance”**between Germany and Austria-Hungary sought military and political assurance for securing the Balkans. It gave AustroHungarian leaders the confidence needed to embark on a war against Serbia.**However, the war was also fought to pursue some**

## **strategic goals**

- **Austria-Hungary**wanted Austrian dominance in the**Balkans**and to teach the Serbs a lesson for supposedly sponsoring**Gavrillo Princip**and**other** **assassins.**-**Russia**fought to protect its alliance with Serbia and to gain possession of the**Bosphorus**and thus access to the**Mediterranean.**- For**France,** a united Germany was a major threat for its colonial endeavours and it also wanted to regain regions of Alsace and Lorraine.
- **German unification** marked with a strong military combined with the strongest industrial base in Europe led to its desire for a greater share in European expansion. World War I shattered established alliances and territorial boundaries, leading to the collapse of empires and the emergence of new nations. This upheaval dismantled the old order, replacing it with instability and setting the stage for future geopolitical tensions, ultimately altering the course of European history.

## Question 70 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q70-unacademy**
**Q13.How far was the Industrial Revolution in Englandresponsibleforthedeclineofhandicrafts and cottage industries in India? (250 Words 15 Marks)**

#

## Answer

**Answer:** **Introduction:** **Romesh Chandra Dutt**calculated that by 1850, the value of Britain’s cotton production had reached**2 billion pounds,**while the number of handloom weavers in India fell from**10 million in 1850**to just**2.5 million by 1900.**This highlighted the inverse relationship between England’s industrialisation and India’s de-industrialisation. Before industrialization in Britain, India had a thriving handicraftmarketthatflourishedinEuropeanmarkets. However, the industrial revolution transformed this dynamic, reducing India to a mere supplier of raw materials and an importer of finished goods. Handicrafts contributed around 25% to India’s GDP and employed 20 million people predominantly from rural areas.**Responsibility of Britain’s Industrialisation for the**

## **decline of handicrafts and cottage industry**

- TransportationofRawmaterialslikecotton,opium and jute to meet demands of British Industries. By 1800, Britain started importing approximately **50** **million pounds** of raw cotton.
- Excessive tariff and conditionalities on Indian goods and thereby compelling Indians to sell raw materials at cheap rates and buy-back British finished/manufactured goods at high prices. This led to the introduction of the concept of **“One way** **freetrade”toputBritishmanufacturingIndustries** on a sound footing.
- In matters of quality, Indian weavers could easily hold the dominance but in the matter of price he was hopelessly beaten by the cheap machine made goods churned out in British factories.
- India, which contributed 24% to global GDP and whose product embellished the European market in the late 18th Century, was made to import half of European cotton from Manchester by 1830. **Karl Marx regarded “It was the British intruder who** **broke up the Indian handloom and destroyed the** **spinning wheel”.** **Industrialisation in Britain fuelled but was not the** **only cause of decline of Indian Handicrafts and**

## **cottage industries**

- Lack of Patronage of local rulers and absence of scientific techniques of production during the later mughals.
- Low significance given to the development of the navy resulted in lack of export opportunities for Indian traders.
- Excessivedependenceonhandloomsandlowscale family traditional business denied **economies of** **scale.**- Frequent recurrence of Famine, plague and epidemics killed artisans in large numbers. Eg: From1765to1858,Bengalexperienced12famines and**four periods of severe scarcities.**

## **Consequences of Deindustrialization**

- Occupational imbalances leading to rural unemployment and underemployment.
- Rise in Disguise unemployment. Eg: Agrarian labour rose from 7 crore to 11 crore in 1775-1825.
- Rise of new proletariat class of landless labour.
- Land became a saleable commodity. With the advent of industrialization in Britain, the Indianeconomywastransformedintoasteppingstone to support England’s economic growth, and in this process, handicraft and cottage industries were among the hardest hit. The shift toward mass production and mechanisationledtosignificantde-industrializationin India, undermining traditional crafts and devastating local artisans.

## Question 71 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2023-gs1-q71-unacademy**
**Q3.Bring out the socio-economic effects of the introduction of railways in different countries of the world. (10 Marks, 150 Words)**

#

## Answer

## **Approach**

- **Introduction:** Define railways as a transformative 19th-century technology and highlight their global spread.
- **Body:** Discuss socio-economic impacts under key themes—markets, industry, agriculture, urbanisation, and society—using regionspecific examples (Europe, USA, Japan, India, Africa, Latin America).
- **Conclusion:**Present a balanced view: railways were drivers of integration and growth, but their outcomes depended on context—nation-building in industrial states, extraction in colonies.**Answer:** **Introduction:**The introduction of railways in the 19th century marked a watershed in global economic history. By drastically reducing transport costs and travel time, railways reshaped production, trade, and societies. However, their effects were uneven—while they advanced industrialisation and national integration in developed countries, in colonies they often reinforced extractive structures.**Body**
**1. Economic Integration**- Linked distant markets, reduced price gaps, and facilitated large-scale trade.
-**Example:**Midwest grain to Atlantic ports (USA); wheat from Russian steppe to Europe; cotton and jute from India to Britain.**2. Industrial Growth**- Stimulated coal, iron, and steel industries through massive demand.

- Enabled mass production and distribution of goods; created modern managerial practices.
-**Example:**Ruhr industrialisation in Germany, steel boom in USA.**3. Agriculture and Land Use**- Encouraged cash crop farming and frontier expansion.

- Improved famine relief in India but also exposed peasants to global price shocks.
-**Example:**Argentina’s pampas integrated into global beef and grain trade.**4. Urbanisation and Mobility**- Created railway towns and industrial hubs (Chicago, Nagpur, Nairobi).

- Expanded migration and commuting; boosted tourism and pilgrimages.**5. Social and Political Effects**- Spread literacy, newspapers, and national consciousness in Europe and Japan.
- In colonies, reinforced racial and class divisions (segregated compartments, “Jim Crow” laws in USA South).
- Enhanced state control and military mobility (Russia’s Trans-Siberian, British India).

##**Regional Snapshot**

-**Britain/Europe:**National markets, managerial revolution, commuter towns.
-**USA:**Nation-building, frontier expansion, but also dispossession of Native Americans.
-**Japan:**Cohesive national economy and rapid industrialisation (Meiji era).
-**India & Africa:**Extraction-oriented networks prioritising ports and resource corridors.
-**Latin America:**Export enclaves (coffee, beef, minerals) benefiting elites, leaving disparities.**Conclusion**Railways were a universal agent of change, compressing space and time while transforming economies and societies. In industrial nations, they fostered integration, industrial growth, and national identity. In colonies, however, they often entrenched inequality and served imperial interests. Thus, the legacy of railways lies not merely in steel tracks but in thediversetrajectoriesofdevelopmenttheyenabled— or constrained—across the world.
[Question ID: 2023-gs1-q71-unacademy]

## Question 72 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2021-gs1-q72-unacademy**
**Q13. “There arose a serious challenge to the Democratic State System between the two World Wars.”Evaluatethestatement.(15Marks,250Words) Tags: History of the World Events from the 18th Century such as World Wars.**

#

## Answer

##**Decoding the Question**

-**Introduction: Briefly describe the interwar** **period between two world wars.**-**Body: Explain the causes behind the collapse** **of democratic state system post first world** **war along with the challenges posed to the** **democratic state system between 1919-39.**-**Conclusion: Conclude the answer suitably.** **Answer:** A succession of economic, social, and political crises plagued the interwar years (1918 to 1939), which led to the growth of authoritarian and ultranationalist regimes in various forms throughout the world. The established democratic system in the West was severely threatened by this. All the great alternatives to democracy, whether they were of ancient or modern origin, had substantial political, economic, diplomatic, and military losses. Following the Allied’ victory in World War I, the monarchy, aristocracy, and oligarchy of antiquity were no longer considered legitimate.

## **Cause of Collapse of Democratic System**

- The Great Depression’s economic hardships prompted enormous social discontent throughout the world, which in many cases resulted in the fall of democratic governments and a large rise in fascism.
- By 1930, unemployment in Germany had reached a record high of 22%.
- Inthe1930s,therewasaseverethreattodemocracy all over the world due to the dissolution of the League of Nations and the emergence of militant dictatorships.

## **Challenges to Democratic State System**

- **Fear of Communism:** Communism was spreading fast after the Russian Revolution of 1917 and as communism is against any private property, it created tension among capitalist nations.
- **Nazism in Germany:** People in Germany, which were afflicted by unemployment, enraged by territorial losses, and disheartened by an incompetent government, favoured strong leaders like Hitler, who swiftly transformed democracy into dictatorship. In 1933, Hitler’s Nazi party was invited to join the coalition government, but conservatives thought the “communist party” posed a bigger danger.
- Hitler rose to power in Germany with lax institutional controls. The Nazi Party quickly emerged as Germany’s sole political force. Communism was prohibited. The constitution was put on hold.
- Persecution of Jews: The Jewish community faced more persecution once the Nazis came to power and put their antisemitic philosophy and laws into effect.  Approximately six million European Jews were killed by the Nazis and their allies in a slaughter that became known as the Holocaust.  Jews were effectively barred from participation in politics, higher education, the workforce, and all professional positions in 1936.
- A lack of understanding of minority rights encouraged the persecution of Jews and Romanis and the spread of imperialist concepts like lebensraum.
- The capitalist bloc countries’ political intolerance of communism resulted in their oversight of German excesses, such as the Munich Agreement.
- Unresolved political rivalries between the Nationalist Party and the Communist Party led to the Spanish Civil War.
- The rise of ultranationalism and enlistment encouraged militarism in world politics.
- Markets deteriorated due to the economic system’s failure, conventional industries disappeared, and their production was disrupted.
- Iron Guard: The term given to a far-right political organisation and party in Romania from 1927 and the beginning of World War II. It advocated the Orthodox Christian faith while being ultranationalist, antisemitic, anti-communist, and anticapitalist.
- Rise of Fascism: Benito Mussolini and other radicals founded a political party during World War I, which marked the beginning of the growth of fascism in Italy (called a fasci).
- Fascism also grew in popularity outside of Europe, particularly in South America, the Middle East, and East Asia.  In China, the Kuomintang (Nationalist Party of China) faction known as **Kai-tsu** **p’ai (Reorganization)** backed Nazism in the late 1930s.  The Al-Muthanna Club of Iraq was a panArab organisation that backed Nazism and used cabinet minister Saib Shawkat to influencetheIraqigovernmentbycreating a paramilitary youth movement.
- JapanrisingasColonialPowers:Japanwentthrough aperiodofindustrializationandmilitarization;the Meiji Restoration saw Japan modernise the fastest and played a role in the country’s rise to great power status and the construction of an empire. After the First World War, the League of Nations’ efforts to keep the peace were unsuccessful. Hitler’s invasion of Poland in 1939 ultimately prompted France and Great Britain to declare war on Germany, starting World War II. More lives would be lost, and morelandandpropertywouldbedestroyedduringthe following six years of fighting than during any other war in history.
[Question ID: 2021-gs1-q72-unacademy]

## Question 73 (Year: 2019 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2019-gs1-q73-unacademy**
**Q13. Explain how the foundations of the modern world were laid by the American and French Revolution. (15 Marks, 250 words) Tag: World History.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to briefly write about** **both French and American Revolutions.**-**In Body,**-**Write how American and French** **Revolution laid the foundation of the** **modern world.**-**In Conclusion, try to mention the overall** **importance of both Revolutions.** **Answer:** **The American Revolution (1776-81) and the French** **Revolution (1789-94)** are the two most important events in history. It gave a deathblow to the old monarchicalsystemofgovernanceandgerminatedthe seeds of modernity. These brought socio- economic transformation, democratic ideas, and values associated to overall human development in various countries.

## **Impact of American Revolution on modern world**

- The American Revolution of 1776 is one of the most significant events in the history of mankind with global impact. America emerged as an independent sovereign nation and the emergence of the first modern democracy on the map of the world.
- It gave the **first written Constitution** to the world and laid the foundation of people’s right of governing themselves.
- It promoted **democratic** **values** like Constitutionalism, republic government, fundamental rights, no representation without taxation etc.
- The liberal & Progressive political system based on the philosophy of Capitalism came into existence. The **Doctrine of Laissez-faire** (Free Trade) came into existence. It also laid the way for an open market and new trade relations with the colonized countries.
- It **inspired anti colonial and anti-monarchical** **movements** in various countries like Netherlands, Belgium, India, Geneva etc.
- In the society it brought **egalitarianism, religious** **freedom and meritocracy,** which helped in overall development of people and improve their quality of life.

## **Impact of French Revolution on Modern World**

- French Revolution was the most important modern revolution, which was an outcome of flawed social structure for radical transformation of society.
- **Itdemocratisedsocietybyendingtheageoldfeudal** system and system of privileges & concession.
- The ideals of **liberty, equality and fraternity** brought political awakening in the whole world and inspired various national movements like Indian National Movement etc.
- It also challenged the despotism & corruption of thechurchandemphasizedontheideaofscientific and rational thinking, which in future laid the **foundation of secularism.**- The revolutionary leaders**abolished the** **monarchy** and altered most of France’s social and political institutions to make them more rational and modern. It also developed the feeling of nationalism among people.
- They proclaimed a republic, instituted parliamentary elections, introduced educational reforms,createdanewrevolutionarycalendar,and reorganized France’s electoral districts to make representation more democratic.
- It also emphasised **on right to vote, abolition of** **slavery**etc. for more egalitarian society and rulebased governance. The French Revolution and American Revolution laid down the foundation of modernity in the world,**through socio-economic and political changes** in the society. The revolutions remained the philosophical basis and aspiration of the people beyond the national boundaries and inspired and promoted change in various countries through rational and scientific thinking.

## Question 74 (Year: 2016 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2016-gs1-q74-unacademy**
**Q7. The anti-colonial struggles in West Africa were ledbytheneweliteofWestern-educatedAfricans. Examine. (12.5 marks, 200 words) Tag: History of the world-Colonization.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, you can start with how West** **Africa was colonized by France and Britain** **and then briefly write about the leaders of** **West-Africa.**-**In Body, mention the role of leaders like** **Kwame Nkrumah, Léopold Sédar Senghor** **(Senegal), Nnamdi Azikiwe (Nigeria), and** **Félix Houphouet-Boigny (Côte d’Ivoire) in** **the struggle against imperialist power.**-**Conclude by mentioning how the Western** **part of the African continent developed** **a general feeling among the intelligentsia** **that the colonies were being deliberately** **exploited.** **Answer:**West Africa was**colonized by France and Britain.**In the early 1930s, the colonial powers had cultivated, inadvertently, a small elite of local African leaders educated in Western universities, where they became familiar with and fluent in**ideas such as self-** **determination.**As a result, great leaders like**Kwame** **Nkrumah, Léopold Sédar Senghor, Nnamdi Azikiwe** **and Félix Houphouet-Boigny came into prominence** **to lead the struggles for African nationalism.** **Role of Great Leaders Who Led Anti-Colonial**

## **Struggles in West Africa**

- **Kwame Nkrumah:**He was the prominent**Ghanaian nationalist** leader who led the Gold Coast’s drive for independence from Britain and presided over its emergence as the new nation of Ghana.
- He was inspired by Karl Marx and Vladimir Lenin, Marcus Garvey, the Black American leader of the 1920s. Eventually, he immersed himself in political work and organised diasporic pan-Africanists.
- Later, he formed the new **Convention Peoples’** **Party (CPP) in 1949,**a mass-based party that was committed to a program of**immediate** **self-government.**- In 1950, he initiated a campaign of**“positive** **action,”**involving nonviolent protests, strikes, and noncooperation with the British colonial authorities. His**efforts led to Ghana’s** **Independence in 1957.**- Under Nkrumah’s leadership,**Ghana adopted** **somesocialdemocraticpoliciesandpractices.** **Nkrumah created a welfare system,** started various community programs, and established schools.
- **Léopold Sédar Senghor:**Senghor was the**first** **African**agrégé, i.e., the highest rank of qualified teacher in the French school system, was the**propounder of African art on modern painting,** **sculpture, and music, which confirmed his belief** **in Africa’s potential contribution to modern** **culture.**- He was the major proponent of the concept**of** **Negritude.**- He founded the**Senegalese Democratic Bloc** **in 1948.**- Senghor emphasis on**territorial rather than** **federal government** which would result in the proliferation of small, unviable states.
- In December 1959, Senghor made an appeal to French President for independence. Thus, Senegal became an **independent republic,** and Senghor was unanimously elected president.
- **Nnamdi Azikiwe:**He was a**Nigerian statesman** **and political leader**who served as the**first** **President of Nigeria.**- He is considered as a**driving force** behind the nation’s independence.
- Azikiwe was the **founder of the National** **Council of Nigeria and the Cameroons** **(NCNC)andledtheNCNCintotheimportant** **1959 federal elections, which preceded** **Nigerian independence.**-**Félix Houphouët-Boigny:**He was the politician and physician**who was president of Côte d’Ivoire** **(Ivory Coast) from independence in 1960 until** **his death in 1993.**- He led the**independence of Cote d’Ivoire** **from France.** - He pursued liberal free-enterprise policies and developed Côte d’Ivoire’s cash-crop agriculture at a time when many other African nations were pursuing costly and abortive attempts at state-run industrialization.
- Under his leadership the country became a major exporter of cocoa, coffee, pineapples, and palm oil. In western part of the African continent, thus, developed a **general feeling among the intelligentsia**that the colonies**were being deliberately exploited**by more firmly entrenched European political and economic systems and that they had developed a new, wider, and mobilizable public to appeal to for support. Their**efforts and fight against the imperialist regime** **were based on demands for self-determination** and played an important role in forcing the process of decolonization of Africa and independence of many great African nations.

## Question 75 (Year: 2015 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2015-gs1-q75-unacademy**
**Q6. Why did the Industrial Revolution first occur in England? Discuss the quality of life of the people there during the industrialization. How does it comparewiththatinIndiaatpresenttimes?(12.5 marks, 200 words) Tag: History of the world.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, start with a brief** **introduction on the Industrial Revolution.**-**In Body,**-**Discuss the factors responsible.**-**Discuss the impact of the Industrial** **Revolution on the quality of life of** **people.**-**Then compare with that in India at present** **times.** **Answer:**BritainwastheFirstcountryintheworldtoexperience industrialization. Industrialization started during the later half of 18th century in Britain. Only much later in the 19th century other countries of Europe started witnessing industrialization. The unique elements of British politico-administrative system, Economy, Society & religion played a pivotal role in the outbreak of the Industrial Revolution much before other countries of Europe.**Politico Administrative Factors:** The Glorious Revolution which replaced the absolute monarchy of Britain with a Constitutional monarchy took place in 1688. It created liberal & progressive political environment & provided political stability, which provided a favourable environment for economic growth. Since there was no inter-state tax in Britain the whole British island worked as a unified market. There was no obstruction in the transport of goods from one end to the other end of the island. If we see other European nations like France & Germany at that time they had multiple divisions with interstate tax systems. British had an extensive colonial empire. British government issued a royal charter in favour of the private companies to carry out colonization in different parts of the world. These colonies ensured the availability of chief raw materials for industrial production and a large market for British finished goods.

## **Economic Factors**

- Britain started experiencing changes in the field of agriculture from the 16th century. By the middle of the 18th century the agricultural Revolution had taken place in Britain.
- The agricultural Revolution was an outcome of progressive law by British Parliament & Scientific invention.
- British Parliament passed an enclosure law where the peasants were allowed to fence their fields to keep the wild animals & hunting habits away to save crops.
- Private ownership of land was recognized by the British Parliament.
- As a result land became sellable commodity & consolidation of land holding took place.
- Emergenceoflargefarmsincreasedtheproduction efficiency.
- In the 17th century agro research played a pivotal role in agricultural development.
- At the beginning of 18th century zethrotal invented a horse drawn seed drill for sowing seeds & later he developed horse drawn hoe.
- Rotherham plough was invented in 1730 consisted of iron blade & it was lighter & easier to use than the traditional wooden plough.
- All these factors resulted in surplus production in Agriculture. As a result some small peasants were liberated from the compulsion of agricultural activities & worked in factories as a labour.
- British mercantile policy had accumulated huge capital which allowed British manufactures to purchase machines and establish factories.
- Being an island nation the routes of international trade were always open for British merchants.
- The Proximity of iron & coal mines had also facilitated the Industrial Revolution in Britain.

## **Social & Cultural Factors**

- The liberal & progressive socio-cultural life of Britain created an environment of free social thinking facilitated scientific inventions.
- British scientists have invented different tools which had immediate application.
- In 1733 the flying shuttle was invented by John K and brought significant changes in weaving technology.
- In 1769 James Watt invented the steam engine.
- In1785powerloomswereinvented.Thisinvention brought major changes in weaving technology.
- Spinning mule was invented in 1794 to spin cotton & other fibres. All these inventions enhanced agricultural production at a large scale & triggered the Industrial Revolution in Britain.

## **Religious Factor**

- Protestant ethics dominant in Britain had also created a liberal & progressive religious environment in Britain **Impact of Industrial Revolution on quality of life of**

## **people**

- The Industrial Revolution in Britain was guided by the ideology of capitalism. The means of production were privately owned. The capitalists were the owners of the factories.
- Before the Industrial Revolution, most people in Europe worked either as farmers or artisans making hand-crafted goods. The ways in which people lived had not changed significantly since the Middle Ages. Once industrialization began, however, work and family life would be transformed forever.
- Small farmers and seasonal laborers of the land started migrating to the urban centres.
- It had positive impacts like various high-quality cheap goods were available, which gave a boost to urbanization. At the same time, it was a moment of flux as the old social structures and institutions were being transformed.
- There was a trend towards nuclear families, increased social mobility, more emphasis on achieving status.
- Ethical and moral foundations undermined due to problems arising from slums, social crimes, etc. Children tended to enter the workforce at younger ages in the industrial districts.
- Many new factory owners preferred to employ children as they viewed them as more docile and tractable than adults.
- Labour exploitation was also another important feature.

## **Comparison with India at present times**

- After independence, India started on the journey of planned industrialization on the socialistic pattern.
- Unlike Britain, the role of the state was vital with a focus on industrial goods.
- There is less exploitation of the working class as many labour laws are formulated to protect their rights.
- Social institutions such as marriage, family are getting transformed due to industrialization.
- However, 47% of the economy is still agriculturebased, much higher than Britain of the 19th century. Rural-urban migration in search of new job opportunities landing people in slums and poor hygienic conditions.
[Question ID: 2015-gs1-q75-unacademy]

## Question 76 (Year: 2015 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2015-gs1-q76-unacademy**
**Q7. To what extent can Germany be held responsible for causing the two World Wars? Discuss critically. (12.5 marks, 200 words) Tag: History of World.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to start with a brief** **introduction on Germany and two world** **wars.**-**In Body, discuss the responsibility of** **Germany in causing two world wars.**-**Concluding with the notion Germany only** **cannot be blamed, it is a collective failure.** **Answer:**Though putting fingers on Germany alone for launching the world wars would be unfair, as the allied powers were equally to be condemned for opening the door for the infamous war to occur in the first place, which has its root on colonial and imperialist interest. However, for most of it, Germany could be held more responsible.**Germany and WW I:**Though Germany did not induce the entire war, it did playaroleinitsinitiation.Aftertheunificationofits30 states, German people gained many strong nationalist feelings. Bismarck created a complex foreign policy to maintain peace in Europe. The main purpose of his foreign policy was to isolate France. The French isolation ended after new alliances were formed between France-Britain and France-Russia and the Triple entente. Over thirty years, they became the strongest industrial power in Europe after the FrancoPrussian war with the strongest economy and military. Their civilians were cocky, which could potentially be dangerous and perceived as a threat to other countries. This creates tension, which is the basis of enemies’ hatred. Germany had also been planning an attack on France and Russia for about three years before the war started. The formation of Triple Alliance and Triple Entente thus**split Europe**into two rival armed camps and worsened the powers’ relations.**Germany and WW II:** Germany was defeated in World War I, and was compelled to pay heavy reparations to the victors. Its economy could not take the pressure, and it fell apart. As a result of the Treaty of Versailles and the economic disasters that followed, the German people became extremely resentful towards the victors of World War I, particularly France and Great Britain. Therefore, Germany began the fight against them in place of conquering Europe and gaining the pride back. Later Hitler opposed the Versailles and Locarno treaties by remilitarizingtheRhineland.Hefacedlittleopposition from other European powers. Germany and Italy together formed the Rome-Berlin Axis. After taht Germany and Japan signed the Anti-Comintern Pact in which Italy joined in the following year. This all led to World War II. Thus, despite having a predominant role in the World Wars, Germany cannot be held solely responsible for causing the world wars. It can be held accountable only to a certain limited extent. Many other factors and prevailingsocio,politicalandeconomiccircumstances of those times played a major role. Other actors, including Britain, Russia, the USA and the League of Nations, can by no means be discounted. They cannot be absolved.

## Question 77 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2014-gs1-q77-unacademy**
**Q9. What were the events that led to the Suez Crisis in 1956?HowdiditdealafinalblowtoBritain’sself- image as a world power? (10 marks, 150 words) Tag: History of the world.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **Introduction- Start with mentioning the** **location and creation of the Suez Canal.**-**In Body,**-**Discuss the major events that led to the** **Suez Crisis in 1956.**-**ShowhowitdealtafinalblowtoBritain’s** **self-image as a world power.**-**Conclude the answer by mentioning the** **aftermath of the Suez Crisis.** **Answer:** The Suez Canal was built in Egypt under the supervision of French diplomat Ferdinand de Lesseps. The man-made waterway opened in 1869 after ten yearsofconstructionandseparatesmostofEgyptfrom the Sinai Peninsula. At 120 miles long, it connects the Mediterranean Sea to the Indian Ocean by way of the Red Sea, allowing goods to be shipped from Europe to Asia and back more directly. Its value to international trade made it a nearly instant source of conflict among Egypt’s neighbours and Cold War superpowers vying for dominance.

## **Suez Crisis is culmination of following events**

- The Suez Crisis began on October 29, 1956, when Israeli armed forces pushed into Egypt toward the Suez Canal after Egyptian president Gamal Abdel Nasser(1918-70)nationalizedthecanal,avaluable waterway that controlled two-thirds of the oil used by Europe.
- TheIsraelisweresoonjoinedbyFrenchandBritish forces, which nearly brought the Soviet Union into the conflict and damaged their relationships with the United States.
- In the end, Egypt emerged victorious, and the British, French, and Israeli governments withdrew their troops in late 1956 and early 1957. **The Suez crisis is often portrayed as Britain’s last fling** **of the imperial dice.** - In 1956, the globe was indeed still circled by British possessions and dependencies, from the Caribbean in the west to Singapore, Malaya and Hong Kong in the east. Much of the African map was still imperial pink.
- Around1940-50sthegreatestpossessionofBritish empire, the Indian subcontinent, had taken its freedom.
- Nationalist movements were flourishing in most of the rest, patronised by Soviet Russia and encouraged by the United States in its selfappointed role as leader of the free world.
- Britain itself was only beginning to emerge from post-war austerity, its public finances crushed by an accumulation of war debt. In July 1956, the last British soldiers pulled out of the canal zone. On July 26, Egyptian president Gamal Abdel Nasser abruptly announced the nationalization of the Suez Canal Company. Prime minister Anthony Eden was scandalized and, riding a wave of popular indignation, prepared a grotesquely disproportionate response: full scale invasion. Thus, the Suez crisis was the final blow to Britain’s self-image as a world power.
[Question ID: 2014-gs1-q77-unacademy]

## Question 78 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2014-gs1-q78-unacademy**
**Q10. The New Economic Policy – 1921 of Lenin had influenced the policies adopted by India soon after independence. Evaluate. (10 marks, 150 words) History of the world.**

#

## Answer

## **Tag**

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, briefly write about Vladimir** **Lenin’s NEP 1921 and how India’s economy** **took inspiration from that.**-**In Body, substantiate how the New Economic** **Policy 1921 of Lenin influenced the policies** **adopted by India like the Mixed Economic** **model or the capitalist tendency in the** **economy.**-**Here, you can conclude with the historical** **relation of India and Russia. Example:** **Russia’s influence on Indian Economy can** **beseenthroughIndia’sEconomicmodeli.e.,** **Mixed Economy.** **Answer:**The**Kronshtadt Rebellion of March 1921 convinced**Vladimir Lenin of the need to**retreat from socialist** **policies and to introduce The New Economic Policy** **(NEP 1921).**In Independent India, the leaders had to decide the type of economic system most suitable for the nation, a system which would promote the welfare of all rather than a few. The political leaders believed that since**planning was not possible in a market** **economy, they took inspiration from Soviet economy**andcombinedthebestfeaturesofsocialismforIndia’s**Economic model.**Few features of Indian Economy**were inspired by Lenin’s NEP 1921.** **Lenin’s The New Economic Policy, 1921 and its**

## **Influence on India**

- The **New Economic Policy, 1921 partially** **revoked the complete nationalization** of industry which was established during the period of War Communism of 1918 to 1921.
- The **New Economic Policy 1921**introduced a system of**mixed economy** which allowed private individuals to own small enterprises, while continuing to control banks, foreign trade, and large industries.
- In 1926, Jawaharlal Nehru, who later became India’s first Prime Minister, visited the Soviet Union and was deeply impressed by the USSR’s socio-economic pattern. Although he was skeptical of Communism’s emerging political shape, **he was inspired by the Soviet** **economic model,**and tried to take inspiration from their plan. **The Industrial Policy Resolution of 1948** **proposed a mixed economy** which was influenced by the New Economic policy of 1921.
- The **economic measures promulgated as part of** **the NEP brought about significant changes,** in the agricultural sector, the distribution system and in the industrial sector and its organizational forms.
- The second five-year plan (1956-61) took the inspiration from NEP 1921 and laid the foundation for economic modernization.  It was based on the **Mahalanobis Model** **that advocated rapid industrialization** with a focus on heavy industries and capital goods.
- At Indian independence, the first Prime Minister of India, Jawaharlal Nehru helped shape an economy that was substantially modeled after the Soviet template i.e., five-year plans, central planning and a dominant public sector that would assume as Nehru, borrowing Lenin’s phrase, put it **the “commanding heights” of the economy.**Historically, Russia’s influence on Indian economy can be seen through India’s Economic Model i.e.,**Mixed**. The**Economy** **New Economic policy 1921 was the** **mix of Capitalist as well as Command economy,**similarly, India adopted an economic policy which has the combination of capitalism as well as socialism. One of the ambitions of NEP 1921 was to**have a link** **between the rural economy and the urban economy.**In Indian economy,**the five-year plan emphasized on** **establishing linkages between the urban and rural** **areas.** ---

## Question 79 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2013-gs1-q79-unacademy**
**Q11. “‘Latecomer’ Industrial Revolution in Japan involved certain factors that were markedly different from what the West had experienced.” Analyze. (10 marks, 200 words) Tags: History of the world.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to give a brief** **introduction on the background of Japanese** **Industrial Revolution.**-**InBody,mentionhowtheJapaneseIndustrial** **Revolution differs from western Industrial** **Revolution.**-**In Conclusion, try to show that, even being** **a ‘latecomer’, how it helped Japan become a** **global power.** **Answer:**JapanmaderapidstridestoindustrialiseaftertheMeiji**Restoration of 1868,**boosting its transportation and communication networks and revolutionising its light industry by the turn of the century. But the Japanese Industrial Revolution (IR) had many factors that were different from the western Industrial Revolution.**Different Factors Involved in the Japanese IR from**

## **the Western IR**

- Scientificdiscoveriesandinventionswerethefocal points in western IR, whereas the Japanese IR **mostly depended upon the reverse engineering of** **the technologies.** - The western world had a huge number of raw materials and energy resources available to them (Coal, iron, etc.), but Japan had to import the raw materials as they had significantly less resources in comparison.
- Westernindustrialisationhadastrongbackground **of agriculture revolution and commercial** **revolution before the IR,** Japan did not have these advantages before the Industrial Revolution.
- In the case of western industrialisation **finance** **used to come from colonies, landholders, and** **businessmen voluntarily.**In the case of Japanese revolution**finance was forcefully collected from** **the agriculture sector by the state.**Japan**did not** **depend upon colonies** for financial assistance.
- Japanese Industrial Revolution **was state-led,**whereas the western Industrial Revolution was**private sector-led.**- Quality wise**western products were better than** **the Japanese products.**Western products, unlike Japanese products, were sold worldwide. The Japanese products did not find any markets to sell their products outside the country, especially in Europe. Though Japanese Industrial Revolution can be**termed** **as ‘latecomer’,**as it began approximately after three decades of the IR in the west, it can also be termed as**the first among the Asian countries** to raise the flag of the Industrial Revolution. It helped Japan modernise their army and industry to an extent that it could stand toe to toe with the major European powers.

## Question 80 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2013-gs1-q80-unacademy**
**Q12. “Africa was chopped into States artificially created by accidents of European competition.” Analyse. (10 marks, 200 words) Tags: History of the world.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to briefly discuss how** **Africa was colonized and scrambled.**-**In Body, show how different European** **countries played their part in creating** **situations where Africa was chopped into** **States artificially.**-**In conclusion, write about the aftermath of** **this divide and the European competition.** **Answer:**Africa was chopped into states by the Europeans in a mechanism that had no participation of the African States or the African tribes and communities. The current African states are broadly a consequence of colonisation in which**competition and accidents** **playedasignificantrole.Europeanstradedonthecoast,**except in South Africa, and only in the late nineteenth century did they venture into the interior. After this, some**European countries reached an agreement to** **divide up Africa as colonies for themselves.**

## **Factors Leading to the Chopping of African States**

- Franceafter1830,Germanyafter1850andtheU.S. after the Civil War soon started industrialisation. By 1850, the Industrial Revolution had been accomplished, with industries becoming a dominant factor in British life as well. The effect of the Industrial Revolution was felt worldwide.
- Many European countries had by now started following the **Policy of Mercantilism.**Under this policy, governmental control was exercised over industries and trade. It was based on the theory that**more exports and less import indicated** **national strength.**- Africa was**rich in mineral resources, had an** **abundance of cheap/free labour, and it was easy** **to conquer** and hence, the race to acquire the territories began.
- This **race to occupy territory would often result in** **wars,**and the territory would be divided between the victors. War was time consuming and costly, and thus, to avoid wars the Europeans came up with a**strategy to sign agreements and divide the** **territories** between themselves.
- Bytheendofthenineteenthcentury,thechopping **off of the African States was complete.**

## **Division of the African Territories**

- After Belgium captured Congo in 1878, other Europeans met in Berlin to divide the territories within their influence. By the end of the 1870s, France and Britain were already on the march **in** **Western Africa.** Britain colonised Nigeria, France got Morocco, Portugal got Angola, and the U.S. founded Liberia.
- InEasternAfrica,BritaincapturedKenya,German captured Cameroons, French got Madagascar, and Italy received Somalia, Eritrea, Ethiopia. After World War I, Germany was defeated and the areas under its control were divided between the victor powers.
- In Southern Africa, the Dutch settled in South Africa for its strategic location, proximity to ports, and availability of gold. Soon, the Dutch lost to Napoleon, and eventually, it came under British control. An English explorer, Cecil Rhodes, founded Rhodesia (present-day Zimbabwe and Zambia) by exploring and establishing his influence in the region.
- In Northern Africa, the French and the English forces were fighting for territories. Eventually, Algeria and sub-Saharan Africa were under France’s control, the English controlled Egypt and Sudan, and Italy was given Libya and Tunisia. The clear evidence that the division of the African States was through accidents can be seen in **their** **boundaries, which are primarily in straight lines,**as they have been**marked in a paper in Berlin.**The European scramble to partition and occupied African territory is often treated as a peripheral aspect of the**political and economic rivalries** developed between the new industrial nations in Europe itself.

## Question 81 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2013-gs1-q81-unacademy**
**Q13. American Revolution was an economic revolt against mercantilism. Substantiate. (10 marks, 200 words) Tags: History of the world.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to give a brief** **introduction of American Revolution.**-**In Body,**-**Define what mercantilism means.**-**Describe how American Revolution was** **aneconomicrevoltagainstmercantilism.**-**Conclude with highlighting the fact that it** **was a victory of American capitalism over** **mercantilism.** **Answer:**The**American Revolution of 1776**was the greatest phenomenon in the History of Modern Age. This revolution left a**lasting impact**not only on Americans but on the**people throughout the world.** **Mercantilism:**By the middle of the 18th century, thirteen British colonies had emerged in North America. Before the Industrial Revolution, British companies**followed the** **policy of mercantilism**with respect to their colonies.**In mercantilism, a business enterprise that makes** **profit primarily through trade, buying goods cheap** **and selling them at higher prices.**By the middle of the 18th century, the level of development in American colonies was not less than Britain in any way. These colonies had matured in every manner and the American had come of age. Economically the**American colonies were well**

## **advanced in several spheres like**

- Plantation industry was highly developed.
- Many modern industries had also emerged. The American Iron of steel industry was more developed than its British counterpart.
- Trade & Commerce was also quite advanced.
- The resources were in plenty. **American Revolution as an Economic Revolt Against**

## **mercantilism**

- Economically **the Americans were not dependent** **on Britain in any way.**Until 1763, the**burden of** **taxes on Americans was less than being faced by** **British citizens** on the mainland.
- After the economic development in America the British Parliament enacted several new laws **and** **imposed new taxes.** Sugar Act (1764), Currency Act (1764), Stamp Act (1765), Quarter Act (1765), were the examples of the same.
- These new **taxes and the rigid restrictions**were opposed by Americans and the developments that followed triggered revolution. Finally, the**Tea** **policy of Lord North proved to be the last straw** on the camel’s back. It tripped the patience of Americans & the war commenced.
- The **East Indian company was facing a serious** **financial crisis**in 1772-1773. To help this company,PrimeministerLordNorthformulateda**tea policy.**This policy allowed East India company to export its tea directly to America by paying an additional duty. This policy was beneficial to Britain & East India companies, but it**was against** **the interest of Americans.**- SomeoftheidealsoftheRevolutionlikeprotection of private property rights, the Doctrine of Laissezfaire(FreeTrade),judicialsupremacy,meritocracy are**all anti-mercantilist ideals.**After American Revolution, liberal and progressive political system based on the**philosophy of capitalism** **came into existence.**The mercantilist regulation got uprooted. Thus, the American Revolution was an economic revolt and resulted in**the victory of** **capitalism over mercantilism.** ---

## Question 83 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q83-unacademy**
**Q8.Distinguish between gender equality, gender equity and women’s empowerment. Why is it important to take gender concerns into account in programme design and implementation? (150 Words 10 Marks)**

#

## Answer

**Answer:** **Introduction:** **(WHO )** - Gender refers to the characteristics of women, men, girls and boys that are socially constructed.

- This includes norms, behaviours and roles associated with being a woman, man, girl or boy, as well as relationships with each other.
- As a social construct, gender varies from society to society and can change over time. **Difference between Gender Equality, Gender Equity,**

## **and Women’s Empowerment**

## **Gender Equality**

- Concept where men and women have equal access to opportunities, resources, and rights.
- Conceptrootedinliberalfeminism-egalitarianism (Betty Friedman)
- **More focus on ‘de jure ’ equality (sort of equality** **before law)**-**Example:** The Equal Remuneration Act, 1976 in India, Article 15 (1) of the Indian Constitution, Entry of Women in Defence Services

## **Gender Equity**

- Aims to ensure ‘fair treatment’ adjusting for ‘historical imbalances’ (Amartya Sen’s Capability Approach)
- It involves ‘differential treatment’ to achieve equality of outcomes
- **Examples:** Women’s Reservation Act 2023, Article 15(3), Maternity Benefits Act, Special Schemes Sukanya Samridhi Yojna

## **Women’s Empowerment**

- Process of increasing the concept of women to make life decisions
- Focus on agency, autonomy and self-reliance
- Examples: Self Help Group Movement, Beti Bachao-Beti Padhao **Importance of Considering Gender Concerns in**

## **Program Design and Implementation**

- To challenge the societal norms and promote gender equality
- To create equal opportunity for both genders
- To shift the imbalance in the decision-making
- To address the disparity in access to resources
- To improve the effectiveness: make programs relevant for both genders
- Wouldaidinsustainabledevelopmentinalignment with SDGs
- **Economic Argument:** McKinsey Global Institute (MGI), said advancing women’s equality could lead to a $28 trillion increase in the global GDP. For India, this means a potential annual GDP boost of $770 billion by 2025, 18% higher than the expected business-as-usual GDP.
- To achieve Social Justice

## **Conclusion**

- Would aid in inclusive growth

## Question 84 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q84-unacademy**
**Q9.Intercaste marriages between castes which have socio-economic parity have increased, to some extent, but this is less true of interreligious marriages. Discuss. (150 Words 10 Marks)**

#

## Answer

## **Answer**

## **Introduction**

- Inter-caste and inter-religious marriages are indicators of social mobility and cultural integration **Reason for Improvement in the Rate of Inter-caste**

## **Marriages**

- Similar economic status reduces caste consciousness.
- Dilution of caste-based identities, especially in urban settings.
- Modern education and employment lessen caste significance.
- Endogamy often persists due to stark economic differences; it gets reduced in cases of economic equality.

## **Reasons for Relatively less Interreligious Marriages**

- Strong barriers exist due to distinct religious identities which are difficult to dilute.
- Religion is a key regulator of marriage customs.
- Growing religious polarisation discourages interreligious marriages.
- Lower level of social acceptance
- The Special Marriage Act - Provides a legal framework for interreligious marriages but complicates the process.

## **Conclusion**

- Need for greater tolerance, education and social change

## Question 85 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q85-unacademy**
**Q10.In dealing with socio-economic issues of development, what kind of collaboration between government, and private sector would be most productive? (150 Words 10 Marks)**

#

## Answer

## **Answer**

## **Introduction**

- Public-private collaboration is important in addressing socio-economic issues of development.
- **Why:**To enhance resource mobilisation, innovation, and implementation capacities.**Areas Of Productive Collaboration Between**

## **Government, And Private Sector**

- **Infrastructure Development:** Public-Private Partnerships (PPP) model to fund and manage large-scale infrastructure. Example: Highway development in India
- **Healthcare:** Collaboration in healthcare services, ensuring wider access and quality.
- **Example:** Ayushman Bharat with private hospitals providing insurance coverage to low-income groups.
- **Education:** Partnerships to boost skill development, particularly in vocational education.
- Example: Skill India Initiative, with private companies offering skill training programs.
- Technology and Innovation: Private sector expertise in technology combined with government support for innovation hubs.
- Example: Digital India program
- Agriculture and Rural Development: Joint efforts in agri-tech solutions, financial inclusion, and marketing.
- Climate Change: Collaboration in renewable energy projects and sustainable resource management.
- Example: Solar Energy projects in India, with both sectors working on energy expansion.

## **Conclusion**

- Collaborationiskeytoaddressingsocio-economic challenges.

## Question 87 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q87-unacademy**
**Q18.Despite comprehensive policies for equity and social justice, underprivileged sections are not yet getting the full benefits of affirmative action envisaged by the Constitution. Comment. (250 Words 15 Marks)**

#

## Answer

**Answer:** **Introduction**- Affirmative action to promote**equity and social**many underprivileged sections are yet to**justice,** fully benefit.

- Recently, **The Supreme Court**ruled that**sub-** **classification of SCs and STs**is allowed under Articles 14, 15(4), and 16(4), promoting over .**substantive equality** **formal equality**

## **Body**

- The Constitution mandates equity and social justice through policies like reservations for Scheduled Castes (SCs), Scheduled Tribes (STs), and Other Backward Classes (OBCs).
- Actors such as **historical marginalisation,** **implementationgaps,andsocietaldiscrimination** contribute to this issue.
- The caste system historically marginalised Scheduled Castes, denying them education, resources, and basic rights.
- Scheduled Tribes faced social isolation, lack of essential services, and exploitation of land and resources.
- Dalit and Adivasi women face compounded disadvantages of caste, class, gender, experiencing exclusion, exploitation, and violence.

## **Challenges to provide the benefits**

## **Institutional Challenges**

- **Ineffective implementation of affirmative action** **policies**due to bureaucratic**inefficiencies,** **corruption, and political manipulation.**-**Lack of awareness**among the underprivileged sections about their**rights and benefits.**-**Educational and economic barriers**that prevent access to affirmative action programs**(Sachar** **Committee Report)**

## **Structural Gaps in Policy Frameworks**

- **Creamy Layer Exclusion Debate for OBCs:**According to the**National Commission for** **Backward Classes (NCBC),**as of 2021, nearly**60%**of the benefits from OBC reservations go to the**creamy layer,** leaving many economically weaker OBC members without support.
- **Overburdened Welfare Schemes:**Schemes like**MGNREGA, while** well-intentioned, have faced issues like fund misallocation and bureaucratic inefficiencies.
- **Need for Intersectional Policies:The 2019-2020** **Annual Report**from the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment highlighted that marginalised women,particularlyfromSCandSTcommunities, have less than**25%**representation in welfare programs.**Way forward**- Improving**education**and**awareness** programs among underprivileged communities.
- **Strengthening monitoring** mechanisms for better policy implementation.
- Encourage **de-stigmatization campaigns** and affirmative action for societal integration.
- Explore **alternative models** of affirmative action, such as need-based quotas or expanded economic support.

## **Conclusion**

- Reiterate the gap between **policy formulation and** **policy implementation.**- Call for a**multi-dimensional approach**involving**state, society, and the private sector** to ensure that the intended benefits of affirmative action reach the most marginalised.
- Emphasise the need for **constant review** and adjustment of policies to better align with the evolving socio-economic landscape.
