## Question 88 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q88-unacademy**
**Q19.Globalization has increased urban migration by skilled, young, unmarried women from various classes. How has this trend impacted upon their personal freedom and relationship with family? (250 Words 15 Marks)**

#

## Answer

## **Answer**

## **Introduction**

- Globalisation has influenced urban migration patterns, particularly among young, skilled, unmarried women. Factors like better **job** **opportunities, access to education, and social** **freedoms** in urban areas often attract this demographic.
- Urban centres tend to offer more diverse environments where **women can pursue careers** **and personal growth, contributing to shifts in** **family structures and social norms.**- This trend has led to notable changes in**personal** **freedom and family dynamics.** **Body:** **Impact on Personal Freedom**-**Increased** Autonomy:Migration to urban areas provides women with greater opportunities for education and allows them to pursue personal and professionalgoalspreviouslydeemedunattainable.
- **Financial Independence:** Employment in urban settings fosters financial independence.
- **Individual Choice in Partner Selection:** The move from parental choice to individual preference in marriage reflects broader societal changes. Social media platforms have become popular avenues for dating, emphasising personal choice over traditional matchmaking.
- **Changing Marriage Dynamics:**Many women are delaying marriage to focus on their careers.**Impact on Family Relationships**-**Shift from Joint to Nuclear Families:** Rural-urban migration is leading to a decline in the traditional joint family system. This transformation impacts familial support systems and alters intergenerational relationships.
- **Elderly Dependents in Villages:** Elderly family members often remain in villages, managing agricultural activities with assistance from hired labourers or younger relatives.
- **Skewed Sex Ratios:**Male migrants frequently leave their wives behind in villages due to high living costs in cities, contributing to skewed sex ratios. The**2011 Census**reported a sex ratio of**919 females for every 1,000 males** in India, exacerbating societal issues related to gender imbalance.
- **Single Parenthood and Non-Traditional Families:**Theriseofsingle-parentfamiliesandacceptanceof various family structures indicate shifting norms and increasing flexibility in personal life choices.**Change in Employment Patterns and Family Systems**-**Separation of Family Members:** Changes in employment patterns often lead to family separation. For instance, if one son secures a white-collar job in the city, the remaining family members experience shifts in dynamics and support structures.
- **Impact on Agricultural Households:.**As younger members migrate for work, reliance on family support diminishes, impacting farming practices and community ties.**Rising costs related to** **education and healthcarealso lead to apreference** **for smaller families.** **Conclusion** - The combined effects of globalization, migration, and changing employment patterns have profoundly transformed India’s traditional family structure.
- There is a growing inclination toward nuclear families with fewer members, in contrast to the previously larger joint families.
- Additionally, the preference for sons has skewed sex ratios in these smaller family units. Overall, these trends indicate significant changes in both personal freedom for women and the nature of familial relationships in contemporary India.
[Question ID: 2024-gs1-q88-unacademy]

## Question 89 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2024-gs1-q89-unacademy**
**Q20.Critically analyse the proposition that there is a high correlation between India’s cultural diversities and socio-economic marginalities. (250 Words 15 Marks)**

#

## Answer

## **Answer**

## **Introduction**

- India is described as the **“Melting pot of Cultures”.**-**Ethnic diversity, Linguistic diversity**(over 1600 languages are & dialects are used),**Religious** **diversity**(home to major religions of the world),**Regional diversity, Socio-economic diversity** (as there’s stark difference in one’s social & economic condition).
- In India, **socio-economic marginalisation**is deeply interrelated with factors like caste, religion, gender, ethnicity, and geography.**Eg: Dalits and** **Adivasis face exclusion due to the caste system,** **which restricts access to education, jobs, and** **social mobility.**-**Sachar Committee Report (2006):**Marginalised groups like SCs, STs, and Muslims face higher poverty, illiteracy, and poor healthcare, reflecting how cultural diversity links to socio-economic marginality.**Body:** **High Correlation between Cultural Diversities and**

## **Socio-Economic Marginalities**

## **Social Dimension**

- **NITI Aayog’s Multidimensional Poverty Index** **(2023)** indicated high incidences of poverty among SC, STs.
- **Intersecting Identities:** Many individuals belong to multiple marginalised groups simultaneously. For example, a Dalit woman faces discrimination not only based on her caste but also her gender.
- **Marginalised groups face barriers to quality** **education, perpetuating low-paying, insecure** **jobs, and deepening poverty.**-**Marginalised communities have limited access to** **healthcare, leading to higher mortality rates and** **lower life expectancy.**

## **Political dimension**

- Example: Only 7 women ministers in present Union council of ministers & no Muslim minister in present Union government.
- **Sachar Committee Report (2006)** highlighted underrepresentationofmarginalisedcommunities like Muslims in govt jobs.

## **Economic dimension**

- **Adivasis suffer from land dispossession, while** **both Adivasis and Dalits have high rates of** **poverty and landlessness, worsening their** **economic vulnerability.**-**Dalit Capitalism: The emergence of Dalit** **capitalism—where SC entrepreneurs seek** **economic empowerment—has faced significant** **challenges.**-**Xaxa Committee**for Tribal population in India: They lack adequate political representation,**economicdeprivationandculturaldiscrimination.**-**Example:** ‘Report of the Expert Committee on Tribal Health’ by Ministry of Tribal Affairs states that as per Census, 2011 over two-thirds of the tribal population is working in the primary sector (as against 43% of the non-tribal population).

## **Historical dimension**

- **The Purdah system** limited mobility & access to resources for women.
- **TheBritishcolonialpolicies,suchastheZamindari** **system,** disproportionately marginalised tribal and lower-caste communities.

## **Environmental Dimension**

- **Climate Change:** Marginalised communities, especially Adivasis and those living in coastal areas, are particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.

## **Technological dimension**

- **Digital Divide:** As the world becomes increasingly digitised, access to technology and digital literacy has become crucial for socio economic progress.

## **Affirmative actions to end the Correlation**

## **Social Mobility and Empowerment Programs**

- **ScheduledCasteandScheduledTribe(Prevention** **of Atrocities) Act, 1989,** coupled with reservations in education and jobs, has led to increased literacy and employment rates among SCs and STs.
- **National Legal Services Authority (NALSA): free** **legal aid for poor & vulnerable sections of society.**

## **Economic Growth and Inclusive Development**

- Schemes like **PM Jan Dhan Yojana**and**MGNREGS** have expanded financial inclusion, with a significant proportion of beneficiaries from marginalised communities.
- **Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan**to promote universal education, and the**Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana** to ensure housing for all.

## **Political Representation and Affirmative Action**

- Recently passed **Nari Shakti Vandana Adhiniyam** to increase political representation of women.
- Extension of Reservation for SC/STs in Parliament for additional 10 years by **104th Constitutional** **Amendment Act, 2019.**-**Articles 15 and 17 of the Indian Constitution** attempt to redress these social inequities by prohibiting discrimination and untouchability.

## **Technological Interventions in Bridging Gaps**

- Examples: **Digital India**initiatives, such as**DigiLocker, Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT)** **through Jan Dhan Accounts**and**Aadhaar,** have improved access to government services for marginalised groups.
- **e-Shram, a digital platform** for unorganised workers,hasenabledSCs,STs,andruralminorities to access social security benefits, illustrating technology’s role in addressing marginality.

## **Conclusion**

- **Although affirmative action (reservations) exists,** **issues like corruption and poor implementation** **hinder the effectiveness of these policies. Also** **policies of the government aimed to reduce** **marginalities however failed to address the** **cultural biases.**-**Dr. B.R. Ambedkar** advocated for constitutional safeguards to address these inequalities.
- In the words of **Amartya Sen,**“Development is about expanding the real freedoms that people enjoy.”**ANSWERS AND EXPLANATION** ---

## Question 90 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2023-gs1-q90-unacademy**
**Q9. Explain why suicide among young women is increasing in Indian society. (10 Marks, 150 Words)**

#

## Answer

## **Approach**

- **Introduction:** Define suicide in sociological terms and support with data on rising trends.
- **Body:** Analyze multiple social, cultural, economic, and psychological causes behind suicides among young women.
- **Conclusion:**Link the crisis to lack of social integration and stress the need for support systems.**Answer:** **Introduction:**Durkheim defined suicide as an act resulting from the victim’s own behaviour, knowing its outcome. In India, suicides among young women have become a grave concern. According to the NCRB, 13,121 women died by suicide in 2020, accounting for 15.3% of total suicides, with the highest incidence among those aged 15–29. A Lancet study further revealed that India accounted for 37% of global female suicide deaths in 2016, reflecting the scale of the crisis.**Reasons for Increasing Suicides among Young** **Women in India**-**Dowry Harassment:** Abuse and torture by husbands and in-laws remain a major trigger.
- **Mental Health Issues:** Depression, anxiety, and other disorders, combined with lack of awareness and stigma around mental health, heighten risks.
- **Gender Discrimination:** Limited access to education, employment, and autonomy fosters despair and feelings of entrapment.
- **Societal Expectations:** Pressure to conform to traditional roles, marriage, and family duties creates stress and inadequacy.
- **Abuse and Violence:** Physical, sexual, and emotional abuse severely affects psychological well-being.
- **Lack of Support Systems:** Absence of empathy, counseling centers, or peer/family support worsens isolation.
- **Academic Pressure:** Young students face intense exam stress, contributing to self-harm.
- **MaritalDisharmony:Conflicts,financialdisputes,** and breakdown of relationships push many, especially housewives, toward suicide.
- **Victim Shaming:** Survivors of sexual violence often face social stigma, deepening trauma.
- **Relationship Failures:** Breakups, adultery, and mistrust are significant stressors, as NCRB notes.
- **Rising Consumerism:** Aspirations linked to affluence often generate dissatisfaction and depression.
- **Upbringing Styles:**Overprotective or neglectful parenting may foster low frustration tolerance and emotional instability.**Conclusion:** As Durkheim emphasised, weak social integration fuels suicides. In Indian society, young women often lack social and institutional support due to entrenched gender discrimination. To address this crisis, stronger family systems, accessible mental health care, gendersensitive policies, and societal empathy are imperative.
[Question ID: 2023-gs1-q90-unacademy]

## Question 92 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2023-gs1-q92-unacademy**
**Q11. What are the main features of Vedic society and religion? Do you think some of the features are still prevailing in Indian society? (15 Marks, 250 Words)**

#

## Answer

## **Approach**

- **Introduction:** Briefly define the Vedic period and its socio-religious importance.
- **Body:** Describe key features of Vedic religion and society, linking them to present-day continuities.
- **Conclusion:**Conclude by highlighting the enduring relevance of Vedic ideals in modern Indian culture.**Answer:** **Introduction:**The Vedic period (1500 BCE – 600 BCE) was a formativestageinIndianhistory,layingthefoundations of religion, society, polity, and culture. Unlike other ancient civilizations such as Mesopotamia, Egypt, or Greece, the traditions of the Vedic age have continued with modifications, leaving a lasting imprint on Indian society.**Body:** **Main Features of Vedic Religion**-**Worship of Nature:** Deities like Indra (rain), Agni (fire), and Surya (sun) were worshipped. This persists today in practices like sun worship, river festivals (Chhath), and tree worship.
- **Yajnas:** Sacrificial rituals performed in open spaces for prosperity and welfare. Yajnas are still practiced in Hindu households and temples.
- **Polytheism leading to Monism:** Though polytheistic, the Rig Veda reflects the idea of an underlying supreme reality (“Ekam Sat”). Hinduism retains this diversity of deities within a broader spiritual unity.
- **Prayers and Hymns:** Recitation of mantras and hymns formed the essence of worship. The tradition of chanting Vedic hymns remains integral to rituals.
- **Oral Tradition:**Vedic knowledge was preserved orally, ensuring continuity. UNESCO has recognized Vedic chanting as Intangible Cultural Heritage, showing its survival today.**Main Features of Vedic Society**-**Varna System:** Initially occupation-based (Brahmins, Kshatriyas, Vaishyas, Shudras), it later ossified into a rigid caste system, still a reality in Indian society.
- **Position of Women:** Women enjoyed relatively high status in early Vedic society—educated, participated in assemblies, and had freedom in marriage. This autonomy declined in later Vedic times, but debates on women’s empowerment continue in modern India.
- **Marriage Practices:** Predominantly monogamous, though polygyny/polyandry existed. Marriage as a sacred institution remains central in Indian society.
- **Kinship and Gotra System:** Clan-based identity was strong, evolving into the Gotra tradition, which continues in Hindu marriage norms.
- **Tribal Chiefdom and Assemblies:** Kings (Rajan) were often elected by tribal assemblies like the Samiti. This reflects an early democratic ethos, echoed in India’s democratic polity today.
- **Social Differences:**The conquered dasas and dasyus were marginalized, forming the basis of caste hierarchies. This legacy persists in the form of caste-based divisions and Dalit identities.**Conclusion:** Swami Dayanand Saraswati’s call to “Go Back to the Vedas” underlines their timeless influence. The Vedic period’s emphasis on spirituality, community, oral traditions, and social organization continues to shape Indian society. Its legacies—both positive (cultural richness, rituals, philosophy) and negative (caste hierarchies, social stratification)—still resonate, making the Vedic age a living foundation of India’s civilizational identity.
[Question ID: 2023-gs1-q92-unacademy]

## Question 93 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2023-gs1-q93-unacademy**
**Q18. Does urbanization lead to more segregation and/or marginalization of the poor in Indian metropolises? (15 Marks, 250 Words)**

#

## Answer

## **Approach**

- **Introduction:** Define urbanization and briefly note its social impact.
- **Body:** Discuss how urbanization often marginalizes the poor but also provides opportunities for mobility; include counterpoints.
- **Conclusion:**Emphasize the need for inclusive and sustainable urban planning.**Answer:** **Introduction:**Urbanization is the process of population shift from rural to urban areas, accompanied by industrial growth, economic transition, and modernization. While it creates opportunities for social mobility and cultural amalgamation, it also generates challenges such as housing shortages, environmental strain, and social inequalities, particularly affecting the poor.**Impact of Urbanization on Poor People**-**Slum Formation:** Unplanned growth leads to large slums with limited access to sanitation, water, and electricity.
- **Gentrification and Ghettoization:** Rising real estate prices displace poorer residents and create socio-economic enclaves.
- **Infrastructure Gap:** Affluent areas receive better facilities, while marginalized neighborhoods remain underserved.
- **Employment Disparities:** Poor populations often remain confined to informal, insecure, and lowwage work.
- **Limited Social Integration:** Distinct socioeconomic groups interact minimally, reinforcing stereotypes.
- **Access to Education & Health:** Quality services cluster in rich areas, leaving the poor with inadequate facilities.
- **Environmental Injustice:** Marginalized localities face higher exposure to pollution and poor waste management.
- **Policy Neglect:** Urban policies may prioritize beautification over basic services for the poor.
- **Civic Participation Gap:**Marginalized groups often have limited voice in urban planning.**Counter-Points**-**Social Mobility:** Urbanization fosters economic inclusion and upward mobility.
- **Government Initiatives:** Schemes like PM-AWAS and skill development aim to integrate the urban poor.
- **Increased Social Visibility:** Media and NGOs highlight marginalization, prompting interventions.
- **Networking and Collective Bargaining:** Dense urban living aids labor unions and associations in demanding better conditions.
- **Poverty Reduction:**As per the Global MultidimensionalPovertyIndex2023,415million people moved out of poverty in India between 2005/06 and 2019/21, with incidence falling from 55.1% to 16.4%.**Conclusion:** Urbanization in Indian metropolises often results in segregation and marginalization of the poor, yet it also offers avenues for empowerment and mobility. The way forward lies in planned and inclusive urbanization aligned with Sustainable Development Goal 11, ensuring “sustainable cities and communities” where growth and equity go hand in hand.

## Question 94 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2023-gs1-q94-unacademy**

- **Conclusion:**Emphasize the need to balance changewithinclusionforanequitablesociety.**Answer:** **Introduction:**Caste identity in India refers to the placement of individuals and groups within a hierarchical social order. It has historically influenced access to resources, occupations,andsocialstatus.Whilemodernprocesses have challenged rigid structures, caste identity still persists in many spheres of life.**Why Caste Identities are Fluid**-**Economic Opportunities:** Urbanization and industrialization enable movement beyond castebased occupations.
- **Migration:** Relocation to urban centers dilutes rigid caste distinctions due to diverse interactions.
- **Education:** Modern education fosters meritocracy and enables mobility irrespective of caste.
- **Inter-caste Marriages:** Rising inter-caste unions blend identities and weaken rigid boundaries.
- **Syncretic Traditions:** Shared religious and cultural practices blur caste lines.
- **Political** **Mobilization:** Caste coalitions during elections merge identities for broader representation.
- **GlobalizationandWesternInfluence:Exposureto** global cultures reduces adherence to caste norms, especially among youth.
- **Social Activism:**Anti-discrimination movements promote equality and reduce caste-based barriers.**Why Caste Identities are Static**-**Historical Legacy:** Deep-rooted customs maintain caste hierarchies.
- **Ritualistic Practices:** Caste-specific rituals reinforce continuity of identities.
- **Political Representation:** Caste-based politics and reservations sometimes strengthen identities.
- **Endogamy:** Social pressure to marry within caste maintains boundaries.
- **Economic Disparities:** Certain castes dominate particular economic sectors, sustaining hierarchies.
- **Media and Popular Culture:** Perpetuation of caste stereotypes strengthens static images.
- **Caste-based Violence:** Discrimination and atrocities reveal entrenched caste perceptions.
- **Community Support Systems:**Caste-based associations provide security and networking, encouraging continuity.**Conclusion:** CasteinIndiaisbothfluidandstatic,shapedbymodern forces of change but also reinforced by traditions and politics. The challenge lies in ensuring caste does not remain a barrier to development, by building an inclusive and equitable social framework.

## Question 95 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2023-gs1-q95-unacademy**
**Q20. Discuss the impact of the post-liberal economy on ethnic identity and communalism. (15 Marks, 250 Words)**

#

## Answer

## **Approach**

- **Introduction:** Briefly explain post-liberal economy and its broad social effects.
- **Body:** Analyze how liberalization reshaped ethnic identities and influenced communalism with examples.
- **Conclusion:**Suggest inclusive strategies to balance growth with social harmony.**Answer:** **Introduction:**The post-liberal economy refers to the framework that emerged in India after the 1991 economic reforms, marked by liberalization, privatization, and globalization. While it brought economic growth and global integration, it also reshaped social structures, influencing both ethnic identity and communalism.**Impact on Ethnic Identity**-**Media and Cultural Exposure:** Global media inflow diluted some local traditions while also enabling ethnic cultures to showcase themselves worldwide.
- **Migration:** Urbanization led to intermixing of communities, sometimes diluting ethnic distinctions but also fostering hybrid cultural identities.
- **Commodification:** Ethnic symbols and festivals were marketed for tourism and consumerism, preserving them but often in commercialized forms.
- **Economic Mobility and Aspiration:** Rising middle-class aspirations encouraged global lifestyles, reducing emphasis on traditional markers of ethnic identity.
- **Revival of Traditional Arts:** Growing prosperity enabled renewed pride in folk art, craft, and traditions, seen in state-supported cultural festivals.
- **New Markets and Consumerism:**Global brands appropriated ethnic motifs, sparking both cultural pride and concerns of distortion.**Impact on Communalism**-**Resource Competition:** Economic growth created unequal access to jobs and resources, often perceived along communal lines (e.g., Sachar Committee findings on Muslims).
- **Political Mobilization:** Leaders used communal rhetoric to mobilize votes amidst rising inequality and dislocation.
- **Global Religious Movements:** Liberalization increased exposure to transnational religious movements, reshaping communal alignments.
- **Inequalities and Marginalization:** Benefits of liberalization were uneven, creating resentment among marginalized groups and reinforcing communal divides.
- **Urbanization:** Cities became melting pots of communities, fostering both coexistence and conflicts (e.g., Mumbai riots of the 1990s).
- **Preservation vs. Modernization:** Tensions grew between traditional practices and modern globalized lifestyles, intensifying communal sentiments.
- **Information Technology:**Social media amplified communal narratives, both bridging gaps and fueling polarization.**Conclusion:**The post-liberal economy has deeply influenced ethnic identity by reshaping traditions and communalism by intensifying both integration and division. Going forward, inclusive economic structures and equitable social policies are essential to ensure that globalization strengthens pluralism rather than fragmenting society.**ANSWERS AND EXPLANATION** ---

## Question 96 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2022-gs1-q96-unacademy**
**Q8. Explore and evaluate the impact of ‘Work From Home’ on family relationships. (150 Words, 10 Marks) Approach**

#

## Answer

- **Introduction:** Begin by highlighting the shift to the ‘Work From Home’ (WFH) model and its potential impact.
<p align="center"><img src="images/unacademy_p181_img79.png" alt="Diagram" /></p>

- **Body:** Discuss both the positive impacts and the negative impacts in detail.
- **Conclusion:**Conclude with an acknowledgment of the need for supportive interventions.**Answer:** **Introduction:** The widespread adoption of ‘Work From Home’ (WFH) during the COVID-19 pandemic and its continued practice in various industries have significantly impacted family relationships. This exploration of WFH’s impacts uncovers both encouraging and challenging outcomes.

## **Body**

## **Positive Impacts**

- **Strengthened Family Bonding:** WFH enables employees to spend more time at home, fostering deeper connections and understanding among familymembers.Thishaspotentiallystrengthened various relationships within the family, including parent-child and spousal ties.
- **Shared Responsibilities:** WFH has blurred the line between professional and personal lives, offering an increased opportunity for shared household tasks, which can promote gender equality in domestic responsibilities.
- **Care for Dependents:** The physical presence at home allows employees to attend promptly to family needs. This is particularly beneficial for thosewithchildcareoreldercareduties,potentially reversing the trend of increasing registrations in old-age homes in India.
- WFH promotes healthier **Healthy Lifestyle:** habits, such as rest and home-cooked meals. In some cases, it may also restrict detrimental habits like smoking and drinking, which are often not practiced at home in India.
- **ReducedCommuteStress:Theeliminationofdaily** commuting reduces stress and fatigue, fostering a more relaxed and productive work environment.

## **Negative Impacts**

- **Work-Life Imbalance:** The blurred boundaries between professional and personal life can extend work hours, leading to reduced quality family time and causing stress and strain in relationships.
- **Invasion of Privacy:** The constant intrusion of privacy due to work calls and meetings at home can cause frustration and potentially lead to conflicts within the family.
- **Mental Exhaustion:** The increase in remote meetings and rigid deadlines to ensure accountability can cause mental exhaustion, affecting familial relationships negatively.
- **Distractions:** The home environment, while comforting, can also present distractions like TV, personal work, or childcare, affecting work quality.
- **Instances of Domestic Violence:** There have been some reports suggesting an increase in domestic violence, as work-related stress spills over into familial relationships.
- **Economic Stress:**WFH has also led to job losses and income instability in some sectors, causing economicstressthatcanstrainfamilyrelationships.**Conclusion:** The WFH presents opportunities for increased family bonding, shared responsibilities, and healthier lifestyles, but at the same time, it also poses significantchallengeslikework-lifeimbalance,privacy invasion,mentalexhaustion,andeconomicstress.Both employers and policymakers must take these factors into account to develop supportive interventions that can leverage the benefits and mitigate the adverse effects of WFH on family relationships.

The expanded reach of e-commerce and ride-hailing services underpins theshifttowardsamore consumer-centriclifestyle in these areas.**Conclusion:** The interplay of a rising middle class and the growth of Tier 2 cities, stimulated by changing consumption patterns and technological advancements, situates these cities as potential powerhouses for India’s future economic growth. The symbiosis between these elements serves as a driving force in reshaping India’s economic and social fabric, setting the stage for a prosperous future.

## **category**

Moreover,**certain laws like the Scheduled Castes and** **Scheduled Tribes (Prevention of Atrocities) Act,** **1989,** apply uniformly to all tribal communities.
- **Socio-economic Marginalization:**Despite their diversity,manytribalcommunitiessharecommon**experiences of socio-economic Marginalization.** Theyoftenranklowerthanothergroupsonvarious socio-economic indicators, including literacy rates, health outcomes, and access to resources. Considering them as** **a single category is crucial**in the context of land rights, as well as efforts to conserve forests and biodiversity. This is highlighted in legislation like the Forest Rights Act, of 2006, which recognizes the collective rights of tribal communities over forest lands.**Conclusion:** While these are broad contexts where consideringtribalcommunitiesasasinglegroupmight be appropriate, it is crucial to remember that a onesize-fits-all approach might not be effective given the immense diversity among these communities. ## Question 99 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2022-gs1-q99-unacademy**
**Q18. Analyse the salience of ‘sect’ in Indian society vis-a-vis caste, region and religion. (250 Words, 15 Marks) Approach**

#

## Answer

- **Introduction:** Introduce the answer with the fact that the sect is a social institution.

## **Body**

- Highlight the Sect and Caste
- Highlight the Sect and Region
- Highlight the Sect and Religion
- **Conclusion:**Conclude the answer by mentioning that understanding sect dynamics is crucial for building a pluralistic and inclusive society.**Answer** **Introduction:** The sect is a collection of practises and doctrines centered on a concept of the sacred. Given that any religion’s foundation is its community of adherents, the sect is a social institution. Because religion has a hierarchy of gods, priests, prophets, and adherents, it is an organization that allows for variety to be accommodated.

## **Sect and Caste**

- Socioeconomicinequalityisfrequentlymaintained by the intersectionality of caste and sect. There is already discrimination since some religions are historically linked to particular castes.
- Within the upper caste groups, sects form that are not materially poor but feel spiritually deprived. For example, the Hare Krishna Movement.

## **Sect and Region**

- Indicates that the notion of continual change is fundamental to Indian society today. Indian society is the outcome of the adoption of various sects in various areas.
- It has undergone numerous changes along the way, influenced by both social reform movements within India (Sikhism) and those from outside the country (Islam). For instance, in the state of Punjab, the Sikh community is divided into different sects, such as the Nanakpanthis and the Nirmalas, each with its own practices and beliefs.
- NumerouscastesandsectsmakeupIndiansociety. Based on the culture they adhered to and the identity of their concept of God, people organized themselves into different faiths.
- A caste group might be considered its own sect if its members share identical customs, beliefs, and rituals.

## **Sect and Religion**

- Sects within bigger religious groupings in India have a huge impact on the religious landscape by providing a variety of beliefs and practices.
- As an illustration of the flexibility of Hinduism, consider how sects like Vaishnavism and Shaivism coexistpeacefullydespitehavingdifferentpractices and gods. **Conclusion:** Along with caste, region, and religion, sects are important in Indian society. ## Question 100 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2022-gs1-q100-unacademy**
**Q19. Are tolerance, assimilation and pluralism the key elements in the making of an Indian form of secularism? Justify your answer. (250 Words, 15 Marks) Approach**

#

## Answer

- **Introduction:** Write an introduction about the hindustani way of life.
-

## **Body**

- Mention a few points about tolerance.
- Discuss about assimilation cultures in India.
- Discuss the pluralist acceptance of diversity in India.
- Conclude the answer by **Conclusion:**mentioning the tolerance, assimilation, and pluralism continue to be crucial components of Indian secularism.**Answer** **Introduction:**Centered around the tenets of Dharma Nirapekshata and Sarv Dharm Samabhav, it respects all religions equally. It does not strive for a strict division of church and state. Indian culture is a syncretic tradition because many religions have interacted in areas where they are shared, such as folklore, culinary patterns, clothing, and lifestyle. The medieval era saw a rise in the popularity of Persian, Arabic, and Urdu. During this time, Indian culture changed, paving the way for the distinctive “Hindustani” way of life.**Body:** **Tolerance:-** It is the capacity to tolerate and accept the differences among those who adhere to other cultures, faiths, and ideologies.
- Tolerance enables people to coexist peacefully without worrying about prejudice or retaliation. Not only must we accept those who hold various ideas, but we must also recognise and value the diversity of Indian society.
- ThePreamble,whichdescribesIndiaasasovereign, socialist, secular, and democratic republic, pledges tolerance to be the cornerstone of the Indian Constitution.
- All people are afforded equality before the law and equal protection under the law under **Article 14.**- Discrimination on the basis of religion, ethnicity, caste, sex, or place of birth is forbidden by**Article** **15.**- Minorities have access to cultural and educational rights under**Articles 29 and 30.** **Assimilation:-** It is the process by which individuals from many cultures, religions, and philosophical perspectives unite to create a shared sense of national identity.
- India’s past is rife with instances of assimilation, where members of many cultures and religions merged to create a unique Indian identity. Indian food, a fusion of different regional cuisines, is one of the best examples of absorption.
- The amalgamation of various cultures to forge an original Indian identity also gave rise to Indian music, dance, and literature.
- ‘Freedom of Conscience’ is guaranteed by **Article**
**25.**Everyone has the freedom to profess, practice, and spread their faith.**Pluralism:-Itistheacceptanceofdiversityinthesocial**environment. India is a multireligious, multilingual, and multicultural nation.

- Pluralism accepts and acknowledges this diversity while giving all groups an equal chance to succeed.
- Minority communities are protected by the Indian Constitution, which guarantees their rights and opportunity equality. Diversity should be celebrated as part of pluralism, which goes beyond simply accepting it.
- Every religious group or individual has the right,**under Article 26,**to create and operate institutions for religious and charitable reasons and to control its own internal religious affairs.**Conclusion:**Tolerance, assimilation, and pluralism continue to be crucial components of Indian secularism, fostering inter-communal harmony and giving all communities a sense of belonging.

## Question 101 (Year: 2022 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2022-gs1-q101-unacademy**
**Q20. Elucidate the relationship between globalization and new technology in a world of scarce resource, with special reference to India. (250 Words, 15 Marks) Approach**

#

## Answer

-**Introduction:**Write an introduction by mentioning the definition given by the WHO.

##**y Body**

- Mention the examples of technological globalization
- Highlights the globalization’s positive and negative impacts over the world.
- Discuss the positive and negative impacts of globalization on India.
-**Conclusion:**Write a suitable conclusion.**Answer** **Introduction:** According to WHO, globalization can be defined as ” theincreasedinterconnectednessandinterdependence of peoples and countries.

- Itisgenerallyunderstoodtoincludetwointerrelated elements: the opening of international borders to increasingly fast flows of goods, services, finance, people and ideas; and the changes in institutions and policies at national and international levels that facilitate or promote such flows.”

## **Body**

## **Example of Technological Globalization**

- **Growth** **of** **Multinational** **Technology** **Corporations:-**Liberalization of trade and economic globalization have facilitated the growth of multinational corporations**like Microsoft,** **Apple and Sony.**-**Mobile Banking:-** Mobile banking has allowed people to access money both at home and around the world at a faster pace than ever.
- **Mechanization of Manufacturing:-** Using machines for manufacturing lowers labor costs which have two competing effects: downward pressure on labor costs (leading to cheaper goods) and loss of traditional manufacturing jobs.
- **Media Globalization:-**One effect of the spread of technologies and technological knowledge (primarily from the developed to the developing world) has been to also globalize the media that is presented on technologies like computers and cell phones.**In a world where resources are scarce, globalization** **and new technology have a complex interaction** **that has an impact on resource availability and** **distribution, both positively and negatively.**

## **Positive Impact**

- Globalisation and new technologies can aid in improving resource usage efficiency and lowering the demand for specific resources. Examples of new technologies that **can assist** **to lessen the dependency on non-renewable** **resources include fossil fuels and renewable** **energy sources.**- Globalisation**can improve resource use** **efficiency**by enabling nations to specialise in the production of particular resources and tradethemwithothernations.Ascountriesare able to get the resources they require through trade rather than depending on domestic production,thiscanhelptolowerthedemand**for resources as a whole.**

## **Negative Impact**

- The availability and distribution of resources may be negatively impacted by globalisation and new technologies. For instance, **rising** **demand for particular resources, like land** **and water, can result in resource depletion** **and environmental deterioration.**- Additionally,**unequal resource allocation** **has the potential to worsen already**existing disparities and spark disputes over resource access.**Globalisation and new technology have both** **positive and negative effects on the distribution and**

## **availability of resources in India**

- **Positive:-**- By implementing new technology, such as renewable energy sources,**(For example**
**- International Solar Alliance)**India has made considerable strides towards improving resource usage efficiency and lowering the demand for specific resources.
-**Through the use of technology, EdTech** **platforms like Unacademy** democratise education and lessen the need for expensive physical infrastructure.

- Agrotech businesses **like DeHaat and** **AgroStar employ technology** to boost crop yields and resource management in order to address problems like soil and water scarcity.
- **Engagement in defense** with international powers and partners like Israel.
- Global **cooperation in space** with nations including the United States and France.
- **Negative:-**- India also has enormous issues with**resource** **depletion, environmental degradation,** **unequal resource distribution,** and other related issues.
- Workers in traditional sectors may be replaced as a result of rapid technological progress. For instance, **factory automation may result in** **employment losses.**- Globalised consumer goods have the potential to**increase waste and overconsumption,**stressing already**limited resources.**-**Dataprivacyissueshavealsobeenexacerbated** by globalisation. The risk of data breaches rises as user data is stored by international computer businesses.
- **Social inequality**is made worse by the fact that rural and economically underprivileged people have less access to digital education and e-commerce.**Conclusion:**It is critical that India establish policies and practises that would maximise the positive effects of globalisation and the use of new technology while minimising their negative effects in order to meet these concerns.**ANSWERS AND EXPLANATION** ---

## **Body**

Only oral tradition has been used to transmit knowledge from one generation tothenext,withtheyoungergenerationplayingamajor role in this process. These insights go by the names of mental objects or indigenous knowledge.

## **ReasonsWhytheTribalKnowledgeSystemisUnique**

- They **depend on local resources** and practice cautious interactions between living beings and natural processes within an ecosystem in order to protect human life.
- Since everyone in a tribal community shares the traditional understandings, they might be thought of as the **Knowledge Keepers.** They are sometimes referred to as the protectors of the nature as the tribesmen specialise in the preservation of traditional information.
- The **gender ratio and the proportion of women** in thelabourforcearebetterthanthenationalaverage, according to census 2011 data. Additionally, polyandry was practised by a number of tribal civilizations, like the Todas tribes of the Nilgiris, which elevates the status of women.

- **Resources are exploited** beyond the capacity of sustainability.

Even if there is interaction between tribal and mainstream culture, it is**important to protect and** **conserve tribal knowledge,**as well as to involve them in disaster relief and policymaking, to make India**truly inclusive in both nature and spirit.** ---

## Question 103 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2021-gs1-q103-unacademy**
**Q18. Discuss the main objectives of Population Education and point out the measures to achieve them in India in detail. (15 Marks, 250 Words) Tags: Population and Associated Issues**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **Introduction: Define Population Education** **and provide some statistics related to it.** -

## **Body**

- **Mention the main objectives of** **Population Education.**-**Highlight the measures to achieve** **Population Education in India.**-**Conclusion: Conclude with highlighting the** **importance of Population Education.** **Answer:** Population Education covers any or all matters related to population that influence(s) development, standard of living and quality of life. The concept of Population Education has gradually evolved by adapting itself to the changing needs, requirements and emerging issues and concerns. According to the UN Department of Economic and Social Affairs estimates, India’s population will reach 1.5 billion by 2030 and hit 1.64 billion in 2050.

## **Main Objectives of Population Education**

- Todevelopanunderstandingofsomedemographic concepts and processes.
- To develop among the younger generation an understanding of the most important phenomenon of the modern world viz., rapid growth of population and its causes.
- To develop an understanding of the influence of population trends on the various aspects of human life - social, cultural, political and economic.
- To develop an understanding of the close interaction of population growth and the developmentalprocesswithparticularreferenceto development programmes for raising the standard of living of people.
- To develop an understanding of the evil effects of over-population on the environment and the concomitant dangers from pollution.
- To develop an understanding of scientific and medicaladvancementenablingtogetanincreasing controloverfamines,diseasesandultimatelydeath and the imbalance thus created between death rate and birth rate.
- To develop an understanding of biological factors and phenomenon of reproduction which are responsible for continuance of the species.

## **To develop an appreciation of**

- Thesmallfamilynorm;asproperanddesirable.
- The relation between population size and the quality of life.
- Thefactthatfamilysizeisamatterofdeliberate choice and human regulation rather than of accident or forces beyond human control.
- Todevelopanattitudeofresponsibilityandmutual help and cooperation in all aspects of personal and family living.
- To develop an appreciation of the relationship between the preservation of the health of the mother, the welfare of the children and the small size of the family.
- To develop an awareness of population policies and programmes of the country.
- To provide students with a basic demographic vocabulary so that they are able to read and interpret demographic material with some understanding. **Measures to Achieve the Objectives of Population**

## **Education in India**

- India is the first nation in the world to have a government-sponsored population policy that encourages family planning to reduce the number of families.
- Initiatedin1952duringtheFirstFiveYearPlan (1951– 1956), the National Family Planning Programme employed a “clinical” approach to family planning and created a number of clinics with the hope that the public would utilise the resources.
- The ‘cafeteria approach’ in which various alternative means of family limitation (temporary and permanent) were propagated from the middle of 1960s also did not make much headway as the official emphasis remained on terminal methods alone.
- Later, many legislations were brought out, including on abortion (medical termination of pregnancy) and the age of marriage. ‘Incentives and disincentives’ scheme was also introduced.
- Consequently, a comprehensive National Population Policy integrated Population Education.
- Concept and Development with the overall strategy of socio-economic development was evolvedinApril1976withaviewtopromoting family planning at a faster pace by involving in the programme the other development departments of the Government, both at the Centre and in the States.
- The National Population Policy (2000) provided a framework for the following ten years in a number of areas for population development, with the aim of enhancing people’s quality of life, improving their well-being, and giving them opportunities and choices to become valuable contributors to society.
- By 2045, it hopes to stabilise the population at a level that satisfies the demands of social progress, environmentally sound development, and sustainable economic growth.
- An extensive, multi-round survey known as the National Family Health Survey (NFHS) was carried out in a representative sample of Indian

## **households. NFHS has two distinct objectives**

- To offer vital information on family welfare and health needed for policy and programme objectives.
- To inform people about significant new health and family welfare challenges.
- With effect from 1980, the Ministry began a Population Education Program to incorporate Population Education into the formal educational system after realising the potential of education in addressing the issues associated with the population growth rate.
- A key initiative of the ministry of education’s central division is the Population Education programme.
- The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare actively participated in its development along with collaboration with the United Nations Funds for Population Activities (UNFPA).
- India’s Population Education programme may be dated back to the third Five Year Plan (1961- 66). By 2027, India is expected to surpass China as the most populated country. If not properly utilised, this enormous population could become a problem. In order to ensure that this enormous population continues to be an advantage, policymakers **must** **embrace Population Education programmes to** **develop policies and inform the public about them.** ---

## Question 104 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2021-gs1-q104-unacademy**
**Q19. What is Cryptocurrency? How does it affect global society? Has it been affecting Indian society also? (15 Marks, 250 Words) Tags: Effects of Globalisation on Indian Society**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **Introduction: Write about Cryptocurrency.**-**Body: Mention cryptocurrency’s global** **impact and also on Indian Society.**-**Conclusion: Conclude with showing the** **importance of Cryptocurrency in the future.**
**Answer:** **A cryptocurrency, also referred to as a crypto, is a** **form of encrypted digital money.**A computerised database that contains individual ownership records and is meant to serve as a medium of exchange is used. Based on blockchain technology, the coin is unregulated and decentralised. It typically isn’t issued by a centralised authority and doesn’t have a physical existence (like paper money).**Cryptocurrency’s Global Impact:** Boththecryptocurrencyandtheblockchaintechnology that powers it, have advantages and disadvantages, but because the disadvantages outweigh the advantages, the government is taking precautions. The effects of cryptocurrencies on politics, society, and the economy

## **are all interconnected**

- Cryptocurrencies deprive governments of the ability to issue currency. As a result, economic policy becomes ineffective, and the bond between the citizenry and government is eroded.
- The cost and speed of cryptocurrency transactions are both low. Capital becomes more mobile and volatile as a result, endangering macroeconomic stability and having negative social effects.
- A new asset class that has emerged is cryptocurrency (alternative of gold). On the other side, the volatility of bitcoin’s value has produced both kings and paupers.
- Like gold, the supply of cryptocurrencies is constrained. Additionally, in recent years, the price of cryptocurrencies has increased more quickly than the price of other financial assets. Consequently, cryptocurrency can become a wellliked investment choice.

## **Cryptocurrency’s Impact on Indian Society**

- The nation that receives the highest remittances is India. Contrarily, consumers squander money on conversion and processing fees, which they can avoid by switching to cryptocurrencies.
- It tends to reduce the cost of generating physical currency, enabling the use of money for societal advancement. The breadth of counterfeiting is also constrained.
- The significant investment made in cryptocurrencies by the nation’s youth has led to the emergence of a new crypto society in India. However, in the era of digital currency, those without the means to pay do not have access to technology. Blockchain and cryptocurrency assets **could be a part** **of the Fourth Industrial Revolution,** and Indians shouldn’tbecompelledtoignorethem.Cryptocurrency frameworks must be developed, which will require international alliances and cooperative strategies.

## Question 105 (Year: 2021 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2021-gs1-q105-unacademy**
**Q20. How does Indian society maintain continuity in traditional social values? Enumerate the changes taking place in it. (15 Marks, 250 Words) Tags: Social Issues**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **Introduction: Provide a brief overview of** **Indian Society.**

## **Body**

- **Mention how traditional social values** **are upheld in Indian society.**-**Discuss why India has been able to** **preserve continuity in its societal values.**-**State the reasons why social values are** **undergoing changes.**-**Conclusion: Conclude with a balanced** **argument where traditional and modern** **values can co-exist.** **Answer:**Indian Society continues to exist**since ancient times.**Though it has undergone changes,**some of the** **elements**of our society continue to exist even today. Our traditional value system has included societal ideals like**compassion, solidarity, spirituality, non-** **violence,**etc., since the dawn of humanity.**Upholding the Traditional Social Values in the Indian**

## **Society**

- **Family System:**Through**socialisation,**the institution of the family has made sure that**traditional values**are passed down from**one** **generation to the next.** Family has been the backbone of India society and great support system for the members.
- **Festivals:**They not only have a**religious angle** **but societal inclination**as well. It brings people togetherandgivethemfeelingofonenessandgives them a**collective identity**of a community. The community celebration of festivals**strengthens** **ideals like brotherhood, fraternity, purity, the** **victory of good over evil,** etc.
- **Gatherings:**Social activities, such as**parties and** **bhajan-kirtans,**offer a platform for exchanging opinions and values. Here people from**all walks** **of life**come to a common place. Ideas and ways of life are discussed, and**common understanding** **develops** from here.
- **Marriages:**Indian marriages are more of a community affair than private matters. Marriages, sometimesacrossclassandcasteshavecontributed to preserving community values.**Reasons India has been able to Preserve Continuity in**

## **Traditional Societal Values**

- **Elasticity of Indian culture (Flexibility):**Indian culture has traditionally been open to**embracing** **various, even opposing, points of view.**People of every religion can be found here**living and** **practising their religion and rituals.** As a result of this flexibility there is also healthy mixing of cultures and viewpoints.
- **Different values and cultures are assimilated:**Due to**assimilating ability of Indian culture,**all the types of**races and nationalities**are found here. Historically, most of the**invading groups,** **wanderers, scholars and tourist**when visited India, became Indianised.**Mughals, Persians and**to a certain extent**British** too are examples of it.
- **Religious figures:**In various times saints like**Buddha, Mahavira, Shankaracharya, Kabir** and others continually pushed for spirituality to triumphovermaterialismandpeacefulcoexistence over violent dominance.

## **Factors Triggering Changes in Social Values**

- **Increasing Individualism in Society:**Due to the**market economy**more and more individuals are earningforthemselvesandbecomingindependent. Hence, giving rise to**individualism**in society. Group values are eroding as individualism is rising. The selfish**pursuit of personal goals**while frequently**ignoring the demands of society as a** **whole has**intensified due to**materialism and** **obsessive competition.**-**Nuclear Families:**More and more families are**becoming nuclear families,**and the**traditional** **joint family**concept is getting**replaced** with nuclear families.
- **Modern Education:**Modern education system has inculcated progressive ideals**like gender equality** **andtheeliminationofcaste-baseddiscrimination** **from our society.** These ideals are slowly and gradually replacing the centuries-old beliefs and thoughts.
- **Technology:**Information technology has sped up**information transfer**and rendered the old**social** **structures obsolete.**Social media has a negative and positive impact on our social ideals today. However, the**balance of traditional social values**in Indiahasbeenalteredbymodernisationpressures.The two, though, are continually altering their interaction. The relevance of and contribution to world peace of the “Vasudev Kutumbakam” and other classical Indian concepts is still emphasised. A**balance has to be set** **between both the values where one does not disturb** **the existence of another, though they may overlap.** **ANSWERS AND EXPLANATION** ---

## Question 106 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2020-gs1-q106-unacademy**
**Q8. Has caste lost its relevance in understanding the multi- cultural Indian Society? Elaborate your answer with illustrations. (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Salient features of Indian Society.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to give definition of** **caste system and try to link with the demand** **of the question.**-**In Body,**-**Give elaborate structure of caste system** **in contemporary India.**-**Writeaboutcertainchallengespresented** **by the caste system.**-**Mention the steps taken to abolish the** **evils of the caste system.**-**In Conclusion, provide an insight in the** **present context, to uphold diversity.** **Answer:**Caste system is a**part of multicultural Indian society**which is characterized by hereditary transmission of a style of life which includes an occupation,**ritual status** **in a hierarchy,**and customary social interaction and**exclusion based on cultural notions** of purity and pollution. Till date, the caste system dominates the pluralistic foundation of Indian society and throws up many challenges.

## **Present situation of caste system in Indian Society**

## **Neo-casteism**

- Since 1991, India has seen a rapid rise in new opportunities due to globalization and liberalization, but upper castes have benefited disproportionately through these changes.
- The lower ranked castes are often deficient in basic skills needed to take advantage of new opportunities.
- India has seen a steady expansion of educational opportunity through the decades. Thus,accesstohighereducationhasexpanded, but translation into occupational mobility has not been straightforward due to the presence of labour market discrimination.
- Thus, the reality behind the caste system is that caste continues to mediate economic outcomes and continues to assert its presence in society and politics.

## **Modern occupation and Caste**

- It turns out that traditional occupations have not broken caste boundaries.
- The spectrum of modern occupations is not caste based i.e. caste is not legally recognized, exceptinthearenaofcompensatoryaffirmative action.
- All Indians are free to choose their occupation of choice. However, the higher ranked castes are concentrated at the top of the occupational spectrum and the lower-ranked castes are concentrated at the bottom.
- Labour markets in the formal sector, which predominantly contain the modern occupations, reveal several features of not being meritocratic. There are hereditary “reservations” in business houses, i.e., being born into a business family guarantees the top spots.

## **Prevalence of Caste based Politics**

- Caste can take various forms in politics when parties choose candidates in elections, they keep in mind the caste composition of the electorate and nominate candidates from different castes so as to muster necessary support to win elections.
- When governments are formed, political parties usually take care that representatives of different castes and tribes find a place in it. **ChallengesimposedbycastesysteminMulti-Cultural**

## **Indian Society**

## **Caste-related violence**

- According to a UN report, approximately 31,440 cases of violent acts committed against Dalits were reported in 1996.
- The National Crime Records Bureau says that there has been a growth in the total number of crimes reported under the Act in recent years, but conviction rates have been low.
- Crimes against members of Scheduled Caste communities grew by 7.3% and against Scheduled Tribes by 26.5% in 2019.

## **Untouchability**

- Many villages are separated by caste, and they may not cross the line dividing them from the higher castes. They also may not use the same wells or drink in the same tea stalls as higher castes.

## **Discrimination**

- Theyoftendonothavethefacilitytoelectricity, sanitation facilities or water pumps in lower caste neighborhoods.
- Access to better education, housing, and medical facilities are hard to come by for lower castes.

## **Division of labour**

- They are restricted to certain occupations like sanitation work, plantation work, leather works, cleaning streets, etc.

## **Action taken to annihilate evils of caste systems**

- The Indian Government has **enacted laws to** **remove untouchability** and has also brought in many reforms to improve the quality of life for the

## **weaker sections of society. Few among them are**

- Constitutionally guaranteed Fundamental human Rights.
- Abolition of ‘untouchability’ in 1950 under Article 17 of the Indian Constitution.
- Scheduled Caste and Scheduled Tribe (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989.
- Provision of reservation in places like educational institutions, for employment opportunities etc.
- Establishing social welfare departments and national commissions for the welfare of scheduled castes and tribes These **measures adopted by the government have** **brought some relief**to the weaker sections of society. The**urban areas have shown a good amount of impact**and some improvement. However,**people in rural** **areas and villages still face extreme discrimination.** There is a long way to go in achieving the objectives set to eradicate and abolish discrimination, based on caste and creed.
[Question ID: 2020-gs1-q106-unacademy]

## Question 107 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2020-gs1-q107-unacademy**
**Q9.COVID-19 pandemic accelerated class inequalities and poverty in India. Comment. (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Poverty and Developmental Issues.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, write how Covid-19 is more** **than a health crisis.**-**In Body,**-**Substantiate how inequality and** **poverty is growing by giving data by** **International Bodies like WEF, UNDP,** **ILO etc.**-**Steps taken to minimize the socio-** **economic crisis imposed by Covid-19**-**Conclude by giving positive and futuristic** **suggestions.** **Answer:**The COVID-19 pandemic is the defining global health crisis in recent history of mankind and throws the greatest challenge since World War-II. But pandemic more than a health crisis has compounded into an unprecedented socio-economic crisis. The COVID-19 pandemic highlighted the growing inequalities and pushed many below the Poverty line.**The COVID-19 pandemic is increasing inequality**

## **and poverty in India**

## **According to World Economic Forum**

- Higher-paid workers are working from home while lower-paid blue-collar workers typically do not have this option.
- A higher share of low-paid workers is in essential services such as nursing, policing, teaching, cleaning, and store attendants where they are more likely to encounter people who are infected..
- Lower paid workers are more represented in the sectors that have suspended activities such as hotels, restaurants, and tourism services.
- The pandemic is increasing poverty and inequality between the rich who can afford to provide social safety nets, and the poor that do not have the capacity to do so.
- **Oxfam’s2019“TimetoCare”reportfoundthatthe** **richest 1 percent of Indians** held more than four times the wealth held by the bottom 70 percent of the country, that is, four times the wealth of 953 million people.
- **The World Bank** has noted that India is at risk of losing many of its hard-won gains against poverty. In its 2020 India Development Update, the World Bank noted that half of India’s population is vulnerable with “consumption levels precariously close” to the poverty line.
- **The International Labor Organization** (ILO) estimatedthatnearly400millionworkersinIndia’s informal economy are at risk of falling deeper into poverty.

## **Inequality in access to basic health care**

- According to UNDP data, in developing countries like India, for every 10,000 people, therearesevenbeds,2.5doctorsandsixnurses whereas for the same no of people, developed countries have 55 hospital beds, more than 30 doctors and 81 nurses.
- **The UN World Food Program and Food and** **Agriculture Organization**also reported that “food insecurity is spiking as people lose their incomes and have to reduce consumption**“.**

## **Digital Inequality**

- Lockdowns have made the digital divide more apparent.
- Billions of people do not have reliable broadband internet, which limits their ability to work, or to continue their education.
- The Annual Status of Education Report (ASER) revealed two startling facts. One, only a little under a third of India’s school children are accessing online education—the only means available to students with schools shut down for the last seven months. Second, even fewer can take live online classes.

## **Gender inequality increasing**

- India ranks 112th out of the 153 countries mapped in the World Economic Forum’s 2020 Global Gender Gap Index.
- The pandemic has disproportionately affected women, especially those who are part of the labour workforce.
- According to an analysis, COVID-19, and gender equality: Countering the regressive effects, conducted by McKinsey Global Institute in the year 2020, women are more vulnerable and susceptible to COVID-19related economic effects because of the already existing gender inequalities.
- AccordingtotheCenterforMonitoringIndian Economy, the already low labour participation rate for women has shrunk even further after the pandemic to 11% compared to 71% for men.

## **Greatest migrants’ crisis**

- The pandemic has exposed the precarious existence of millions of migrants who operate the levers of the informal economy.
- Repeated extension of the lockdown has caused economic devastation to workers in the informal economy as it has left them with no income, no food and no shelter.
- The Centre for Monitoring Indian Economy estimates that about 140 million people have lost jobs since the lockdown.
- Pervasive unemployment or underemployment, a feature of the Indian economy before the lockdown, worsened after it.

## **Steps taken to narrow the gap**

- Gig economy has been embraced like never before in India, due COVID-19 situation.
- IndiaannouncedaRs1.7-lakh-crorereliefpackage to take care of poor workers and those who need immediate help to combat the coronavirus pandemic.
- The Pradhan Mantri Garib Kalyan Scheme, which covers approximately 80 crore poor people includes cash transfers and food security
- UNDP has US$30 million Rapid Response Facility to provide funds within 72 hours, and more than 83 countries have benefitted including India.
- Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act (MGNREGA) and additional budget allocation to National Food Security Act (NFSA) have come to the rescue of the people.
- Labour and Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code reforms to accommodate the market demands.
- A change in the Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises definition to suit the need of the hour.
- Many state-level package announcements. **Growing inequality is neither good for society nor the** **economy.Itleadstogreaterfissures,moreviolenceand**undermines democracy and democratic processes. GovernmentcanmakethePublicDistributionSystem**universal**(so that no poor goes hungry),**expanding** **rural jobs guarantee**schemes and starting a similar urban jobs guarantee scheme. The Government should**aim to offer a reasonable** **amount of social security** to gig workers as well as protection under employment laws while retaining the inherent flexible nature of the gig work arrangement. Getting back on track will be a long-term process from this evil and we all need to be prepared for it.
[Question ID: 2020-gs1-q107-unacademy]

## Question 108 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2020-gs1-q108-unacademy**
**Q10.DoyouagreethatregionalisminIndiaappearsto be a consequence of rising cultural assertiveness? Argue. (150 words, 10 marks) Tag: Regionalism & Secularism.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to write the definition of** **regionalism.**-**In Body, mention positive and negative sides** **of regionalism and why regionalism as a** **concept is more negative.**-**Conclude with measures that can tackle the** **issue.** **Answer:** Regionalism is an expression of a common sense of identity and purpose by people within a specific geographical region, united by its unique language, culture, language, etc. In a positive sense, it inspires people to develop a sense of brotherhood and oneness which pursues to protect the interests of a particular region and promotes the welfare and development of the state and its people. In the negative sense, it implies excessive attachment to one’s region which is a great threat to the unity and integrity of the country.

## **Shades of regionalism**

## **Positive sides of Regionalism**

- British exploitative economic policies totally neglected some regions, giving way to economic disparities and regional imbalances fueled the idea behind regionalism.
- Language coupled with regional and tribal identity and not religion, provided the most powerful instrument for the formation of Ethnonational identity in India.
- Regional recognition in terms of statehood or state autonomy creates self-determination to the people of that region and they feel empowered and content. Internal selfdetermination of community, whether linguistic, tribal, religious, regional, or their combinations, has endured the predominant form in which regionalism in India has sought to express itself, historically as well as at present time.
- Regional identities in India have not always defined themselves in opposition to and at the expense of, the national identity. For exampleTripura Tribal Autonomous District Council (TTADC), formed in 1985, has served to protect an otherwise endangered tribal identity in the state by providing a democratic platform for former separatists to become a party of governance, and thereby significantly reduced the bases of political extremism in the state.

## **Negative sides of Regionalism**

- Regionalism is said to be extreme when the loveandinterestsofaparticularregionbecome absolute and different ‘regions/languages’ are seentobemutuallyincompatible,antagonistic, and hostile.
- The hostility and hatred towards other regions **generate ideology of ‘ism’** like Separation, Succession, Autonomy, Son of Soil. Example, Isac Muivah’s National Socialist Council of Nagaland, the Islamic fundamentalist groups in J&K, ULFA in Assam are examples of such an extreme dimension of regionalism.
- Many times, linguistic or ethnic minorities within the states come together and unite against the majority community in that state. This kind of sub-regionalism was validated by the State Reorganization Act of 1956. The most recent examples include the formation of Uttarakhand, Jharkhand, Chhattisgarh, and Telangana.
- Rise of Regional parties in political scenarios whichexploitvotebanksbasedonregionalism.
- Regionalism impacts politics as days of coalition government and alliances are taking place. Regional demands become national demands, policies are launched to satisfy regional demands and generally those are extended to all pockets of the country, hence national policies are now dominated by regional demands. E.g., MSP given to sugarcane, it was helpful for farmers in Maharashtra but it was implemented across all states resulting in agitations of farmers belonging to UP, Punjab and Haryana.
- Regionalism also becomes a barrier in international diplomacy, as in 2013 Tamil Nadu regional parties were against the Prime Minister of India, attending the Commonwealth heads meeting (CHOGM) in Sri Lanka. These actions have their immediate implication on the relation of India with Sri Lanka or other countries of the forums or in case of West Bengal not agreeing to the Land Boundary agreement and Teesta River Water allocation when the leaders at centre level were ready to do it. **Regionalism is considered more negative than**

## **positive**

- **Low rate of economic growth:** The economic growth of India has been varying since independence. But with respect to high growth of population, the economic growth has not been enough to catch the development at full speed. In the last decade, the economic growth was progressive, but now they are reeling under the influence of Covid-19 pandemic.
- **Socio-economic and political organization of** **states:** The states have been incompetent to do the adequate land reforms and the feudal mentality persists. Bhoodan and Gramdan movements, after independence, were not enthusiastically carried and even land under land banks were not efficiently distributed.
- **Lowerlevelofinfrastructuralfacilitiesinbackward** **states:** The extent of infrastructural development, such as- power distribution, irrigation facilities, modern markets for agricultural products, roads, has been at backstage which can be seen in states like Jharkhand and Odisha.
- **Low level of social expenditure by states on** **education, health and sanitation:** These subjects are core for human resource development. The states those have spent heavily on these subjects, fall under the developed and advanced states, for example Tamil Nadu, where health care services in Primary health centers are benchmark for other states.
- **Political and administration failure:** This is a source of tension and gives birth to subregional movements for separate states. Jharkhand, Chhattisgarh,UttarakhandandrecentlyTelangana aretheresultofthesefailures.Manysuchdemands are in pipeline such as- Vidarbha, Saurashtra, etc. These failures also weaken the confidence of private players and do not attract investors in the states.
- **“Son of the Soil” doctrine explains a form of** **regionalism,**which has been in discussion since 1950. According to it, a state specifically belongs to the main linguistic group inhabiting it or that the state represents the exclusive homeland of its main language speakers, who are the sons of the soil or residents. Regionalism could be good or bad for a nation. The Constitution of India under Article-19 provides every citizen a Fundamental Right to move around and settle down peacefully in any part of the country. GOI initiatives like**“Ek Bharat Shreshtha Bharat**‘’is to reinforceasenseof“nationhood”,identityandcultural pride among the people. The need of the hour is to developeachregionofIndia,throughdecentralization**of power to local governments** and empowering people for their participation in decision-making.

## Question 109 (Year: 2020 | Paper: GS I | Marks: 10)

**Question ID: 2020-gs1-q109-unacademy**
**Q18. Is diversity and pluralism in India under threat due to globalization? Justify your answer. (250 words, 15 marks) Tag: Effects of Globalization on Indian Society.**

#

## Answer

## **Decoding the Question**

- **In Introduction, try to define globalization** **and present that globalization can be a** **threat and sometimes it is not a threat to** **values and culture.**-**In Body,**-**Assess both sides of globalization like** **how it is impacting pluralism and** **diversity.**-**Mention, due to globalization, how the** **Indian culture and values reach out at** **international forums.**-**Conclude on a positive and futuristic note.** **Answer:**Globalization is the**free exchange of goods, services,** **people, ideas**and even culture across the world. But sometimes, it is bent towards development of a single world culture. Some people argue that all cultures will become similar leading to homogenization of culture while others may think that local identity is too strong to be subverted.**Trendswherediversityandpluralismareunderthreat**

## **due to globalization**

- **Loss of community affiliation:** With rise in metropolitan of Indian population, the individualism is flourishing, and the social relations are now based on commercial benefits.
- **Family:** Economic migration and the choice for individual space have broken the joint structure of family in India. At this junction, the old-aged and children are deprived of the required care.
- **Use of English and loss of vernacular language:** Under the growing trends of convert culture in education and service-based economy, English education has expanded rapidly at the cost of several vernacular languages.
- **Diminishing of classical music over western** **music:** The changing taste of music among Indian youthhasleftaquestionmarkoverthesurvivability of traditional classical music in India.
- **Marriage:** The growing acceptance to the livein relationship has questioned the sanctity of the institution of marriage in our society. It represents the dominance of western culture and the Indian way of living.
- **Fashion and aesthetics:** With the rise in corporate culture, the Indian dressing style has remained merely an occasional stuff that too in cultural occasions only.
- **Foodchoice:Withtheriseofchainrestaurantsand**hotels,thefoodchoiceofIndianyouthhasinclined towards the Italian and Chinese fast foods.**Despite these facts, another dimension of thoughts** **about globalization points to the universalization** **of our local beliefs and cultural values rather than**

## **demolition. For example**

- Observance of **World Hindi Day** on 10th January and organization of World Hindi Conference.
- **Indian festivals are now being celebrated across** **the world:** The most significant example is the Diya stamps issued by UNO to celebrate Diwali. Even a local religious festival of Chhath Puja is celebrated in Silicon Valley, USA.
- **ObservanceofInternationalYogaDayon21stJune** has popularized the Yoga throughout the globe.
- **Indian classical music**is being liked across the world and it is appreciated at various international platforms. Thus,**culture is an ever-evolving entity**which**constantly changes through diffusion and** **amalgamation.** One should embrace their cultural identity and values and it is our duty to preserve the cultural identity, however, globalization is not a matter to fear, and infusion of global identity should be welcomed.
